Overview

Supercharge Your Sites with Ajax Right Now...No Scripting Expertise Needed!

You've heard how great Ajax is--how it can help make your Web sites more usable, more interactive, more responsive, more successful. Ajax Construction Kit lets you put Ajax to work right now, even if you've never written a script! Just learn a few essentials, check out a few examples, then run the live CD and discover all the plug-and-play code you need to hit the ground running. Ajax Construction Kit's built-in applications work right out of the box. And with easy guidance from Michael Morrison, you'll gradually deepen your understanding--learn how to customize, extend, and reuse these applications--and even build skills for creating new ones. Walk away an expert.

Use Ajax Construction Kit's ten complete applications to

*   Dynamically load Web data

*   Dynamically populate lists

*   Tap into RSS news feeds

*   Use AutoComplete to reduce input errors

*   Create more responsive GUIs with real-time validation

*   Build a killer interface for viewing images

*   Present information or advertising with slick pop-ups

*   Provide an up-to-the-minute weather forecast

*   Build an e-commerce product shipping calculator

*   Add ratings tools to your site

And more...

CD-ROM Includes

*   Your complete Ajax projects: Run the Ajax projects (HTML, JavaScript, and PHP source code) that are included on the CD from your Windows, Mac OS X, or Linux system.

*   Your complete Ajax environment: Boot the CD as an Ubuntu Linux live CD to immediately begin working with the Ajax projects (no setup required).

*   Ready-to-run tools on the CD include the XAMPP Web server, Firefox Web browser, and Bluefish HTML editor.

System Requirements:

*   Platform: Windows, Linux, Mac (OS X or higher)

*   Processor: Intel-based processor (Pentium or higher)

*   Memory: 128 MB RAM or more

*   Disk space: None required; USB thumb drive optional to store data

*   Optical drive: CD reader

Negus Live Linux Series

Your Practical, Hands-On Guides to Getting Real Results with Free Software

Add the free software and examples from the book's CD to your Windows, Mac OS X, or Linux system to follow along with the projects described in the book. Or boot the CD directly to use a complete, prepackaged set of free software tools to play, display, and modify those projects. Start as a novice, by trying out examples...and finish with professional Ajax-enabled Web content!

SERIES EDITOR CHRISTOPHER NEGUS is the bestselling author of the Red Hat Linux Bible series, Linux Toys series, and this series's flagship title, Linux Live CDs.

### Dedication

> To my home-building mentor, David Hill, who patiently guided me through the construction of our home while I attempted to juggle a writing career and far too many sports and hobbies.

## Negus Live Linux Series

Your practical, hands-on guides to getting real results with free software

Add the free software and examples from the book's CD to your Windows, Mac OS X, or Linux system to follow along with the projects described in the book. Or boot the CD directly to use a complete, prepackaged set of free software tools to play, display, and modify those projects. Start as a novice, by trying out examples...and finish with professional Ajax-enabled Web content!

Overseeing the series is Christopher Negus, bestselling author of the Red Hat Linux Bible series, Linux Toys series, and the signature book for this series, Live Linux CDs.

## Acknowledgments

I'd like to thank Debra Williams Cauley and the good folks at Prentice Hall for making this project a reality, as well as Chris Negus for being excited and supportive from the get-go.

A huge thanks also goes to my good friends Stephen Tallent and Fletcher Moore, who came through mightily in the midnight tech-support department, not to mention passing along lots of good ideas and sharing their time generously.

And lastly, my wife deserves the biggest thanks of all for simply being the coolest person on the planet, at least in one nerd writer's humble opinion.

## About the Author

Michael Morrison is a writer, developer, toy inventor, and author of a variety of books covering topics such as Java, Web scripting, game development, and mobile devices. Some of Michael's notable writing projects include JavaScript Bible, 6th Edition (Wiley, 2006), Teach Yourself HTML and CSS in 24 Hours, 7th Edition (Sams Publishing, 2005), Beginning Mobile Phone Game Programming (Sams Publishing, 2004), and Java Unleashed (Sams Publishing, 1997). Michael is the instructor of several Web-based courses, including DigitalThink's Introduction to Java 2 series, JavaBeans for Programmers series, and Win32 Programming series ([www.digitalthink.com](http://www.digitalthink.com)).

In addition to his primary profession as a writer and technical consultant, Michael is the founder of Stalefish Labs ([www.stalefishlabs.com](http://www.stalefishlabs.com)), an entertainment company specializing in games, toys, and interactive media. When not glued to his computer, skateboarding, playing hockey, or watching movies with his wife, Masheed, Michael enjoys hanging out by his koi pond.

## Introduction

This whole book idea started as a bet I had with my editor that we couldn't pick two random techie topics and tie them together in a book. Two darts flew through the air and the topics "Ajax" and "live CDs" ended up being the result. This book could've just as easily been about Blu-ray on Rails or iPod design patterns. So it's just dumb luck that you're picking up a book on Ajax and live CDs.

And if you believe that, maybe I can interest you in helping fund this startup company I've been thinking about launching! The truth is, Ajax and live CDs are surprisingly good bedfellows in that live CDs provide the perfect environment for building and testing Ajax applications. The whole idea of a "live CD" is that it includes a complete operating system on a single CD. Along with a complete operating system, you get to include any applications you want, such as a Web server that can serve up Ajax applications.

One thing that is unique about Ajax applications as compared to purely client-side applications developed solely in JavaScript is that they require something to happen on the server. In other words, the very premise of Ajax is communication between a client and a server. This means you not only need a server, but you need a server that you have a bit of control over. At the very least, you need the ability to quickly install and test server-side scripts that will handle the server-side processing for your Ajax applications. Live CDs fit the bill nicely in terms of giving you an unbelievably customizable platform for building, testing, and hosting Ajax applications.

As cool as live CDs can be, I have to be honest and say that this book is 95% about Ajax. The live CD facet of this book is primarily a convenience due to the fact that Ajax applications are more demanding of their environment than pure client applications.

OK, so the book is primarily about Ajax, but what does that really mean? It means that in a relatively few number of pages you learn how to do the following things with Ajax:

*   Dynamically load e-book chapters for a Web-based book reader ([Chapter 3](#ch03_split_000.html_ch03))

*   Instantly select and view live stock quotes ([Chapter 4](#ch04.html_ch04))

*   Turn any Web page into a news aggregator that understands RSS news feeds ([Chapter 5](#ch05.html_ch05))

*   Add auto-complete functionality to your Web site ([Chapter 6](#ch06.html_ch06))

*   Validate form data and look up location data in real time ([Chapter 7](#ch07.html_ch07))

*   Create a "Web 2.0" image viewer to modernize the way your pages handle images ([Chapter 8](#ch08.html_ch08))

*   Use informational popup windows to dynamically display small nuggets of content ([Chapter 9](#ch09.html_ch09))

*   Add a live weather condition popup window to any link ([Chapter 10](#ch10.html_ch10))

*   Calculate shipping charges on the fly with a U.S. Postal Service Ajax request ([Chapter 11](#ch11.html_ch11))

*   Incorporate an animated five-star rating system into your Web pages ([Chapter 12](#ch12.html_ch12))

These are the kinds of dynamic applications that Ajax makes possible. More importantly, this book uses a construction kit approach to guiding you through adding these features to Ajax applications. Although you will ultimately learn a lot about Ajax as you explore the many examples presented throughout this book, the focus of the book is not on teaching you Ajax. The focus is on showing you how to do cool things with Ajax. Sometimes this requires you to learn something entirely new; other times it simply requires cutting and pasting code. The point is that this book is about doing things.

In my opinion, doing is learning, so you'll ultimately leave this book with a much more thorough understanding of Ajax than when you started. But more importantly, you'll leave it with your own set of Ajax building blocks that you can take with you and apply to your own projects.

### The Audience for This Book

I won't lie to you—this book by its very nature has to delve into JavaScript and PHP on a regular basis as the various Ajax examples are presented. However, the original goal of the book was for it to be approachable by anyone with knowledge of HTML and CSS (Cascading Style Sheets), and not necessarily a background in scripting, either client (JavaScript) or server (PHP). I'm not entirely sure that was a realistic goal, but it served me well in trying extremely hard to present enough Ajax coverage for the examples to make sense but not so much to scare away scripting newcomers.

All that being said, you absolutely must know a thing or two about HTML to tackle this book. CSS isn't quite as critical, but it does come up from time to time, so you might as well consider a basic knowledge of CSS a prerequisite. From there, it would serve you well to know something about JavaScript because it is the basis of Ajax. However, I believe you can duck and weave your way through the JavaScript code in this book with a limited knowledge of Web scripting, and still leave with a toolbox full of Ajax goodies that can be readily reused. The PHP code that pops up throughout the book is very simple, and I explain exactly what's going on, so you can definitely get by without knowing anything about PHP.

*   Turn any Web page into a news aggregator that understands RSS news feeds ([Chapter 5](#ch05.html_ch05))

*   Add auto-complete functionality to your Web site ([Chapter 6](#ch06.html_ch06))

*   Validate form data and look up location data in real time ([Chapter 7](#ch07.html_ch07))

*   Create a "Web 2.0" image viewer to modernize the way your pages handle images ([Chapter 8](#ch08.html_ch08))

*   Use informational popup windows to dynamically display small nuggets of content ([Chapter 9](#ch09.html_ch09))

*   Add a live weather condition popup window to any link ([Chapter 10](#ch10.html_ch10))

*   Calculate shipping charges on the fly with a U.S. Postal Service Ajax request ([Chapter 11](#ch11.html_ch11))

*   Incorporate an animated five-star rating system into your Web pages ([Chapter 12](#ch12.html_ch12))

These are the kinds of dynamic applications that Ajax makes possible. More importantly, this book uses a construction kit approach to guiding you through adding these features to Ajax applications. Although you will ultimately learn a lot about Ajax as you explore the many examples presented throughout this book, the focus of the book is not on teaching you Ajax. The focus is on showing you how to do cool things with Ajax. Sometimes this requires you to learn something entirely new; other times it simply requires cutting and pasting code. The point is that this book is about doing things.

In my opinion, doing is learning, so you'll ultimately leave this book with a much more thorough understanding of Ajax than when you started. But more importantly, you'll leave it with your own set of Ajax building blocks that you can take with you and apply to your own projects.

### The Audience for This Book

I won't lie to you—this book by its very nature has to delve into JavaScript and PHP on a regular basis as the various Ajax examples are presented. However, the original goal of the book was for it to be approachable by anyone with knowledge of HTML and CSS (Cascading Style Sheets), and not necessarily a background in scripting, either client (JavaScript) or server (PHP). I'm not entirely sure that was a realistic goal, but it served me well in trying extremely hard to present enough Ajax coverage for the examples to make sense but not so much to scare away scripting newcomers.

All that being said, you absolutely must know a thing or two about HTML to tackle this book. CSS isn't quite as critical, but it does come up from time to time, so you might as well consider a basic knowledge of CSS a prerequisite. From there, it would serve you well to know something about JavaScript because it is the basis of Ajax. However, I believe you can duck and weave your way through the JavaScript code in this book with a limited knowledge of Web scripting, and still leave with a toolbox full of Ajax goodies that can be readily reused. The PHP code that pops up throughout the book is very simple, and I explain exactly what's going on, so you can definitely get by without knowing anything about PHP.

### The Organization of This Book

Although this book follows the charter of the Negus Live Linux Series to provide hands-on experience quickly, the organization of the book is slightly unconventional. The book is divided into three parts:

*   [Part I](#part01_split_000.html_part01), "Ajax Boot Camp"— This part of the book wastes no time laying just enough groundwork so that you aren't overwhelmed by the intensity of the sample Ajax applications to come. You explore several commercial real-world Ajax applications and get acquainted with the Ajax Toolkit that ships on the accompanying CD-ROM. You'll leave this part of the book revved up to see Ajax in action.

*   [Part II](#part02_split_000.html_part02), "Building Real-World Ajax Applications"— This part of the book, which constitutes the remainder of the book, is focused entirely on using Ajax to solve real-world problems. From dynamically resolving a ZIP code into a city/state for a data entry form, to injecting an interactive five-star rating system into a page, this part of the book wastes no time and takes no prisoners. Don't get me wrong, it's still a fun read, but don't expect a bunch of tutorial talk or an essay on the history of Ajax. This part of the book in particular is all about doing!

*   [Part III](#part03_split_000.html_part03), "Appendices"— This part provides additional information you may find helpful. [Appendix A](#app01_split_000.html_app01), "The Past, Present, and Future of Ajax," paints a picture of where Ajax came from and where it's headed. This is in no way critical to understanding how to add Ajax-powered features to your Web pages, but it does add some perspective to the buzz swirling around Ajax these days. [Appendix B](#app02.html_app02), "A Quick and Dirty XMLHttpRequest Reference" is exactly that—a reference for the JavaScript object that serves as the core of Ajax. This object is actually hidden for the most part throughout the book thanks to the Ajax Toolkit, but if you ever want to dig deeper into my code and modify the Toolkit itself, you'll need to get to know the `XMLHttpRequest` object.

    Finally, [Appendix C](#app03.html_app03), "Using the Live Linux CD," is arguably the handiest of all the appendixes because it explains exactly how to use the live Linux CD that comes with the book. Don't worry a bit if you've never used or even heard of a live CD, and don't think you need to be a Linux expert or even have Linux installed. [Appendix C](#app03.html_app03) addresses the role of the live CD and guides you through how to use it for your particular hardware setup.

### About the CD-ROM for This Book

The CD-ROM that comes with this book is where live CDs enter into the Ajax picture. The CD includes a bootable Ubuntu (Linux variant) operating system that includes the Apache Web server and all of the sample Ajax applications covered in the book. Perhaps more interesting is the fact that you can run the applications directly from the CD once it boots up, which is pretty powerful considering that you would otherwise be required to have your own Web server up and running.

There are actually a variety of different ways that you can access and use the Ajax sample applications on the live Linux CD. You don't need to have Linux running on your computer—all you need is a computer with a Pentium III or better processor. The live CD can be run from Windows, Mac OS X, or another Linux variant. To find out more details about how to use the CD to explore the example applications, check out [Appendix C](#app03.html_app03), "Using the Live Linux CD."

### What You Need to Use This Book

Live Linux CDs are available to run on a range of computer hardware, such as standard PC architecture (i386), PowerPC (PPC), and 64-bit AMD (AMD64). I recommend a minimum of a Pentium III-class PC and at least 128MB of RAM for running the CD that comes with the book. As for hard-disk requirements, you don't even need to have a hard disk in the computer to run the live CD. You might consider getting a USB flash drive (also sometimes called a pen drive, thumb drive, or memory stick). Because live CDs are typically run from read-only media, USB flash drives are good to keep handy in the event that an application needs to write data, as is the case in [Chapter 12](#ch12.html_ch12), "Adding an Ajax-Powered Rating System to Your Site." And again, please refer to [Appendix C](#app03.html_app03) for detailed instructions on how to use the live CD.

# Part I: Ajax Boot Camp

|  

&#124;   &#124; [Chapter 1](#ch01_split_000.html_ch01) Ajax and a Brave New World Wide Web &#124;

 |

|  

&#124;   &#124; [Chapter 2](#ch02.html_ch02) Inside an Ajax Application &#124;

 |

## Chapter 1\. Ajax and a Brave New World Wide Web

Ajax in Cartoons

| ![](mtizys9yzdjyzznhzzgwmdc1cy9lown0mg04as9wcgl0zwovlmdoc29uy3a-.jpg) | Ajax Corporation is a fictional corporation used by Metro-Goldwyn-Mayer (MGM) as a parody of Warner Brothers' Acme Corporation, which is featured prominently in Road Runner cartoons. The Ajax Corporation is primarily referenced in Tom and Jerry cartoons. |

So I'm waiting for my order at a fast food restaurant the other day, and I started to question why the word "fast" had any business in the description of this particular place. I was eager to have a seat and flip through a magazine, not loiter in a crowded lobby waiting for my "fast" food. This situation got me thinking about Ajax and how it improves the overall Web experience. Yes, in an arguably twisted way, it's possible to look at Ajax through the greasy lens of a hamburger wrapper. And in just a moment you'll see how.

Although it isn't strictly considered an acronym, the name Ajax ultimately derives from Asynchronous JavaScript and XML. The key benefits to Ajax are that it allows you to receive small chunks of data over a Web connection asynchronously, meaning you don't have to wait around for it, and in most cases you can avoid a full Web page reload. The real trick to using Ajax effectively is to get your brain wrapped around what it can do, which is far more important than what it is or why people seem so enamored by it. Ajax is one of those truly rare Web innovations whose benefits are immediately understood once you see it used in the proper context. And sort of like the popular 3M commercials you sometimes see on television, you don't use Ajax to make things, you use it to make things better. This chapter starts you off with a healthy dose of Ajax realism, with a clear focus on what types of problems it aims to solve and why that matters to you. Yes, I also get around to explaining what it is and why people are enamored by it, but that's just icing on the cake.

### Dining with Ajax

Getting back to the fast food analogy in the opener, the scenario is a crowded fast food restaurant where you have to stand around waiting for your food before you can sit down and get comfortable. Contrast this fast food dining experience with a full-service restaurant where your meal is delivered to your table after you've already sat down and ordered. There is a considerable luxury in being seated at a table going about your business while someone is busy working away in the kitchen preparing the meal. In the fast food alternative, you must stand around waiting for your food before you can take a seat and get comfortable, assuming you consider fast food seating comforting. The point I'm getting at is that with the fast food approach, the time it takes to prepare your food is time out of your pocket because your dining experience is on hold until the food is ready. With a sit-down dinner, you're able to enjoy a drink, munch on an appetizer, and read a magazine, or talk it up with your dining companion while you wait. These are very different experiences, as illustrated in [Figure 1.1](#part01_split_001.html_ch01fig01).

##### Figure 1.1\. The time it takes to prepare food goes relatively unnoticed in a full-service dinner because you are free to enjoy the dining experience.

![](mtizys9yzdjyzznhzzgwmdc1cy9lown0mg04as9wcglhy2cwl2suahnmmwkwyze-.jpg)

Other than the fact that I love eating, especially eating out, the point of this discussion is that Ajax is a Web development technique that offers to upgrade your Web pages to a sit-down dining experience. Traditional Web applications force you to wait while data is searched or otherwise processed on the server. Not so with Ajax. An Ajax Web application goes off and performs its data processing in the background, leaving you to continue to interact with the Web page in the meantime. This may seem like a subtle distinction, but it is not. Just ask yourself, would you rather stand in line at a not-so-fast-food restaurant or relax at a cozy table in a sit-down restaurant?

Note

| ![](mtizys9yzdjyzznhzzgwmdc1cy9lown0mg04as9wcgl0zwovlmdoc29uy3a-.jpg) | One small problem with my restaurant analogy for Ajax is that it implies that Ajax Web applications are somehow slower than traditional Web applications. This is not the case. Ajax applications are no faster or slower in terms of raw data processing. In fact, they often give the impression of being faster because processing is typically going on in the background when you don't even realize it. |

## Chapter 1\. Ajax and a Brave New World Wide Web

Ajax in Cartoons

| ![](mtizys9yzdjyzznhzzgwmdc1cy9lown0mg04as9wcgl0zwovlmdoc29uy3a-.jpg) | Ajax Corporation is a fictional corporation used by Metro-Goldwyn-Mayer (MGM) as a parody of Warner Brothers' Acme Corporation, which is featured prominently in Road Runner cartoons. The Ajax Corporation is primarily referenced in Tom and Jerry cartoons. |

So I'm waiting for my order at a fast food restaurant the other day, and I started to question why the word "fast" had any business in the description of this particular place. I was eager to have a seat and flip through a magazine, not loiter in a crowded lobby waiting for my "fast" food. This situation got me thinking about Ajax and how it improves the overall Web experience. Yes, in an arguably twisted way, it's possible to look at Ajax through the greasy lens of a hamburger wrapper. And in just a moment you'll see how.

Although it isn't strictly considered an acronym, the name Ajax ultimately derives from Asynchronous JavaScript and XML. The key benefits to Ajax are that it allows you to receive small chunks of data over a Web connection asynchronously, meaning you don't have to wait around for it, and in most cases you can avoid a full Web page reload. This chapter starts you off with a healthy dose of Ajax realism, with a clear focus on what types of problems it aims to solve and why that matters to you. Yes, I also get around to explaining what it is and why people are enamored by it, but that's just icing on the cake.

Getting back to the fast food analogy in the opener, the scenario is a crowded fast food restaurant where you have to stand around waiting for your food before you can sit down and get comfortable. The point I'm getting at is that with the fast food approach, the time it takes to prepare your food is time out of your pocket because your dining experience is on hold until the food is ready. With a sit-down dinner, you're able to enjoy a drink, munch on an appetizer, and read a magazine, or talk it up with your dining companion while you wait. These are very different experiences, as illustrated in [Figure 1.1](#ch01lev1sec1_split_001.html_ch01fig01).

![](mtizys9yzdjyzznhzzgwmdc1cy9lown0mg04as9wcglhy2cwl2suahnmmwkwyze-.jpg)

| ![](mtizys9yzdjyzznhzzgwmdc1cy9lown0mg04as9wcgl0zwovlmdoc29uy3a-.jpg) | One small problem with my restaurant analogy for Ajax is that it implies that Ajax Web applications are somehow slower than traditional Web applications. This is not the case. Ajax applications are no faster or slower in terms of raw data processing. In fact, they often give the impression of being faster because processing is typically going on in the background when you don't even realize it. |

### Ajax, Sting, and Asynchronicity

In 1983 the Police released the album Synchronicity, and it went on to sell millions of copies and launch major hits such as "Every Breath You Take." I bet Sting never realized that a few decades later his album title would be the antagonist in numerous tech books and articles. As it turns out, synchronicity is the bane of Web user interfaces, as it implies that one thing must finish before another can start. In a synchronous Web page, you cannot start Task B until Task A has completely finished, even if Task A takes a while. For example, a synchronous online music service wouldn't allow you to continue browsing for music while it's busy serving up a song for download.

Note

| ![](mtizys9yzdjyzznhzzgwmdc1cy9lown0mg04as9wcgl0zwovlmdoc29uy3a-.jpg) | One reason Sting has probably never made the connection between the album Synchronicity and the tech world is because the album actually refers to a psychological principle developed by Swiss psychologist Carl Jung. In this context, Synchronicity refers to a "meaningful coincidence," as in when two seemingly unrelated things take place that have more of a connection than mere coincidence. I apologize if I'm better at explaining techie computer topics than I am psychology principles. |

Another example more to the point with Ajax is the shipping cost in an online shopping cart, which is often a critical part of a shopping transaction and in some cases can determine whether an item is worth purchasing. Take this from someone who has bought an automobile online—shipping charges can make or break an online purchase. The problem is that many shopping carts are designed to get all of your information, in many cases including your credit card number, before calculating and revealing the shipping charge. I don't know about you, but if I haven't yet committed to a purchase, there's no way I'm going to enter my credit card number.

Even for shopping carts that are designed to calculate shipping early in the checkout process, you still have the issue of a server request/response, which can take time and will ultimately result in a page reload in order to reveal the shipping charge on the page. While this server request is taking place, the order-entry user interface is effectively frozen, which is not a good thing. It would make so much more sense if the shipping charge was seamlessly calculated and displayed as you enter the shipping address. Since the item being purchased is already in the cart, all the shopping cart application needs is a ZIP code to fire off a server request and get busy calculating the charges. This action can take place in the background while you're filling out the rest of the order information. Similarly to the dining example, the wait involved in calculating the shipping charge is all but eliminated because you're allowed to do something else useful while it is happening.

### To Reload, or Not Reload

As I alluded to already, a frozen user interface is only part of the problem with traditional Web applications. The other side of the equation is the fact that a page must be reloaded for every server response. More specifically, when the server sends the shipping charge back from the server, the page must be reloaded to reflect the change because the server is responding with an entire Web page. Every change to a page that is based upon a server response requires a page refresh. This is a terribly annoying problem not only because page reloads/refreshes take time, but also because the page effectively flickers and blinks with each change.

Note

| ![](mtizys9yzdjyzznhzzgwmdc1cy9lown0mg04as9wcgl0zwovlmdoc29uy3a-.jpg) | There is wide debate over the notion of applying version numbers to the Web as a whole. It's very difficult to pigeonhole such a divergent beast into a numbering system, no more than you would label a highway system Autobahn 3.0\. However, the general idea that there are discrete, evolutionary steps in the design of Web applications is absolutely valid. So maybe the highway analogy isn't so far off after all—think of traditional Web applications as dirt roads, whereas Ajax-powered Web applications are asphalt interstate highways. |

There is also a significant efficiency issue in that the entire contents of the page have to be resent with each page reload, even if only a tiny piece of data (a shipping charge, for example) is being updated. This is a waste of bandwidth and a waste of the user's time. This type of Web application is what has generally become known as a Web 1.0 application. Ajax is playing a major role in ushering in a new generation of applications that fall under the Web 2.0 moniker, which "feel" more like true desktop applications.

Ajax solves the page-reload problem by reducing server requests and responses to only the exact data you need, as opposed to an entire page. In the case of the shipping charge request, the server just sends back a dollar amount, in which case the page updates only the shipping charge area of the page with server data, and then calculates a new total for the purchase. The entire page is not resent, no reload is necessary, and no flicker occurs. [Figure 1.2](#ch01lev1sec3.html_ch01fig02) illustrates how Ajax improves the shipping charge calculation process.

##### Figure 1.2\. Ajax makes it possible to calculate a shipping charge without interrupting the user experience.

[[View full size image]](mtizys9yzdjyzznhzzgwmdc1cy9lown0mg04as9wlmzwawlnywnhmc9rx2hzddfsmdjj.jpg)![](mtizys9yzdjyzznhzzgwmdc1cy9lown0mg04as9wcglhy2cwl2suahnmmwkwyzi-.jpg)

### What Exactly Is Ajax?

I've resisted the urge thus far to get overly specific about what Ajax is because it is much more important for you to understand what it does. To say that Ajax is a programming technique that involves the combination of JavaScript and XML is pointless if you don't understand the kinds of problems it solves. Hopefully enough groundwork has been laid that you now have a basic grasp of the problems Ajax addresses.

So then, what is Ajax? Ajax is a specific Web development approach that involves the usage of JavaScript and quite often XML. That's it. It's not a fancy browser plug-in or hip new programming language. It's just a programming technique involving technologies that have already been around a while.

The real muscle behind Ajax is a single JavaScript object that makes it possible to communicate with a Web server asynchronously. In traditional Web applications, the browser itself typically presents the only means of communicating with a server, and more importantly, it does so a Web page at a time. Ajax introduces the notion of using JavaScript to communicate with a server. Behind the scenes it's still the browser doing the work, but the net effect is that your JavaScript code is able to carry on a conversation with a Web server to send and receive discrete pieces of data. And this communication takes place asynchronously, which means it occurs in the background without interrupting anything else.

For now, it's not terribly important for you to know any of the specifics regarding the JavaScript object that makes Ajax possible. The next chapter gets into more detail concerning the nuts and bolts of Ajax and how to assemble Ajax code.

Note

| ![](mtizys9yzdjyzznhzzgwmdc1cy9lown0mg04as9wcgl0zwovlmdoc29uy3a-.jpg) | Keep in mind that the whole point of this book is to provide you with a plug-and-play Ajax framework that minimizes code development. You'll be able to reuse the code in this book with a minimal knowledge of the Ajax code and how it works. Not only that, but you can use the enclosed live Linux CD to experiment with many of the examples directly from your PC. |

### Real-World Ajax

This book is inherently noninteractive, and I can prove it. Click here with your finger and you'll see what I mean. See, no new page appeared. Knowing that the printed page is rather static, I encourage you to take a few moments to tinker with some real Ajax applications online to get a feel for how they compare with traditional Web applications. The next few sections present some of the more popular and compelling Ajax applications, and help direct you toward their specific features that benefit the most from Ajax:

*   Google Suggest

*   Netflix

*   Flickr

*   Google Maps

Since you probably already use some of these Web applications, it will be even more insightful as you find out how Ajax makes specific features possible. After this little real-world tour, you'll revisit the shipping charge calculator example in the context of a real Ajax application that is provided on the enclosed live Linux CD.

#### Google Suggest

Google Suggest ([http://labs.google.com/suggest/](http://labs.google.com/suggest/)) is a great example of a relatively simple Ajax application that is sneakily powerful. It turns out that "sneakily powerful" is something you may be thinking to yourself a lot as you explore Ajax and its many possibilities. Google Suggest is the regular Google search engine but with a suggestive text-entry feature that attempts to help you narrow down your search based upon actual Google search data.

As you type in a search phrase in Google Suggest, a dropdown list immediately appears with possible matches for the phrase. To accept one of the suggested phrases, just click it with your mouse, or cursor down and select it. What makes this Ajax application so cool is how each possible match has the number of matches listed out to the side of it. In other words, you are provided with an immediate clue as to the popularity of a particular search phrase.

As you experiment with Google Suggest, the key thing to note from a development perspective is how it is seamlessly making a round-trip to the server with each character you enter. That's pretty amazing when you think about it. Talk about a responsive user interface! This is exactly the kind of "feel" that Ajax is intended to impart on modern Web applications. [Chapter 6](#ch06.html_ch06), "Reading the User's Mind with Auto-Complete," revisits Google Suggest as it shows you how to employ your own "auto-complete" feature using Ajax. But don't wait until [Chapter 6](#ch06.html_ch06), go ahead and try out Google Suggest right now.

#### Netflix

Chances are you've already been basking in the comfortable glow of Ajax without even knowing it. If you use Netflix ([http://www.netflix.com/](http://www.netflix.com/)) for movie rentals, then you're already somewhat of an Ajax veteran, at least from the user side of things. Netflix uses Ajax to carry out a popular trick where a popup window automatically appears when you hover over a piece of information (a movie icon in this case) with the mouse pointer.

If you simply hover over the small icon of a movie, a popup window appears with a complete movie description and related movie information. All this movie data is retrieved from the server via Ajax in the background as the hover begins. From the user's perspective, this is all gravy because you don't even realize a server communication is taking place until it is over (the popup window appears). Again, Ajax adds a significant usability feature in an entirely seamless manner.

One interesting thing to note about using Ajax for popup windows is how the Netflix popup disappears automatically once you move the mouse pointer off of the movie in question. This helps to improve the perception that the popup is purely informative and not an annoying advertising popup that you have to click to get rid of—in the case of Netflix, the popups truly are a welcome addition to the user interface. You can experience Ajax on Netflix even if you don't have a Netflix account—just go to [http://www.netflix.com/](http://www.netflix.com/) and click Browse Selection to find some movies to hover around. Incidentally, you learn how to inject informational popup windows into your own pages in [Chapter 9](#ch09.html_ch09), "Presenting Information with Slick Popups."

Note

| ![](mtizys9yzdjyzznhzzgwmdc1cy9lown0mg04as9wcgl0zwovlmdoc29uy3a-.jpg) | For another interesting usage of Ajax for displaying popup windows, check out the Invisible Ad advertisement service at [http://www.theinvisiblead.com/](http://www.theinvisiblead.com/). It is a very unconventional advertising concept involving a huge grid of tiny squares that you must hover over with your mouse in order to view ads. This type of advertising is known as pixel ads because you literally pay for each pixel of ad space on the screen. Groundbreaking? Silly? You be the judge! |

#### Flickr

Without a doubt my favorite commercial Ajax application thus far, Flickr ([http://www.flickr.com/](http://www.flickr.com/)) is Ajax-powered to the max. If you aren't familiar with it, Flickr is an online photo-sharing community. What makes Flickr so unusual is how thoroughly it uses Ajax to give it the feel of a desktop application. There are very few tasks within Flickr that require a page reload or that make you sit around twiddling your thumbs during a server request/response. [Figure 1.3](#ch01lev1sec5.html_ch01fig03) shows how intuitive the editing of a photo caption is in Flickr thanks to a heavy dose of Ajax.

##### Figure 1.3\. Editing a Flickr photo caption occurs "in place" and without any page refreshes, even when saving the modified caption.

[[View full size image]](mtizys9yzdjyzznhzzgwmdc1cy9lown0mg04as9wlmdwcglqywnhmc9rx2hzddfsmdvj.jpg)![](mtizys9yzdjyzznhzzgwmdc1cy9lown0mg04as9wcglhy2owl2suahnnmxawyzu-.jpg)

The figure shows a picture in my photo collection with its caption being edited. It's important to understand that the edit field and its associated Save and Cancel buttons appeared automatically when I clicked the photo's caption. After changing the caption, you simply click the Save button and the changes are committed to the server immediately. No waiting around for a new page to open, no confirmation dialog box, nothing. The caption just changes from edit mode back to a static piece of text. Like I said earlier, "sneakily powerful" comes to mind when you think about how cleanly the caption-editing task is made thanks to Ajax.

Flickr doesn't stop with photo caption editing when it comes to its use of Ajax. There are all kinds of clever ways that Ajax permeates the Flickr user interface. I could spend a few more pages spoon-feeding them to you, or I could just turn you loose to try them out on your own. You'll need to create a free Flickr account to truly experience the user interface, but that only takes a few simple steps. You can start out at [http://www.flickr.com/](http://www.flickr.com/).

#### Google Maps

I have to admit that it pained me a little to crown Flickr my favorite commercial Ajax application because there is another that is dear to my heart. I'm talking about Google Maps ([http://maps.google.com/](http://maps.google.com/)), which in many ways is the godfather of Ajax applications. It was only a few short years ago that Web developers across the globe were scratching their heads over how Google Maps could possibly be doing what it was doing, and with such speed. As it turns out, the trick up the sleeve of Google Maps was and still is Ajax. In my mind, Google Maps is still the ultimate example of processing data in the background to improve the user experience.

The primary trick employed by Google Maps is constantly anticipating and loading map data before it is needed. In other words, Google Maps isn't concerned so much about what part of the map you are currently viewing as it is what part of the map you're going to be viewing next. Safe guesses include the areas around the currently viewed area, as well as zoomed-in and zoomed-out versions of the current area. It turns out that Google Maps generally outworks the user by loading data faster than the user is able to process it. So by the time you get around to exploring a different part of the map, Google Maps is already ahead of you and has preloaded the map data. The net effect is a shockingly responsive user interface that would be impossible without the assistance of Ajax in loading map data in the background.

Ajax also enters the picture with Google Maps when it comes to customizing a map with your own information. Google publishes a special API (Application Programming Interface) for interacting with Google Maps, and it is based entirely upon Ajax. For example, you can create your own custom markers (push pins) to place on map locations and even tie them to custom popup windows that display additional information about the locations.

### Ajax Isn't for Everything

I'm guessing the little tour of real-world Ajax applications has gotten you excited about injecting a little Ajax into your own Web pages. That's a very good thing. However, I must caution you that Ajax is not a one-size-fits-all solution. Like most things in life, a little moderation can go a long way toward getting the most benefit out of Ajax.

Earlier in the chapter you found out about the main problems that Ajax solves. Working back from these problems, it's possible to come up with a list of scenarios where Ajax makes a lot of sense. By analyzing this scenario list, it's also possible to determine when Ajax isn't an ideal solution. Use this list as a basis for trying to determine whether Ajax truly stands to help your Web pages. Understand that the list isn't all encompassing, but is intended more as a general guide for assessing the practical usefulness of Ajax. Here goes:

*   First and foremost, Ajax always involves server communication, or more specifically, data flowing from the server to the client Web browser. If your Web page does not hit a Web server for data of some sort, there's no point in using Ajax. You can certainly still use JavaScript to make the page more interactive, but Ajax isn't strictly necessary.

*   One of the key points of Ajax is to update a page without requiring a refresh/reload. If your application inherently requires a new page to be opened, such as a link to a new page in a series of discrete pages, then Ajax is probably not the answer. On the other hand, maybe you can merge several pages into a single Ajax-powered page that doesn't need reloading.

    Note

    | ![](mtizys9yzdjyzznhzzgwmdc1cy9lown0mg04as9wcgl0zwovlmdoc29uy3a-.jpg) | Some people mistakenly refer to any highly responsive Web user interface as an "Ajax user interface." Strictly speaking, such an interface must involve server communication in order to qualify as an Ajax solution, at least in my humble opinion. That's not to say that responsive user interfaces aren't closely tied to Ajax, because they certainly are. But the fact remains that Ajax is about getting data from a server asynchronously. I'm not trying to split hairs semantically in regard to what is and isn't Ajax; I'm just trying to help you know when it makes sense to use Ajax. |

*   Another key point of Ajax is asynchronous communications, which is how Ajax shuttles data in the background so that other stuff can take place in the foreground. If your application specifically needs one thing to happen before another, then Ajax probably isn't a good idea. Maybe a certain block of user entry data must be validated before going any further, in which case you probably really do want to open a new page or make the user wait while the data is verified. In either case, Ajax doesn't really help.

The point I'm making here is that Ajax is incredibly powerful when used properly, but knowing when to use it is important. The three preceding points should serve as guides for assessing whether or not it makes sense to inject Ajax into your application. Additionally, the examples you explore throughout the remainder of the book will shed additional light on when and how Ajax should find its way into your Web pages.

### The Shipping Charge Calculator Revisited

Earlier in the chapter I made a big deal about how annoying it is in an online shopping cart to find out the shipping charge only by first divulging financial information or stepping through a sequence of Web pages. I made a case for how Ajax could dramatically improve the situation by obtaining the shipping charge from a server in the background once you enter the ZIP code for where the order is being sent. You would then be free to continue entering order information instead of waiting for the server or being interrupted with a page reload. [Figure 1.4](#ch01lev1sec7.html_ch01fig04) shows what such a shopping cart might look like prior to entering the ZIP code for shipping.

##### Figure 1.4\. Part of the check-out process for any online shopping cart involves gathering the shipping information and displaying a total.

[[View full size image]](mtizys9yzdjyzznhzzgwmdc1cy9lown0mg04as9wlmdwcglqywnhmc9rx2hzddfsmddj.jpg)![](mtizys9yzdjyzznhzzgwmdc1cy9lown0mg04as9wcglhy2owl2suahnnmxawyzc-.jpg)

In this example, the check-out process has already progressed to the point of the purchase item being selected; all that remains is getting the shipping information, calculating a total, and completing the payment. This particular example stops short of completing the payment, instead focusing on streamlining the retrieval of the shipping charge and calculating a check-out total based on those charges.

With Ajax now in the mix, it becomes possible to get the shipping charge directly from a shipping provider's server (in this case the U.S. Postal Service) as soon as the ZIP code is entered. [Figure 1.5](#ch01lev1sec7.html_ch01fig05) shows the order entry page with the ZIP code partially entered. Notice at this point that the shipping charge has yet to be retrieved because I'm still entering the ZIP code.

##### Figure 1.5\. Nothing happens while the ZIP code is being entered because you need the entire ZIP code to request the shipping charge.

[[View full size image]](mtizys9yzdjyzznhzzgwmdc1cy9lown0mg04as9wlmdwcglqywnhmc9rx2hzddfsmdhj.jpg)![](mtizys9yzdjyzznhzzgwmdc1cy9lown0mg04as9wcglhy2owl2suahnnmxawyzg-.jpg)

Note

| ![](mtizys9yzdjyzznhzzgwmdc1cy9lown0mg04as9wcgl0zwovlmdoc29uy3a-.jpg) | The USPS Web Tools API, which is responsible for allowing you to retrieve a shipping charge via Ajax, also supports the verification of shipping information. For example, you could take order entry to another level entirely by using the USPS server to find out the city and state from a ZIP code, thereby eliminating two pieces of user-entered information. You learn more about the USPS Web Tools API in [Chapter 11](#ch11.html_ch11), "Building an Ajax Shipping Calculator," when we dissect the shipping rate calculator example in a bit more detail within the context of a real Ajax application. |

Once the ZIP code is completely entered and I move to the next field in the user interface (Phone Number), the shipping charge magically appears and the check-out total is calculated (see [Figure 1.6](#ch01lev1sec7.html_ch01fig06)). This shipping charge was obtained behind-the-scenes directly from a U.S. Postal Service server and then reflected on the page without any noticeable delay or page interruption.

##### Figure 1.6\. The shipping charge is retrieved and the check-out total is calculated in the background without distraction.

[[View full size image]](mtizys9yzdjyzznhzzgwmdc1cy9lown0mg04as9wlmdwcglqywnhmc9rx2hzddfsmdlj.jpg)![](mtizys9yzdjyzznhzzgwmdc1cy9lown0mg04as9wcglhy2owl2suahnnmxawyzk-.jpg)

Even if the USPS server had taken its sweet time returning the shipping charge, the user experience wouldn't have suffered much because the user is free to continue entering information. Additionally, any changes to the ZIP code result in a new server request and a new shipping charge. Not only that, but the specific USPS shipping service (Parcel Post, Priority Mail, etc.) can be changed, which also results in a new shipping charge being retrieved.

That last little trick in this Ajax example is revealed when you enter a ZIP code that isn't supported. Maybe the specific item can't be shipped to a certain region—or maybe, as in this example, you limit the ZIP code for testing purposes. Regardless of the motivation, if the ZIP code doesn't result in a valid shipping charge, an error notice is displayed next to the ZIP code field, as shown in [Figure 1.7](#ch01lev1sec7.html_ch01fig07).

##### Figure 1.7\. An error message appears next to the ZIP code field if you enter a ZIP code that isn't accepted.

[[View full size image]](mtizys9yzdjyzznhzzgwmdc1cy9lown0mg04as9wlmdwcglqywnhms9rx2hzddfsmdbj.jpg)![](mtizys9yzdjyzznhzzgwmdc1cy9lown0mg04as9wcglhy2oxl2suahnnmxawyza-.jpg)

[Chapter 11](#ch11.html_ch11) explores the inner workings of this example in more detail. For now, just know that the example is limited to only working for the ZIP code 20008, meaning that any other ZIP codes will result in an error. This limitation has to do with using the USPS Web Tools API in "test mode," not the example code itself.

### Summary

The last thing I wanted to do in this chapter was give you a long-winded summary of how Ajax came to be and who made it possible. I realize your motivation in buying this book is to do some cool stuff with what is without question one of the coolest programming techniques to hit the Web in quite some time. The goal of this chapter was to get you acclimated with what Ajax can do and why it is relevant to both your current applications and the future of the Web.

Anyone developing applications for the Web should be thinking about how to make their applications better: more interactive, more intuitive, and less like the Web of old. We are standing at the edge of an exciting new Web that has the potential to erase the line between Web app and desktop app. When you think about it, why should that distinction matter anyway? An application is just a way to do something useful or fun with a computer, and Ajax is a tool that can make many online applications dramatically better. The rest of this book shows you exactly how to make your applications better with Ajax.

## Chapter 2. Inside an Ajax Application

Ajax on the High Seas

| ![](mtizys9yzdjyzznhzzgwmdc1cy9lown0mg04as9wcgl0zwovlmdoc29uy3a-.jpg) | Several ships in the United States Navy have taken the name Ajax over the years, with the first USS Ajax launched in 1864\. The most recent USS Ajax was a repair ship that saw duty in both World War II and the Vietnam War, and was ultimately decommissioned in 1986. |

If it's one thing I hate, it's computer books that spend the first five chapters laying the groundwork for everything to come. Too many books feel like they have to teach you a little bit about everything instead of focusing on the one topic at hand. For this reason, you start off this chapter with just a bit more setup and then dive right into the guts of Ajax. If you think you need to brush up a bit on HTML and CSS, or maybe you think a quick JavaScript or XML tutorial might be helpful, then by all means take a break and brush up on them. Just don't hold your breath for me to waste your afternoon holding your hand through those topics when we've got Ajax business to take care of.

I'm not trying to scare you away; I'm just trying to get you fired up to jump into the cool stuff that you bought this book to learn how to do. By the end of this chapter, you'll have completed your first fully functioning Ajax application. Not only that, but you'll also have a pretty good sense of how to customize that application to do things it was probably never even intended to do. That's a good thing. Improvise, invent; that's the spirit here. Now let's get started!

The following files are used by the Booker application in this chapter and are located on the live Linux CD-ROM with the book in the `chap02` example code folder:

*   `booker.html`—Main Web page

*   `ajaxkit.js`—Core Ajax functions for the Ajax Toolkit

*   `domkit.js`—DOM-related functions for the Ajax Toolkit

*   `book_alicewonderland.xml`—Alice's Adventures in Wonderland XML e-book

*   `book_frankenstein.xml`—Frankenstein XML e-book

*   `book_mobydick.xml`—Moby Dick XML e-book

*   `book_warandpeace.xml`—War and Peace XML e-book

*   `book_warofworlds`—War of the Worlds XML e-book

### The Nuts and Bolts of Ajax

You probably now have a decent feel for the fact that Ajax isn't so much a single technology as it is a specific application of other technologies. Understanding how these other technologies fit together within the context of an Ajax application is important in understanding how to build and modify your own Ajax applications. So let's explore the component technologies that make Ajax work. [Figure 2.1](#ch02.html_ch02fig01) shows how the major Ajax technologies fit together in a typical Ajax application.

##### Figure 2.1\. HTML, CSS, JavaScript, XML, and a server program such as PHP all work together to coordinate an Ajax request and response.

![](mtizys9yzdjyzznhzzgwmdc1cy9lown0mg04as9wcglmawcwl2cuahnmmmkwyze-.jpg)

Notice in the figure that the Ajax request/response process unfolds in an orderly fashion, as is evident by the numbers 1 through 3\. Keep this figure in mind as you learn more about how the different Ajax components fit together in the next few sections.

#### HTML and CSS

It would be difficult to create any Web page without a little HTML since it is the language of the Web. In Ajax applications, HTML still serves as the structural portion of content on the page, such as text, images, form fields, etc. In fact, most of the HTML code in Ajax applications is nothing special—the interesting stuff takes place primarily in JavaScript code, which I get to in a moment.

If you've done any Web page creation in the past few years, you likely already have an appreciation of Cascading Style Sheets (CSS) and how important they are to creating visually attractive Web pages. CSS allows you to cleanly separate the appearance of a Web page from its structure, with the end result being pages that are more easily maintained, not to mention prettier. CSS enters the Ajax picture in that it provides you with a means of applying styles to change the look and feel of HTML content dynamically. For example, you might want to highlight an invalid data entry field upon validating the data against a database through an Ajax request. The highlighting in this example would be carried out on an HTML form element via CSS.

#### JavaScript

JavaScript's job in Ajax is to orchestrate everything on the client side of the equation, which includes any and all logic that must be carried out by the browser. Don't forget that the very essence of Ajax is an exchange of data between a client (browser) and a server (Web server), so you'll always be thinking in terms of client/server communication when designing and building Ajax applications. JavaScript code runs on the browser, and is used to initiate Ajax requests and handle the resulting data returned by the server.

You use JavaScript to package up user data for sending to the server, as well as receiving the server's response and presenting it back to the user in a meaningful way. Of course, this means that HTML and CSS factor heavily into the JavaScript code for an Ajax application, with JavaScript often utilizing the Document Object Model (DOM) to dynamically alter HTML elements based upon Ajax responses. The dashed lines in [Figure 2.1](#ch02.html_ch02fig01) indicate JavaScript's ability to alter HTML and CSS code dynamically using the DOM.

Note

You aren't expected to understand how to program in JavaScript to be able to reuse the examples in this book in your own Web pages. However, it is impossible to explore Ajax at any depth without digging into JavaScript code, so a cursory understanding of JavaScript is important if you plan on taking any of the sample code to another level.

#### XML and Ajax Data

OK, so you know that an Ajax Web page is still made up of HTML and CSS, and that it can be dynamically tinkered with using crafty JavaScript code that manages Ajax requests and responses. I still haven't explained the nature of the data that is sent between the client and server in an Ajax request/response sequence. And part of the reason is because the format of data in an Ajax transaction is not set in stone. You have the freedom of using several data formats to suit the specific needs of each application. In other words, you can carefully tailor the representation of data to each Ajax application.

One of the most compelling reasons to use Ajax is to take advantage of the XML data standard, which provides a highly structured means of organizing information. XML is particularly adept at representing complex data that would otherwise be difficult to represent, such as a hierarchical database with several levels of nested data. Ajax fully supports XML on either side of the request/response equation, meaning you can send and/or receive data in XML.

While XML is a very good thing, it is not the magical solution to every Ajax problem. In fact, it is perfectly acceptable, and in many cases desirable, to use raw text data as the format for Ajax data exchange. For example, it is very concise to pass data along to the server as simple name/value pairs like you often see in URLs, as in

```
fname=Michael&lname=Morrison
```

Compare this to the same information structured in XML:

```
<fname>Michael</fname>
<lname>Morrison</lname>
```

The URL name/value version of the data is much more effective in this case because the data is so simple.

The bottom line is that the format of data shuttling around in an Ajax application is entirely dependent on the specific needs of the application. Throughout the book you'll encounter applications that use several different data types, including raw text, XML, and another type known as JSON (JavaScript Object Notation).

#### PHP and the Server

One facet of Ajax that is often misunderstood by newcomers is the fact that it is entirely dependent on a server program accepting requests and responding in some way. The server program is the hidden ingredient in the familiar Asynchronous JavaScript + XML recipe that you typically see associated with the term Ajax. On a practical level, this means that you can't just assemble a Web page with some JavaScript code and crank out an Ajax application—there has to be a server-side component that responds to requests from the client.

The role of the server program in an Ajax application is entirely specific to each Ajax application, but in general you will find that most Ajax server programs are responsible for looking up some piece of information and returning it to the client. For example, an Ajax employee directory application might pass an employee ID to the server, in which case the server program looks up the ID in a database and returns the employee's name and contact information. Or, as in the example you pondered in the previous chapter, information about an item to be shipped might be passed to the server, after which the server looks up the shipping rate and returns it.

Unlike on the client side of an Ajax application, which is exclusively the domain of JavaScript, the server side is wide open in terms of what programming language is used to create the server program. Fortunately, there are some popular options that are widely supported and relatively easy to modify and use. One of these options is PHP, which is a very popular server scripting language that is handy in creating Ajax applications. Similar to JavaScript code that is run on a browser, PHP code is run on a server. Referring back to [Figure 2.1](#ch02.html_ch02fig01) once more, you can see how a PHP program on the server receives an Ajax request from the browser (thanks to JavaScript), and then responds after processing the request.

The interesting thing about PHP code, and all server-side scripts for that matter, is that they ultimately end up passing data back to the client. When working with PHP, you have to always keep in mind that whatever you're doing will culminate in data being shuttled back to the browser. In the case of an Ajax application, a PHP script is shuttling data back to an Ajax request.
