[Figure 6.2](#ch06lev1sec1.html_ch06fig02) reveals how the dropdown list in Lyricsfly.com will eventually resolve to a single song if you continue to type the song name.

##### Figure 6.2\. The more you type into [Lyricsfly.com](http://Lyricsfly.com), the less critical the dropdown list becomes.

[[View full size image]](mtizys9yzdjyzznhzzgwmdc1cy9lown0mg04as9wlmdwcglqzmlhmc9nx2hzddzsmdnj.jpg)![](mtizys9yzdjyzznhzzgwmdc1cy9lown0mg04as9wcglmawowl2cuahnnnnawyzm-.jpg)

In this figure I've entered enough of the song name to reduce the dropdown list to a single song. Of course, the whole idea is to prevent the user from having to type a lot, but you get the idea that the auto-complete feature could function reasonably well without the dropdown list.

The main point in showing how auto-complete can be implemented without a dropdown list is to set up the challenge for the Completer sample application. There is a fair amount of complexity in dynamically creating and managing a custom dropdown list, and I want to keep this example as clean as possible. You get to Ajax-driven custom popup windows later in the book, but for now I want to stick with a simple user interface without a bunch of tricky overhead code. Therefore, we'll scrap the dropdown list and create an auto-complete feature purely within a text input control.

This means the application will only be suggesting one word or phrase at any given time, but you'll see that even a single suggested phrase can go a long way toward making an application more helpful and intuitive.

So the task at hand for the Completer application is to provide an auto-complete feature solely for a text input control. In terms of how this challenge impacts Ajax, the client part of the application is responsible for sending the server the text the user is entering as each character is typed. The server takes a given piece of input text and finds the closest match in its list of possible completions (suggestions). The server then returns the remaining part of the completion to the client so that it can be added to the text input control and selected.

To make the application mildly educational, Completer uses a list of famous historical people as the basis for its auto-complete functionality. This means the user will begin by starting to enter a name, and the application will quickly (ideally) fill in the text field with its best guess as to who the person might be. If the user is happy with the suggestion, he or she doesn't need to type any more. If not, the user can elect to continue typing and the application will try again with another completion attempt. Unless the user is typing too fast for the Ajax requests to keep up, a request will be made for each individual keypress (key release, actually, but that's not the point).

In summary, the Completer application issues an Ajax request for each character entered by the user in an attempt to automatically complete the entry of a historical name. Once the name is successfully entered, the user can click a button to view a biography of the historical person on Wikipedia, the popular online encyclopedia. The Wikipedia facet of the application is just a token prize for successfully using Ajax to enter a historical name.

### The Design: Designing an Auto-Complete Application

You already have a basic idea as to what is taking place in regard to the data being passed back and forth between the client and server in the Completer application, and you even know that the user interface consists primarily of a text input control. [Figure 6.3](#ch06lev1sec2.html_ch06fig03) shows how the user interface factors into each Ajax request within the application.

##### Figure 6.3\. Each character of user input results in an Ajax request that returns a completion string.

![](mtizys9yzdjyzznhzzgwmdc1cy9lown0mg04as9wcglmawcwl2cuahnmnmkwyzq-.jpg)

The figure shows how a single text input control serves as both the source and the destination for Ajax requests. Although this may seem unusual at first, it makes perfect sense when you consider that the text input control is playing two roles: It is both accepting input from the user and attempting to give the user information by guessing what is being typed. The key thing to note here is how the completion text in the figure is highlighted (selected), which means that if the user types another character it will overwrite the completion text. This of course triggers another Ajax request with a new response and new completion text. And so the dance continues until the user arrives at the desired input.

One interesting thing to note about the data in the Ajax request for the Completer application is how simple it is. There isn't any XML, or any data formatting at all for that matter. In fact, you're really only sending a few characters back and forth to the server at a time. Just so you understand what I mean, let's step through a quick example involving a notable figure in American history, Thomas Edison. You would start entering this name by typing a `t` or a `T` in the text box. At this point the text box contains the following:

```
T
```

Because you've entered a character, an Ajax request is immediately dispatched and the text `ecumseh` is returned to the text box to appear along with the `T` you entered:

```
Tecumseh
```

Tecumseh is the famous Shawnee Indian leader who died in the War of 1812\. The name `Tecumseh` is initially suggested by the Completer application because `Tecumseh` appears before `Thomas Edison` alphabetically in the list of possible completions. You see this list a bit later in the chapter, but for now you can hopefully see how the alphabetic nature of the list plays into the completion process.

If you continue by typing another letter, `h` this time, the text `ecumseh` is immediately replaced by the `h`, giving you the following:

```
Th
```

Another Ajax request is sent out, and this time the text `eodore Roosevelt` is returned. Geez, are we ever going to get to Thomas Edison? Here's what the text box now holds:

```
Theodore Roosevelt
```

Clearly, `Theodore Roosevelt` is a step closer to `Thomas Edison` but still alphabetically before it, which is why this name was suggested. As it turns out, typing one more character is all it takes to finally hone in on Mr. Edison:

```
Tho
```

The server receives one more Ajax request and returns `mas Edison`, resulting in the following text box contents:

```
Thomas Edison
```

Now you have your name, a total of 13 characters, but you only had to type three of them. That's the power of the auto-complete feature. Hopefully this little exercise not only shines some light on the usefulness of the feature but also on the nature of the Ajax requests and responses required to carry it out.

#### The Client Request

The client request for the Completer application is very simple in that it sends along the user input as it is being entered in the text input control. This user input is just raw characters of text without any special formatting. In many cases, the input is just a handful of characters, although it could be more in cases where there isn't an auto-complete match and the user continues to type. Even so, you're talking about tens of characters, not hundreds of characters.

Note

| ![](mtizys9yzdjyzznhzzgwmdc1cy9lown0mg04as9wcgl0zwovlmdoc29uy3a-.jpg) | Fortunately, it doesn't really matter to an Ajax request how many characters you send its way, although the more data sent the longer the request transmission will take. However, you're still talking about relatively tiny amounts of data in the grand scheme of things. |

Before moving on to the server response details, it's worth addressing a concern that has been brought up regarding Ajax applications such as Completer. I'm referring to the fact that Completer is capable of issuing a flutter of requests in response to the user typing characters. Although the server-side script of Completer is simple and speedy, relying solely on data embedded in the PHP code, an application with a larger data set would likely get the completion text from a database. This means you're looking at a database hit every time the user types a character, which is potentially a problem.

The problem here is the prospect of lots and lots of database hits overwhelming a database server. This may or may not be a problem for any Ajax applications that you build using the auto-complete feature, but it's a bit of a warning to make any frequently called Ajax code extremely efficient; that is, minimal processing of minimal data. And if you should still have problems, you may need to explore a server configuration that yields better results for a high frequency of small data requests. Don't panic just yet—consider this a tiny alert to file away for future reference. Chances are you won't have any problems if you use the auto-complete feature with a little healthy restraint.

#### The Server Response

The server script in the Completer application is given the task of matching and returning completion text given a piece of text that has been entered by the user. Unlike many of the other applications in this book, which return data that has been carefully formatted in XML, Completer simply returns a text fragment that serves as a completion for what the user has already entered. This is just raw text with no special formatting, which means you can grab it on the client side and use it directly without parsing any XML code.

Although the server responses in the Completer application are short and sweet, they do occur much more often than Ajax responses you've seen in other applications because they are triggered every time the user types a character. This isn't necessarily a problem, but it is an argument for not using huge completion text in the list of possible completions. This list is sourced by the `completer.php` server script, by the way, which you see a little later in the chapter.

#### Making Sense of the Client-to-Server Conversation

Although you probably already have this particular client-to-server communication exchange figured out, [Figure 6.4](#ch06lev1sec2.html_ch06fig04) shows exactly how the Ajax request and subsequent response relate to the browser (client) and Web server.

##### Figure 6.4\. The Completer application involves unformatted user input and completion text flowing back and forth between the client and server.

![](mtizys9yzdjyzznhzzgwmdc1cy9lown0mg04as9wcglmawcwl2cuahnmnmkwyzu-.jpg)

The text data sent back and forth on each side of the Completer application is very small, typically on the order of a few characters or words. If you look back at the exchange of data earlier in the chapter for the text completion example of "Thomas Edison," you'll notice a sequence of user input characters sent out with each Ajax request. These requests are handled by the `completer.php` server script. Following is an example of a URL for an Ajax request that sends along the character `T` as the user input:

```
completer.php?input=T
```

At this point, the server responds and tries to guess that the user is entering `Tecumseh`, which isn't the case. If the user continues to type by entering the character `h`, the following URL serves as the Ajax completion request:

```
completer.php?input=Th
```

This time the text `Th` is being sent to the server script, which should result in a more accurate completion. The server responds by guessing `Theodore Roosevelt`, which is closer but not quite there. Finally, a URL with a three-character input is what it takes to get the correct completion:

```
completer.php?input=Tho
```

In this example, the server responds with the text `mas Edision`, which is the completion text for the name Thomas Edison (`Tho` + `mas Edison`). The user can now accept the completion and click the Learn More button to find out more about Thomas Edison. That's really all there is to the data exchange in the Completer application.

### Putting Together the Completer Application

Not only is the data exchange straightforward in the Completer application, but the structure of the application is pretty simple, too, consisting of only two files:

*   HTML Web page (`completer.html`)

*   PHP server script (`completer.php`)

These two files directly correspond to the client (`completer.html`) and server (`completer.php`) portions of the application; there are no separate data sources or third-party Web services to contend with. The Completer Web page issues Ajax requests that are handled directly by the PHP server script, which then responds with data that is handled by the JavaScript code in the page.

In reality, the list of completion words and phrases, which you see in a moment as part of the `completer.php` code, should ideally reside in a database of some sort. Some auto-complete lists can get fairly large, and a true database is likely to be more efficient at performing searches within large data sets than straight PHP code. However, I opted for simplicity in this example and just coded the completion list as an array within the PHP server script. As long as your completion lists don't get too terribly unwieldy in size, you should be fine doing the same.

#### The Completer Web Page

The HTML Web page for the Completer application (`completer.html`) has an extremely simple user interface, consisting solely of a text input control and a button for looking up the historical name entered. [Figure 6.5](#ch06lev1sec3.html_ch06fig05) shows the page open in a Web browser awaiting user input.

##### Figure 6.5\. The Completer Web page starts out with an empty text-input box waiting for the user to enter a historical name.

[[View full size image]](mtizys9yzdjyzznhzzgwmdc1cy9lown0mg04as9wlmdwcglqzmlhmc9nx2hzddzsmdzj.jpg)![](mtizys9yzdjyzznhzzgwmdc1cy9lown0mg04as9wcglmawowl2cuahnnnnawyzy-.jpg)

This user interface is primarily established by way of the body of the HTML code, which follows:

Code View:

```
<body onload="document.getElementById('name').value = '';
  document.getElementById('name').focus()">
  <div id="ajaxState" style="display:none"></div>
  <br />
  <div>
    Enter a historical name:
    <input id="name" type="text" size="32"
      onkeyup="getCompletion(this, event)" />
    <input id="learnButton" type="button" value="Learn More..." onclick="showBiography()" />
  </div>
</body> 
```

This code reveals how the page consists of two input controls—one a text box and the other a button. Notice that the text box is cleared in the body's `onload` event handler, and focus is set to it as well. This latter action is a good idea for any Web page where the initial action is the user entering text. Google is a good example of this same strategy—the focus is always set to the search text box.

Also notice that the `onkeyup` event handler for the text input control is set so that it calls the `getCompletion()` function. This is the function responsible for initiating an Ajax request that retrieves a completion suggestion from the Web server. Here's the code for it:

```
function getCompletion(inputControl, evt) {
  evt = evt ? evt : (event ? event : null);
  if (evt) {
    // Ignore keys that don't factor in to the text entry
    var key = evt.charCode ? evt.charCode : evt.keyCode;
    if (key < 32 || (key >= 33 && key <= 46) ||
     (key >= 112 && key <= 123))
      return;

    // Store away the current input
    currentInput = inputControl.value;

    // Send the Ajax request to load the completion
    ajaxSendRequest("GET", "completer.php?input=" +
      encodeURIComponent(inputControl.value),
      handleCompletionRequest);
  }
}
```

Note

| ![](mtizys9yzdjyzznhzzgwmdc1cy9lown0mg04as9wcgl0zwovlmdoc29uy3a-.jpg) | Earlier in the chapter I mentioned a potential performance problem related to Ajax requests such as this one where lots of requests are issued in response to key presses. One clever way around such a problem is to set a timer for issuing completion requests instead of issuing a request in response to every single key press. For example, you might check for a new key press twice a second, in which case the net effect is roughly the same in terms of completion text appearing but the maximum number of requests are throttled back to two per second. |

Most of the initial code in this function takes care of browser inconsistencies with respect to how events are handled. In this case you are interested in getting the value of a key release, which involves a few tricks so that the code will work in any major browser. Once the character code of the key is resolved, it is checked against several ranges of keys to make sure that it is indeed valid entry text. This prevents a control key such as Shift or Enter from being interpreted as valid data entry, which would otherwise issue an Ajax auto-completion request.

If the user input is indeed valid, it is stored away in a global variable called `currentInput`. The server URL for the Ajax request is then assembled based upon the text in the text box, and the request is sent to the server. Notice that the `handleCompletionRequest()` function is responsible for answering the call when the Ajax request returns with a response. Following is the code for this function:

Code View:

```
function handleCompletionRequest() {
  if (request.readyState == 4 && request.status == 200) {
    // Grab the response text and kill any trailing whitespace
    var completion = request.responseText.replace(/\s*$/g, "");

    // Only issue an Ajax request if the input control still
    // has the current input
    var inputControl = document.getElementById("name");
    if (completion && (inputControl.value == currentInput)) {
      if (inputControl.setSelectionRange) {
        // Add the completion to the input control for non-IE
        // browsers
        var inputLen = inputControl.value.length;
        inputControl.value += completion;
        inputControl.setSelectionRange(inputLen,
          inputControl.value.length);
      }
      else if (inputControl.createTextRange) {
        // Add the completion to the input control for IE
        var sel = inputControl.createTextRange();
        sel.text += completion;
        sel.moveStart("character", -completion.length);
        sel.moveEnd("character", 0);
        sel.select();
      }
    }
  }
  ajaxUpdateState();
} 
```

The `handleCompletionRequest()` function receives each Ajax server response, and is responsible for carefully inserting the completion text into the text box and selecting it. Although this task sounds simple enough, yet again browser incompatibilities result in two branches of code: one to accommodate IE and another to accommodate other browsers. It's unfortunate that at this stage of the Web's evolution we're still dealing with completely different approaches to something as simple as selecting text in a text box. But complaining isn't going to get this application working any sooner!

Looking back near the beginning of the `handleCompletionRequest()` function, you'll see a line of code that manipulates the server response text and stores it in the `completion` variable. This manipulation is necessary because the response text picks up some extra characters in the process of being sent from the server to the client. These characters appear at the end of the text and are likely a result of the `echo` command in the PHP script, even though it isn't supposed to append a line-feed, newline, or any other character to the end of a piece of text. At any rate, the standard text `replace()` method is used to trim excess characters off the end of the response text before proceeding.

The remainder of the `handleCompletionRequest()` function includes two different techniques for adding the completion text to the text box and then selecting it. Notice that before the code branches to browser-specific code, the `currentInput` variable is checked against the value of the text box. This is an important check because it makes sure the Ajax request matches the input that triggered it. It's possible for someone to type really fast and enter characters faster than the Ajax requests can resolve. In this case, you only want the last request to count. This piece of code helps to make sure that happens.

Although the two browser-specific code branches are different in their details, they both do the same thing:

*   Append the completion to the end of the user input in the text box.

*   Select the completion in the text box so that if the user enters another character, the completion will be automatically erased.

That wraps up the client-side functionality of the auto-complete feature, but this isn't quite the end of the Completer Web page. If you look back at the HTML code for the body of the page, a function called `showBiography()` is called in response to the user clicking the Learn More button. Here's the code for this function:

```
function showBiography() {
  // Open a link in Wikipedia based upon the historical name
  // entered
  if (document.getElementById("name").value != "")
    window.location = "http://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/" +
      encodeURIComponent(document.getElementById("name").value);
}
```

The idea here is to make the application do something meaningful with the historical name that has been entered. So the `showBiography()` function takes the historical name (contents of the text box) and uses it to build a URL for the Wikipedia online encyclopedia. This URL takes the following form: [http://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Thomas_Edison](http://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Thomas_Edison)

In this example, the name Thomas Edison is simply grafted onto the end of a boilerplate URL, which you can see in the code for the `showBiography()` function. The URL is then opened in the current browser instance by assigning it to the `window.location` property, which is akin to typing in the address bar of the browser. In other words, when you set the `window.location` property to a certain URL, it forces the browser to navigate to that URL.

#### The Completer Server Script

The server side of the Completer application involves a PHP server script named `completer.php`. This script is not only responsible for receiving a request and responding with a completion, but also serves as the data repository for the list of possible completions. I mentioned earlier in the chapter that this list might be better off stored in a true database, especially for applications that utilize large completion lists, but for the sake of simplicity the list works fine in the PHP script for this application.

Following is the abbreviated code for the `completer.php` server script:

Code View:

```
<?php
$people = array(
  "Abraham Lincoln",
  "Adam Smith",
  "Adolf Hitler",
  "Albert Einstein",
  "Alexander Fleming",
  "Alexander Graham Bell",
  "Alexander Hamilton",
  "Alexander the Great",
  "Alfred T. Mahan",
...
  "William Harvey",
  "William Shakespeare",
  "William T.G. Morton",
  "William the Conqueror",
  "Willis Haviland Carrier",
  "Woodrow Wilson",
  "Ziryab"
);
$input = strtolower($_GET['input']);
$inputLen = strlen($input);

if ($inputLen > 0) {
  for ($i = 0; $i < count($people); $i++) {
    if (strtolower(substr($people[$i], 0, $inputLen)) ==
      $input) {
      echo substr($people[$i], $inputLen,
        strlen($people[$i]) - $inputLen);
      break;
    }
  }
}
?> 
```

The most significant code in this script is the array of historical people, which is laid out as a list of alphabetized names in quotes. Notice that each name is separated by a comma, and that the entire list is enclosed within parentheses. This is standard formatting for arrays in PHP, and I point it out so that you know to maintain the format when modifying the completion list or creating one of your own.

One subtle detail of the Completer application that I haven't mentioned yet is how it doesn't care about the case of the user input. In other words, it will match `Thomas Edison` if you enter, `tho`, `Tho`, or `tHo`. The code responsible for this is the first line below the completion list that calls the standard `strtolower()` function.

The remaining code in the server script takes on the task of searching through the completion list one name at a time, looking for a match with the input text. If a match is found, it is echoed to the client, and the script ends. This is an important detail that reveals why the completion list must be in alphabetical order—the first match of the input text is always the one returned. Because the first match always wins, the only way to get consistent results is to alphabetize the list. A bit later in the chapter I offer a little trick where you can use Microsoft Word to automatically alphabetize a list—this can be very helpful if you have a huge list that isn't in any order.

You now have the two pieces of the application put together, so it's time to move on to the most fun development phase, testing!

### Testing Completer

The Completer application is pretty simple when it comes to testing, seeing as how all you have to do is start typing and see what happens. [Figure 6.6](#ch06lev1sec4.html_ch06fig06) shows the application after the character `C` is entered in the text box.

##### Figure 6.6\. Entering a single character in the text box of the Completer application results in an attempted completion.

[[View full size image]](mtizys9yzdjyzznhzzgwmdc1cy9lown0mg04as9wlmdwcglqzmlhmc9nx2hzddzsmddj.jpg)![](mtizys9yzdjyzznhzzgwmdc1cy9lown0mg04as9wcglmawowl2cuahnnnnawyzc-.jpg)

The first historical person found whose name begins with `C` is Charlemagne, which is evident in the figure. Because the completion text is automatically selected, you can continue typing if that name isn't the correct match. Continue by typing the character `o`, which results in a different name, as you might expect (see [Figure 6.7](#ch06lev1sec4.html_ch06fig07)).

##### Figure 6.7\. As you continue typing characters, the application continues to find the best match for the completion.

[[View full size image]](mtizys9yzdjyzznhzzgwmdc1cy9lown0mg04as9wlmdwcglqzmlhmc9nx2hzddzsmdhj.jpg)![](mtizys9yzdjyzznhzzgwmdc1cy9lown0mg04as9wcglmawowl2cuahnnnnawyzg-.jpg)

Ah, Confucius says this is the name I want, so I'm going to stop typing and click the Learn More button to find out more about the Chinese philosopher.

A simple click of a button leads you to a wealth of information about the person whose name you entered with the help of the auto-correct feature. The Wikipedia facet of the Completer application certainly isn't critical in any way, but it does add a touch of practical sizzle.

### Giving Completer an Extreme Ajax Makeover

The Completer application is one of the most broadly applicable examples in this book because it can be applied to virtually any text input, provided that it is somehow possible to predict what the user might type. As you think about using the auto-complete feature in your own pages, the key is to isolate text input that is predictable enough to be modeled with a list of words or phrases, but also not so predictable that a selection list would better serve your purposes.

As an example of where auto-complete might be overkill, consider the example of helping the user type in a state in the United States. Because there is a finite and relatively small list of states, it probably makes more sense to just have the user select from a list of states. On the other hand, maybe you want to use auto-complete to assist someone in entering the name of their pet. Although this might ultimately be a viable application of auto-complete, the list of possible names is so huge and open-ended that it may not be worth trying to help. The point is that auto-complete is certainly an extremely useful user input tool, but you should make sure to think through the specific type of input before assuming that it makes sense to use auto-complete.

With that little rant out of the way, let me say that the first thing you should try to do with the Completer application is change out the completion list entirely. Think of some other text-entry scenario that would be cool to assist the user with. Then change the data set in the `completer.php` server script; just make sure that the list remains in alphabetical order. The application will immediately start trying to auto-complete the text input using your new list of completions.

Tip

| ![](mtizys9yzdjyzznhzzgwmdc1cy9lown0mg04as9wcglwlnavamhzaxrnyw--.jpg) | If you're building a list of words and/or phrases for the Completer application and they aren't already in alphabetical order, I have a little trick to save you some time and energy. You need access to Microsoft Word, but that shouldn't be too terribly difficult considering how prevalent Microsoft Office is these days. Create a new document in Word, and then copy and paste your list of completions into it. Select all of the completions, and then click Table on the Word menu. Then select Sort from the Table menu. A dialog box will appear. Make sure the Ascending radio box is selected, and then click OK. Presto! Your list is now alphabetized. |

Another practical possibility for applying the Completer application to your own Web site is adding a context-sensitive search box to your site. For example, let's say you run a Web site devoted to extreme sports. You could offer a search box that uses auto-complete to suggest extreme sports terms and people. You already know how to help the user enter text into a text control via auto-complete, so all you're missing is the trick to route the input text to Google and perform the search.

Initiating a Google search is as easy as redirecting your Web page to a Google URL that includes the search string. Following is an example of such a Google URL: [http://www.google.com/search?q=tony+hawk](http://www.google.com/search?q=tony+hawk)

This sample URL searches for the text "tony hawk", which would find tons of matches for professional skateboarder Tony Hawk. Notice that a plus sign (+) is used to separate the first and last name. You must use + in place of any spaces in the search terms.

Your remaining task is to redirect the page to the Google URL. Assuming the text input control in your application still has the ID `name`, as in the Completer application, the following code grabs the input text, converts its spaces to plus signs (+), builds a Google search URL, and redirects the browser to it:

```
var searchText = document.getElementById("name").value.replace(  " ", "+");
window.location = "http://www.google.com/search?q=" + searchText;
```

That's all the code you need to convert an auto-completed context-sensitive text field entry into a Google search.

### Summary

This chapter added an extremely handy Ajax technique to your rapidly expanding bag of tricks. The Completer application is very focused and simple in that it only applies to a text control, but when used properly it yields surprisingly effective results in the usability of a Web page. The fact that you can completely ignore the auto-complete suggestion and continue typing means that the feature doesn't get in the way if the completion suggestions happen not to help. This is a common aspect of many Ajax techniques, and a point you should always keep in mind when injecting Ajax into your own pages.

## Chapter 7. Creating Responsive GUIs with Real-Time Validation

Ajax the Cleanser

| ![](mtizys9yzdjyzznhzzgwmdc1cy9lown0mg04as9wcgl0zwovlmdoc29uy3a-.jpg) | You knew it was coming sooner or later, so why wait any longer? Perhaps the most known usage of the term Ajax is the all-purpose cleanser that was introduced by Colgate-Palmolive in 1947\. The cleanser was named after the powerful Greek hero Ajax, which led to the Ajax product slogan: "Stronger than dirt!" |

One of the truly killer applications of Ajax is real-time user input validation, which means that data entered by the user is checked for validity as the user enters it. This can dramatically improve the efficiency of user interfaces because errors are caught as they are made, thus eliminating the need to submit an entire form to find out whether a problem exists. Additionally, applications just feel smarter when they give immediate feedback to the user regarding data entry. As it turns out, Ajax isn't technically required for a lot of real-time validation tricks, but it still does play a role in some instances when it is necessary to load data from a server as part of the validation.

This chapter digs into real-time validation and explores when and where it makes sense to inject such functionality into your own applications. You also take a quick break from Ajax to learn how to validate popular data types such as phone numbers, dates, and email addresses. Ajax then comes back into play as you work through a practical example involving ZIP code validation that performs a live look-up of the respective city and state. This is an extremely handy Ajax trick you can use to significantly streamline the entry of location/address information.

The following files are used by the Validator application in this chapter and are located on the live Linux CD-ROM with the book in the `chap07` example code folder:

*   `validator.html`—Main Web page

*   `ziplookup.php`—Server script for looking up a city/state based upon a ZIP code

*   `ajaxkit.js`—Core Ajax functions for the Ajax Toolkit

*   `domkit.js`—DOM-related functions for the Ajax Toolkit

*   `validatekit.js`—Validation functions for the Ajax Toolkit

*   `wait.gif`—Animated "loading" icon that is displayed during an Ajax request

### The Challenge: Checking User Input in Real Time

The challenge laid down in this chapter is quite broad—improve the retrieval of information from the user by eliminating input errors immediately at the point where they are made. If you use a word processor such as Microsoft Word, which has a real-time spell checker, then you understand how handy this feature can be. In many ways the auto-completion application in the previous chapter can be thought of as roughly similar to a real-time validator. However, auto-complete is more about improving efficiency than it is about correcting mistakes, even though the two tasks often go hand in hand.

To see a practical validator in action, check out Google's Gmail online email service at [http://mail.google.com/](http://mail.google.com/). The invitation feature in Gmail allows you to send an invitation to a friend to join Gmail. The invitation request requires the email address of your friend, which is what you're supposed to enter in the text box. An improperly formatted email address results in Gmail displaying an "invalid address" message just below the email address text box.

Gmail isn't quite as aggressive with its validation as some Ajax applications, which is evident by the fact that you have to click a Send Invite button before Gmail performs the validation. A more aggressive approach would involve validating the address as soon as the user leaves the email text box. This latter approach is often employed in Ajax applications, and in most cases serves as an improvement because it can head off invalid data entry as soon as it is entered.

The general idea behind a modern approach to user input validation is to eliminate the need for a page reload in order to see if data is valid. In many cases you can eliminate the entire trip to the server and perform the validation entirely within the client. Strictly speaking, if the server isn't involved, the validation isn't truly using Ajax. However, there is a notion of an Ajax "feel" to an application that can still be gained via client-side validation. The challenge in this chapter involves both client-only and true Ajax validation.

Speaking of the challenge, this chapter gets away from accomplishing a central task and instead focuses on demonstrating how to validate several different kinds of user input. Sure, it would've been possible to dream up some example involving numbers, dates, email addresses, phone numbers, and ZIP codes, but the additional overhead would just distract from the real emphasis, validating data. So the challenge in the Validator application is to simply present several user-input text boxes, each associated with a particular data type, and validate each one in real time as the user moves through the user interface.

Following are the data types targeted for validation by the Validator application:

*   Integer

*   Number

*   Phone number

*   Email address

*   Date

*   ZIP code

The key to validation isn't just popping up an annoying alert box to let the user know his or her input is bad. In fact, an alert box is the worst way you can go about notifying the user of an input problem. A better approach is to sneakily provide help fields on the form that are invisible unless a validation problem occurs, in which case you display help text for the user. For example, you might display the help text

```
"Please enter a date (for example, 01/14/1975)."
```

when the user enters an invalid date. The help text appears when the data is bad and then magically goes away once the data is fixed.

Of course, the "magic" is in the validation code that supports the help text feature. This code is part of the `validatekit.js` file included as part of the Ajax Toolkit on the CD-ROM with this book, and is also a part of the source code files for the Validator sample application. As was originally mentioned back in [Chapter 2](#ch02.html_ch02), "Inside an Ajax Application," the `validatekit.js` file offers several JavaScript functions you can use to validate different types of data. More specifically, the `validatekit.js` file exposes the following JavaScript functions that can be used for validating user input data:

*   `validateNonEmpty()`—Is the data empty?

*   `validateInteger()`—Is the data a valid integer?

*   `validateNumber()`—Is the data a valid number?

*   `validateZipCode()`—Is the data a valid ZIP code?

*   `validatePhone()`—Is the data a valid phone number?

*   `validateEmail()`—Is the data a valid email address?

*   `validateDate()`—Is the data a valid date?

As you can see, these functions just so happen to match up perfectly with the data types targeted by the Validator application. This means that the challenge just got a whole lot easier because you now have standard JavaScript functions you can use to validate each data type. The challenge primarily becomes figuring out how to wire these functions into the HTML elements on the Validator page so that data within text boxes is properly routed to the JavaScript functions. Also, you'll need to create HTML elements for displaying the help text for each text box so that the validation functions can display help text.

At this point you may be wondering exactly where Ajax fits into the Validator equation, and the answer is that it doesn't... at least not yet. All of the data types targeted by the application can be validated perfectly fine within the client without any assistance from a server. Or at least the format of the data can be validated on the client. ZIP code data is a little unusual because you can validate the format of a ZIP code (exactly five integer numbers, for example) on the client but you won't know if the ZIP code is truly valid in the real world. In other words, is the ZIP code actually in use?

The only way to check for the validity of a ZIP code is to look it up in a database of real ZIP codes to see if it is indeed real. This database would be able to tell you that 90210 is indeed a real ZIP code and that it represents Beverly Hills, CA. As it turns out, such databases already exist and can be accessed via Ajax requests. Even better, most of them can provide you the data in an XML format, making it easy to sort out in your Ajax code. Ajax therefore enters the picture with the Validator application by way of looking up the city and state for a ZIP code to verify that the ZIP code is real.

So now you have a fairly complete validation challenge involving several client-side data validations as well as a true Ajax validation involving ZIP codes and their respective cities and states. You can now turn your attention to the design of the Validator application.

## Chapter 7\. Creating Responsive GUIs with Real-Time Validation

Ajax the Cleanser

| ![](mtizys9yzdjyzznhzzgwmdc1cy9lown0mg04as9wcgl0zwovlmdoc29uy3a-.jpg) | You knew it was coming sooner or later, so why wait any longer? Perhaps the most known usage of the term Ajax is the all-purpose cleanser that was introduced by Colgate-Palmolive in 1947\. The cleanser was named after the powerful Greek hero Ajax, which led to the Ajax product slogan: "Stronger than dirt!" |

One of the truly killer applications of Ajax is real-time user input validation, which means that data entered by the user is checked for validity as the user enters it. This can dramatically improve the efficiency of user interfaces because errors are caught as they are made, thus eliminating the need to submit an entire form to find out whether a problem exists. Additionally, applications just feel smarter when they give immediate feedback to the user regarding data entry. As it turns out, Ajax isn't technically required for a lot of real-time validation tricks, but it still does play a role in some instances when it is necessary to load data from a server as part of the validation.

This chapter digs into real-time validation and explores when and where it makes sense to inject such functionality into your own applications. You also take a quick break from Ajax to learn how to validate popular data types such as phone numbers, dates, and email addresses. Ajax then comes back into play as you work through a practical example involving ZIP code validation that performs a live look-up of the respective city and state. This is an extremely handy Ajax trick you can use to significantly streamline the entry of location/address information.

The following files are used by the Validator application in this chapter and are located on the live Linux CD-ROM with the book in the `chap07` example code folder:

*   `validator.html`—Main Web page

*   `ziplookup.php`—Server script for looking up a city/state based upon a ZIP code

*   `ajaxkit.js`—Core Ajax functions for the Ajax Toolkit

*   `domkit.js`—DOM-related functions for the Ajax Toolkit

*   `validatekit.js`—Validation functions for the Ajax Toolkit

*   `wait.gif`—Animated "loading" icon that is displayed during an Ajax request
