Ajax makes it possible to access such information on a need-to-know basis and then display it in a popup window that only temporarily takes up screen space.

There is obviously a lot of ill will over the abuse of popups thanks to aggressive online advertisers. For this reason, some users are averse to popups of any kind. Knowing this, it's important to be very judicious in your usage of popups, and even when justified don't force them on users. What users really hate is not having any control over the display of a popup, as is often the case with annoying popup ad windows. A more positive usage of popups is to only display them in response to a click or possibly a hover. In the latter case, it is important to automatically hide the popup once the hover finishes (i.e., when the mouse pointer leaves the element it was hovering over).

A popular Ajax popup technique that has been used with success involves the hover approach I just mentioned. I'm referring to a link preview popup where the contents of the page associated with a link are displayed in a small popup when you hover over the link.

A good example of this technique is the Snap Preview site ([http://www.snap.com/](http://www.snap.com/)), which displays a popup window that presents a preview of a linked page. Although you could write your own custom hover preview window in Ajax, there is no need because Snap Preview is in fact an online service that automates the process. Snap Preview allows you to turn all of the links on your Web pages to Snap Preview links.

Note

| ![](ys8wohawzzmxcjm1mjjlzgftdc9zyzc4cjlnmgkvdgmucg9zagpwawdll24-.jpg) | Another service similar to Snap Preview is Kontera ([http://www.kontera.com/](http://www.kontera.com/)), which uses hover popup windows to display advertising associated with a link. Although Kontera links are still ad-based popups, they are much less intrusive than popup windows that appear without any warning. |

To Snap-enable your own pages, you must first register your site with Snap at [http://www.snap.com](http://www.snap.com).

In addition to registering you with Snap, the Snap registration process also provides you with the JavaScript code necessary to add the Snap Preview feature to your pages. This Snap Preview JavaScript code snippet is contained within a standard `<script>` tag. Following is an example of such a snippet, which must be placed in the head of a Web page in order for it to use the Snap Preview feature:

```
<script defer="defer" id="snap_preview_anywhere"
  type="text/javascript"
  src="http://spa.snap.com/snap_preview_anywhere.js?
  ap=1&amp;key=c6a9f...&amp;
  sb=0&amp;domain=www.stalefishlabs.com"></script>
```

This code is actually included as part of a sample Web page I put together (`snapper.html`) to demonstrate how easy it is to use Snap Preview. Here's the code for the body of the page:

```
<body>
  <p>Here are some links:</p>
  <p><a href="http://www.usatoday.com/">USA Today</a></p>
  <p><a href="http://www.amazon.com/">Amazon.com</a></p>
  <p><a href="http://www.nhl.com/">National Hockey League</a></p>
  <p><a href="http://www.snopes.com/">Snopes.com</a></p>
  <p><a href="http://www.michaelmorrison.com/">Michael's Web
site</a></p>
</body>
```

As you can see, nothing specific about this code ties it to the Snap Preview functionality—the Snap Preview script automatically alters every link on a page so that it uses the feature. [Figure 9.1](#ch09lev1sec1.html_ch09fig01) shows what the resulting page looks like with a preview popup window in view.

##### Figure 9.1\. The Snap Preview popup window appears any time you hover over a link on the page, providing a small-scale preview of the page.

[[View full size image]](ys8wohawzzmxcjm1mjjlzgftdc9zyzc4cjlnmgkvzmmuamdwz3a5cznhadbsav9pmhqv.jpg)![](ys8wohawzzmxcjm1mjjlzgftdc9zyzc4cjlnmgkvzmnncdlzmghqm3auawlnmc8-.jpg)

It's very important to note that Snap Preview popups appear and disappear in response to mouse hovers and exits, not mouse clicks. When you hover over a link, a popup automatically appears, and when you hover off of (exit) the link, the popup disappears. Some people feel that even this approach to popups is a bit intrusive because it is easy to inadvertently trigger a preview when moving the mouse around on the page. An even less aggressive approach is to tie information popups to mouse clicks.

And finally we arrive at the challenge for this chapter—information popup windows that are initiated by mouse clicks. I think the Snap Preview approach to popups, which is also employed in a slightly different manner in sites such as NetFlix, is very cool, but I agree with the complaint that it can be annoying when you accidentally trigger a popup by hovering (dragging) over a link. In my opinion, the safer route is to rely on mouse clicks for popup windows. Of course, the click approach doesn't work in some cases, such as Snap Preview, because there truly is a link that must be followed when a click occurs.

The focus of this chapter is on information popup windows that don't apply to traditional links to other pages. The idea here is to essentially create a special "popup link" or "info link" that is only used when you want to display a nugget of information. This makes it possible to trigger the popup on a mouse click as opposed to a hover, and thus minimize any potential negative impact on the usability of a page. The good news is that I've already written the bulk of the code that makes information popup windows possible.

The code that supports information popups is part of the `infowinkit.js` file included as part of the Ajax Toolkit on the CD-ROM with this book, and it's also a part of the source code files for the Informer sample application. As was originally mentioned back in [Chapter 2](#ch02.html_ch02), "Inside an Ajax Application," the `infowinkit.js` file offers two JavaScript functions that are used to support dynamic information popup windows:

*   `initInfoWindow()`—Initialize the information popup window for the entire page

*   `showInfoWindow()`—Show a specific piece of dynamically loaded information in the popup window

The `initInfoWindow()` function is used to initialize the information popup window, and must be called in the `onload` event handler of the body of a page containing info links. The `showInfoWindow()` function is then called for each info link in the link's `onclick` event handler; this is what actually causes the popup window to get displayed. You learn more about the specifics of these two functions as you get into the nuts and bolts of the Informer application later in the chapter.

Getting back to the challenge associated with the Informer application, you know that information popups are used to share information. In this particular example you create a Web page about the sport of skateboarding, using information popups to describe the primary equipment involved. The idea is that the main content on the page simply mentions each piece of equipment, whereas the specifics of each piece are delegated to info links (popups). So, you'll be wrapping info links around several pieces of text on the page so that the text is linked to information popups.

### The Design: Designing an Information Popup Application

The Informer application is really just a normal Web page with a few specialty links that are made interesting with the assistance of information popup windows. Because an info link is triggered via a mouse click, the associated popup window appears over the clicked link. [Figure 9.2](#ch09lev1sec2.html_ch09fig02) shows the conceptual user interface for the Informer application, complete with a popup window that appears over an info link.

##### Figure 9.2\. The user interface for the Informer application consists of a standard Web page with special info links capable of triggering a popup window.

![](ys8wohawzzmxcjm1mjjlzgftdc9zyzc4cjlnmgkvzmnncdlzmghnngkuawlmmc8-.jpg)

The figure reveals how a link on the page results in the display of a popup window that is overlaid on the page where the link is located. Although the code that powers the popups is contained in the Ajax Toolkit, the information that gets displayed in the popups is HTML code that is ultimately your responsibility. This HTML code is stored in separate files that map one-to-one with information popup windows. This allows the Informer application to make an Ajax request for a specific file in response to each popup window. So you can think of the contents of each popup window as a mini Web page.

The design of the application therefore isn't just about importing the appropriate JavaScript code but also involves the creation of content for the popup windows. The good news is that this content is pure HTML code with no special scripting required.

Although the Ajax request within the Informer application takes place inside the `infowinkit.js` file, it's worth evaluating what is involved in making a request on behalf of an information popup. The next few sections quickly explore the Ajax request and response associated with an information popup.

#### The Client Request

The information displayed in popup windows within the Informer application is loaded from discrete files that act as mini Web pages. For example, clicking a link for skateboard wheels will display a popup that shows a picture of wheels and a description of them. This picture and description are stored in an HTML file such as `skatewheels.html`, which is dynamically loaded via an Ajax request when the popup window is displayed. The client request for an information popup window therefore consists solely of the URL for an information Web page.

Keep in mind that the Web page URL sent along in a client request doesn't actually reference a complete Web page. This is because the resulting HTML content will be injected into an existing popup window, as opposed to being opened in a browser as a true Web page. You can think of the HTML content for an information window as an HTML document "fragment."

#### The Server Response

The document "fragment" notion for information popup windows should clue you in to what the server returns in response to a request made by the Informer application. Indeed, the server simply returns a document fragment as stored in the HTML file referenced by the request URL. So, in the skateboard wheel example, the client request includes the URL of the file (`skatewheels.html`), whereas the server response is the actual HTML code for the mini page.

Because the server is responding with a complete file (even if its contents represent a fragment of HTML code), it isn't necessary to do any special processing on the server. Therefore, you don't need a PHP script of any kind for the Informer application.

#### Making Sense of the Client-to-Server Conversation

The Informer application uses very straightforward Ajax communication thanks to the Ajax Toolkit. [Figure 9.3](#ch09lev1sec2.html_ch09fig03) reveals how the process of dynamically loading content into an information popup window involves sending a URL and getting HTML code (content) in response.

##### Figure 9.3\. The Informer application uses a URL as the basis for a client request that results in information content being delivered by the server.

![](ys8wohawzzmxcjm1mjjlzgftdc9zyzc4cjlnmgkvzmnncdlzmghnnwkuawlmmc8-.jpg)

A client request in the Informer application consists solely of the URL for the HTML file representing the information content. No parameters are necessary since you're only interested in the HTML file, so the Ajax request results in the loading of an entire file, which in this case contains HTML code for an information "page."

I quote the word "page" when referring to the information content because it is more of an HTML document fragment as compared to a complete page. The following is an example of the information content that describes skateboard wheels:

```
<table>
 <tr>
   <td rowspan="2"><img src='skatewheels.jpg'
     alt='Skateboard wheels' /></td>
   <td style="font-weight:bold; background-color:#CCCCCC">
     Skateboard Wheels</td>
 </tr>
 <tr>
   <td>Although other materials have been used in the past,
     modern skateboard wheels are all manufactured out of
     urethane. Urethane skateboard wheels come in a variety of
     different sizes and hardnesses for varying terrain.
   </td>
 </tr>
</table>
```

This content is stored in the file `skatewheels.html` and consists of an image, a title, and descriptive text. The HTML code is returned as a server response in Informer, and is then inserted into the popup window that is displayed to provide additional information about skateboard wheels.

### Putting Together the Informer Application

The Informer application makes heavy use of the information window support in the Ajax Toolkit, which is included on the accompanying CD-ROM. In addition to the Ajax Toolkit, the Informer application requires a couple of other components:

*   HTML Web page (`informer.html`)

*   Information content pages

The HTML Web page for Informer is the Web page containing the info links, whereas the information content pages describe specific pieces of content and serve to drive the popup windows. The next couple sections take a look at these parts of the Informer application in more detail.

#### The Informer Web Page

The Informer application consists of several HTML files, but only one of them serves as the main Web page for the application. The main Informer page is where the info links are contained, as shown in [Figure 9.4](#ch09lev1sec3.html_ch09fig04).

##### Figure 9.4\. The Informer Web page consists of a paragraph of text containing info links that launch an information popup window when clicked.

[[View full size image]](ys8wohawzzmxcjm1mjjlzgftdc9zyzc4cjlnmgkvzmmuamdwz3a5czzhadbsav9pmhqv.jpg)![](ys8wohawzzmxcjm1mjjlzgftdc9zyzc4cjlnmgkvzmnncdlzmghqnnauawlnmc8-.jpg)

The figure reveals how numerous info links are present in the paragraph of text found in the Informer Web page. Clicking any of these links results in the display of an information popup window, which is also shown in the figure. Although the popup window is primarily controlled via JavaScript code in the Ajax Toolkit, the body of the Informer page is laid out in its HTML code:

Code View:

```
<body onload="initInfoWindow(325, 200)">
  <div id="ajaxState" style="display:none"></div>
  <br />
  <h2>Informer - Skateboarding Basics</h2>
  <p>Skateboarding has increased considerably in the past few
    years as a major sport among America's youth. Although the
    equipment involved in skateboarding is fairly
    straightforward, there is still some degree of education
    involved for newcomers to the sport. Regardless of what type
    of skateboarding you plan on doing, you will no doubt need
    the core components of a skateboard, which consist of a
    <a href="#" class="info"
    onclick="return showInfoWindow('skatedeck.html', event)">
    deck</a>, <a href="#" class="info"
    onclick="return showInfoWindow('skatetrucks.html',
    event)">trucks</a>, and <a href="#" class="info"
    onclick="return showInfoWindow('skatewheels.html',
    event)">wheels</a>.
    Additionally, it's a good idea to consider a <a href="#"
    class="info" onclick="return showInfoWindow(
    'skatehelmet.html', event)">
    helmet</a> and <a href="#" class="info"
    onclick="return showInfoWindow('skatepads.html', event)">
    pads</a>, especially if you plan on skating any tall
    obstacles such as vert ramps and concrete bowls.
  </p>

</body> 
```

The first important piece of code in this listing is the `onload` event handler for the body of the page, which makes a call to the `initInfoWindow()` function in the Ajax Toolkit. Notice that two parameters are passed into this function (325 and 200), which are the width (325) and the height (200) of the popup window, in pixels. This width and height is fixed throughout the life of the application, meaning that the popup window doesn't change size once the width and height are initially set.

The next piece of code worth a second look is the code that creates the info links via familiar anchor tags (`<a>`). Following is an example of one of the info links that appears in the body HTML code:

```
<a href="#" class="info"
  onclick="return showInfoWindow('skatedeck.html', event)">deck</a>
```

Notice that the `href` attribute, which usually points to the URL for the link, is set to "#". This basically tells the link that it isn't linking anywhere. Instead, the link serves as a trigger for an information popup window, which is displayed via a call to the `showInfoWindow()` function in the `onclick` event handler. When the user clicks the linked text, deck, the `showInfoWindow()` function is called and the popup window is displayed.

The first parameter to the `showInfoWindow()` function is the URL of the page to be displayed. If you recall from earlier, the information pages are actually page fragments because they are intended for display within an existing popup window. You find out more about the structure of these pages in the next section. The second parameter to the function is the standard JavaScript `event` object that is necessary in order to pass along the coordinates of the mouse click.

Note

| ![](ys8wohawzzmxcjm1mjjlzgftdc9zyzc4cjlnmgkvdgmucg9zagpwawdll24-.jpg) | In reality, the "standard" JavaScript `event` object isn't all that standard, meaning that it isn't supported across all browsers. However, the Ajax Toolkit is smart enough to account for browser inconsistencies and adjust the event-handling code appropriately. |

Keep in mind that the `showInfoWindow()` function is ultimately responsible for issuing the Ajax request that loads the information content. To better understand how the `showInfoWindow()` function results in an Ajax request, check out the following code excerpt from the function as it appears within the `infowinkit.js` file:

```
function showInfoWindow(infoURL, evt) {
  ...
  ajaxSendRequest("GET", infoURL, handleContentRequest);
  ...
}
```

Notice that the URL of the "page" being loaded is passed straight through to the Ajax request thanks to the `infoURL` parameter. This is evidence that the Ajax request for an information popup window simply involves the loading of an HTML file.

One other little detail to notice in the HTML code for the body of the Informer page is the setting of the `class` attribute for the info links to "`info`". The `info` style sheet class is established in the `infowinkit.css` file, and it serves to style info links a little differently from standard links. These links provide a visual cue that they do something a bit beyond the ordinary. More specifically, info links appear with a double underline, and also take on a highlight when hovered over.

In order for the `info` style class to work, the `infowinkit.css` style sheet must be imported into the head of the Informer Web page (`informer.html`) using the following line of code:

```
<link rel="stylesheet" type="text/css" href="infowinkit.css" />
```

Similarly, the `infowinkit.js` JavaScript file is imported with the following line of code:

```
<script type="text/javascript" src="infowinkit.js"> </script>
```

Both of these lines of code must appear in the head of the page. Just to clarify how the files are imported, here is the full head of the Informer Web page:

```
<head>
  <title>Informer</title>

  <link rel="stylesheet" type="text/css" href="infowinkit.css" />
  <script type="text/javascript" src="ajaxkit.js"> </script>
  <script type="text/javascript" src="domkit.js"> </script>
  <script type="text/javascript" src="infowinkit.js"> </script>
</head>
```

This code reveals how the Informer Web page also includes the `ajaxkit.js` and `domkit.js` files from the Ajax Toolkit, which offer several helper functions related to Ajax and the JavaScript DOM.

With the guts of the Informer application in place, all you're missing is the content for the information popup windows themselves.

#### The Information Content Pages

If you refer back to the code in the body of the Informer Web page, you'll notice five info links for content related to pieces of skateboarding equipment. Each of these links references an HTML file that contains the content to be displayed in an information popup window. Following are the HTML files referenced by the Informer page:

*   `skatedeck.html`

*   `skatetrucks.html`

*   `skatewheels.html`

*   `skatehelmet.html`

*   `skatepads.html`

Each of these files represents a mini Web page that is displayed in a popup window when a link is clicked. Even though these files are document fragments as opposed to complete Web pages, you can still open and view them in a Web browser to see what they look like. [Figure 9.5](#ch09lev1sec3.html_ch09fig05) shows the `skatedeck.html` file as viewed directly in a Web browser.

##### Figure 9.5\. Each piece of information content in the Informer application is really just a mini Web page.

[[View full size image]](ys8wohawzzmxcjm1mjjlzgftdc9zyzc4cjlnmgkvzmmuamdwz3a5czdhadbsav9pmhqv.jpg)![](ys8wohawzzmxcjm1mjjlzgftdc9zyzc4cjlnmgkvzmnncdlzmghqn3auawlnmc8-.jpg)

The figure reveals how the information content for a skateboard deck consists of an image, a title, and a paragraph of text. I've used the term "fragment" several times when talking about the content files. I say this because there are no `<html>`, `<head>`, or `<body>` tags found in a normal Web page. Instead, the content "pages" include only enough HTML code to describe the content. To see what I mean, take a look at the following code for the `skatedeck.html` file shown in [Figure 9.5](#ch09lev1sec3.html_ch09fig05):

```
<table>
 <tr>
   <td rowspan="2"><img src='skatedeck.jpg'
     alt='Skateboard deck' /></td>
   <td style="font-weight:bold; background-color:#CCCCCC">
     Skateboard Deck</td>
 </tr>
 <tr>
   <td>The deck is the most essential part of the skateboard,
     and is typically constructed out of seven layers of hard
     maple. These layers are glued and pressed in a form, and
     then cut and finished to the desired shape.
   </td>
 </tr>
</table>
```

Note

| ![](ys8wohawzzmxcjm1mjjlzgftdc9zyzc4cjlnmgkvdgmucg9zagpwawdll24-.jpg) | I'll readily admit that I broke the cardinal rule of not using tables for layout purposes, but in this case it was cleaner and simpler to lay out the skateboard content with a table. If you want to be a design purist and ditch the table for CSS, be my guest. |

This code reveals how the skateboard deck mini page is described using a fragment of HTML code. This works because the code is ultimately inserted into an information popup window, as opposed to being opened as a full-blown Web page by itself.

One little detail I neglected to mention is that I only created three of the five HTML content files, deliberately leaving out the `skatehelmet.html` and `skatepads.html` files. The reason for leaving out these files is to test out the Informer application's ability to handle missing content, which you do next.

### Testing Informer

Testing out the Informer application is a matter of opening the main page in a browser and clicking away. [Figure 9.6](#ch09lev1sec4.html_ch09fig06) shows the page after first being loaded, prior to any of the info links being clicked.

##### Figure 9.6\. The Informer application starts out with the information popup window hidden from view because the info links have yet to be clicked.

[[View full size image]](ys8wohawzzmxcjm1mjjlzgftdc9zyzc4cjlnmgkvzmmuamdwz3a5czhhadbsav9pmhqv.jpg)![](ys8wohawzzmxcjm1mjjlzgftdc9zyzc4cjlnmgkvzmnncdlzmghqohauawlnmc8-.jpg)

To view an information popup window and see more details about a particular piece of skateboarding equipment, just click one of the info links. The first thing you see is a popup window with a small animated "loading" icon in the upper-left corner to let you know that content is being loaded. [Figure 9.7](#ch09lev1sec4.html_ch09fig07) shows this icon in action.

##### Figure 9.7\. An animated "loading" icon first appears in an information popup to let you know that content is being loaded.

[[View full size image]](ys8wohawzzmxcjm1mjjlzgftdc9zyzc4cjlnmgkvzmmuamdwz3a5czlhadbsav9pmhqv.jpg)![](ys8wohawzzmxcjm1mjjlzgftdc9zyzc4cjlnmgkvzmnncdlzmghqoxauawlnmc8-.jpg)

Unless you have a really slow Internet connection, the content should load very quickly, in which case the animated "loading" icon is replaced by the actual content. [Figure 9.8](#ch09lev1sec4.html_ch09fig08) shows the Informer application with a popup window in view that is displaying information about skateboard decks.

##### Figure 9.8\. An information popup appears with additional information when you click an info link in the Informer application.

[[View full size image]](ys8wohawzzmxcjm1mjjlzgftdc9zyzc4cjlnmgkvzmmuamdwz3a5czbhadfsav9pmhqv.jpg)![](ys8wohawzzmxcjm1mjjlzgftdc9zyzc4cjlnmgkvzmnncdlzmwhqmhauawlnmc8-.jpg)

Of course, two of the info links on the page have been deliberately set up to fail, which provides a good test of the Ajax Toolkit's error-handling capabilities. [Figure 9.9](#ch09lev1sec4.html_ch09fig09) shows what happens when you click the helmet or pads info link.

##### Figure 9.9\. Clicking an info link with no available information content results in a popup window containing an error message.

[[View full size image]](ys8wohawzzmxcjm1mjjlzgftdc9zyzc4cjlnmgkvzmmuamdwz3a5czfhadfsav9pmhqv.jpg)![](ys8wohawzzmxcjm1mjjlzgftdc9zyzc4cjlnmgkvzmnncdlzmwhqmxauawlnmc8-.jpg)

As the figure reveals, the information popup window handles the missing content about as gracefully as it possibly can, simply displaying a message that the content could not be found.

### Giving Informer an Extreme Ajax Makeover

When pondering a makeover for the Informer application, one idea that quickly came to mind was first finishing the application so that it isn't missing any content. This involves creating the two missing content files for a skateboard helmet (`skatehelmet.html`) and skateboard pads (`skatepads.html`). The point is not for you to become a skateboard expert, but for you to experiment with adding content to an information window.

The best way to create these two files is to first copy one of the existing content files and use it as a template. For example, copy `skatedeck.html` and use it as the basis for the two missing files. You shouldn't have too much trouble finding a picture of a skateboard helmet and pads online with a little searching. Just pair up a picture with a title and some descriptive text and you'll have a content file for each of the missing topics in no time.

A more interesting way to expand on the Informer application is to completely alter the theme of the page. For example, change the focus of the content to something you're interested in, as opposed to skateboarding. Maybe you're into fishing, in which case you might write a paragraph or two about the sport, including your favorite fishing equipment. Then create info links around some of the more interesting parts of the text that could use additional descriptions.

After creating the info links in the main text, you're ready to create the content files that actually get displayed in the popups. Feel free to throw out my skateboarding templates and create something more exciting of your own. One way to change the feel of the popups is to modify their size. Remember that the width and height of the popup window is initially set when you call the `initInfoWindow()` function in the `onload` event handler of the Informer Web page. Try changing the width and height to get a different sized window that is custom tailored to fit your content.

I've really only scratched the surface of what you can do with information popups. I encourage you to seek out unique applications of your own—just keep in mind that the main benefit is being able to display small nuggets of HTML content in temporary windows, as opposed to opening entirely new Web pages as traditional links.

Note

| ![](ys8wohawzzmxcjm1mjjlzgftdc9zyzc4cjlnmgkvdgmucg9zagpwawdll24-.jpg) | Knowing that the size of the information popup window doesn't change once you first initialize it, it's a good idea to try and make all of your content files roughly the same size. That way, they can all be displayed in a single popup window with minimal scrolling. Ideally, there shouldn't be any scrolling, but that's difficult to guarantee across all browsers. |

### Summary

This chapter tackled an area of Ajax development that is both popular and controversial. Although popup windows are legitimately despised when used inappropriately, they can also be quite helpful when used discretely and for a meaningful purpose. Ajax-powered popup windows are even more interesting than standard popups because they allow you to dynamically load bite-size data from a server instead of having to open an entirely new Web page. This chapter guided you through the development of a sample application that uses the information popup window support built in to the book's Ajax Toolkit. Hopefully you enjoyed working with information popups because this isn't quite the last you'll see of them.

## Chapter 10. Forecasting the Weather with Ajax

Ajax the Other Warrior

| ![](ys8wohawzzmxcjm1mjjlzgftdc9zyzc4cjlnmgkvdgmucg9zagpwawdll24-.jpg) | If you think Ajax the Greek warrior was tough, what about Ajax the New York street thug? In the 1979 movie The Warriors, Ajax was the name of a particularly aggressive street gang member who was always up for a fight. If that doesn't sound scary enough, then maybe his fingerless leather gloves and black tank top will scare your sense of fashion! |

Although it can make for mundane conversation in the real world, weather serves as fairly interesting data in the realm of Web services. Weather is a great example of live Web services because it has a very up-to-the-minute feel about it. It's always changing and hard to predict, making it the perfect type of data for live viewing. And weather impacts all of our lives in a very real way, making it a practical information source that is valuable in some Web applications.

This chapter delves into an application that uses Ajax to query a weather server for live weather data, and then displays the weather data in an interesting way. More specifically, the weather data is displayed in a temporary popup window similar to the one shown in [Chapter 9](#ch09.html_ch09), "Presenting Information with Slick Popups." Weather data is ideal for display within a popup because it is concise and often supplementary to the focus of a particular page. In other words, it's the kind of thing you may want to get a quick look at and then dismiss.

The following files are used by the Forecaster application in this chapter, and are located on the live Linux CD-ROM with the book in the `chap10` example code folder:

*   `forecaster.html`— Main Web page

*   `forecaster.php`—Server script for loading a weather forecast

*   `ajaxkit.js`—Core Ajax functions for the Ajax Toolkit

*   `domkit.js`—DOM-related functions for the Ajax Toolkit

*   `infowinkit.js`—Information window-related functions for the Ajax Toolkit

*   `infowinkit.css`—Style sheet for information windows

*   `infowinkit_wait.gif`—Animated "loading" icon that is displayed while loading information into an info window

### The Challenge: Gathering Dynamic Weather Information

This chapter's challenge involves using Ajax to dynamically load weather data for a given location. Live weather information might be useful in all kinds of scenarios: a travel Web site, a sports Web site, really any Web site where weather could feasibly have an impact on the site's content. I personally like the idea of a travel Web site because weather happens to play such a critical role in travel decisions. Maybe you're trying to carefully dodge a storm in the tropics during hurricane season, or maybe you want to check for cold enough weather before embarking on a ski trip early in the season. Just about any Web site that deals with travel could benefit from live weather data.

But it's already possible to get weather conditions very easily, so why bother with Ajax and popup windows? Just go to the [Weather.com](http://Weather.com) Web site and check out the current weather conditions for any city in the world.

Although [Weather.com](http://Weather.com) can't be beaten in terms of the quality of the information it presents, keep in mind that you are required to navigate to it in your browser and then enter the location for the weather report you want. This is a problem if you'd rather not lose the context of the page you're viewing and would instead prefer to get a quick weather update without leaving the page. That's where popup windows enter the picture. By displaying weather data in a popup, you allow the user to continue focusing on the task at hand and not distract him or her by leaving the page. Perhaps more importantly, you remove one excuse for navigating away from your Web site.

So your goal isn't to create a richer or more thorough weather alternative than pure weather sites such as [Weather.com](http://Weather.com). Your goal is to inject live weather data into the context of your existing site, thereby adding some useful new content without the user having to leave the comfort of your pages. Information popup windows are the key to displaying weather data without it competing with the main page being viewed.

The Ajax request involved in this challenge must somehow query a weather service for weather data based upon a specific location. As it turns out, a ZIP code is plenty sufficient to identify a location, and therefore becomes a great piece of data to use for querying weather data. The Ajax response then contains weather data that you must somehow process in order to display meaningful weather information in a popup window.

## Chapter 10\. Forecasting the Weather with Ajax

Ajax the Other Warrior

| ![](ys8wohawzzmxcjm1mjjlzgftdc9zyzc4cjlnmgkvdgmucg9zagpwawdll24-.jpg) | If you think Ajax the Greek warrior was tough, what about Ajax the New York street thug? In the 1979 movie The Warriors, Ajax was the name of a particularly aggressive street gang member who was always up for a fight. If that doesn't sound scary enough, then maybe his fingerless leather gloves and black tank top will scare your sense of fashion! |

Although it can make for mundane conversation in the real world, weather serves as fairly interesting data in the realm of Web services. Weather is a great example of live Web services because it has a very up-to-the-minute feel about it. It's always changing and hard to predict, making it the perfect type of data for live viewing. And weather impacts all of our lives in a very real way, making it a practical information source that is valuable in some Web applications.

This chapter delves into an application that uses Ajax to query a weather server for live weather data, and then displays the weather data in an interesting way. More specifically, the weather data is displayed in a temporary popup window similar to the one shown in [Chapter 9](#ch09.html_ch09), "Presenting Information with Slick Popups." Weather data is ideal for display within a popup because it is concise and often supplementary to the focus of a particular page. In other words, it's the kind of thing you may want to get a quick look at and then dismiss.

The following files are used by the Forecaster application in this chapter, and are located on the live Linux CD-ROM with the book in the `chap10` example code folder:

*   `forecaster.html`— Main Web page

*   `forecaster.php`—Server script for loading a weather forecast

*   `ajaxkit.js`—Core Ajax functions for the Ajax Toolkit

*   `domkit.js`—DOM-related functions for the Ajax Toolkit

*   `infowinkit.js`—Information window-related functions for the Ajax Toolkit

*   `infowinkit.css`—Style sheet for information windows

*   `infowinkit_wait.gif`—Animated "loading" icon that is displayed while loading information into an info window

### The Challenge: Gathering Dynamic Weather Information

This chapter's challenge involves using Ajax to dynamically load weather data for a given location. Live weather information might be useful in all kinds of scenarios: a travel Web site, a sports Web site, really any Web site where weather could feasibly have an impact on the site's content. I personally like the idea of a travel Web site because weather happens to play such a critical role in travel decisions. Maybe you're trying to carefully dodge a storm in the tropics during hurricane season, or maybe you want to check for cold enough weather before embarking on a ski trip early in the season. Just about any Web site that deals with travel could benefit from live weather data.

But it's already possible to get weather conditions very easily, so why bother with Ajax and popup windows? Just go to the [Weather.com](http://Weather.com) Web site and check out the current weather conditions for any city in the world.

Although [Weather.com](http://Weather.com) can't be beaten in terms of the quality of the information it presents, keep in mind that you are required to navigate to it in your browser and then enter the location for the weather report you want. This is a problem if you'd rather not lose the context of the page you're viewing and would instead prefer to get a quick weather update without leaving the page. That's where popup windows enter the picture. By displaying weather data in a popup, you allow the user to continue focusing on the task at hand and not distract him or her by leaving the page. Perhaps more importantly, you remove one excuse for navigating away from your Web site.

So your goal isn't to create a richer or more thorough weather alternative than pure weather sites such as [Weather.com](http://Weather.com). Your goal is to inject live weather data into the context of your existing site, thereby adding some useful new content without the user having to leave the comfort of your pages. Information popup windows are the key to displaying weather data without it competing with the main page being viewed.

The Ajax request involved in this challenge must somehow query a weather service for weather data based upon a specific location. As it turns out, a ZIP code is plenty sufficient to identify a location, and therefore becomes a great piece of data to use for querying weather data. The Ajax response then contains weather data that you must somehow process in order to display meaningful weather information in a popup window.

### The Design: Designing a Weather Forecaster

The application you develop to meet the dynamic weather challenge is called Forecaster, and its design heavily revolves around the existing information window support in the `infowinkit.js` file that is part of the book's Ajax Toolkit. In fact, the Forecaster application is very similar in structure to the Informer application developed in [Chapter 9](#ch09.html_ch09), except it requests live weather information for a particular location as opposed to pre-built HTML content files. [Figure 10.1](#ch10lev1sec2.html_ch10fig01) gives you an idea as to how the user interface of the Forecaster application is structured.

##### Figure 10.1\. The user interface for the Forecaster application consists of a Web page with info links that trigger a popup window containing live weather information.

![](ys8wohawzzmxcjm1mjjlzgftdc9zyzc4cjlnmgkvzmnncdbzmghnmmkuawlmms8-.jpg)

The figure shows how the Forecaster page consists of standard text with city links sprinkled in. Each city link is actually an info link that results in the display of a popup window containing live weather data. This also means that clicking a city link results in an Ajax request that retrieves the weather data so that it can be displayed in a popup window.

In order for an Ajax weather request to work, you actually need access to live weather data in a structured format such as XML. As it turns out, you can use several weather sources on the Internet to accomplish this task. One service I like is the RSS weather feed made available by Yahoo! Weather. This weather data is made available in the RSS format, which is a specific XML data format that is traditionally used to share news, as was shown in [Chapter 5](#ch05.html_ch05), "Tapping into RSS News Feeds." However, there is no reason an RSS feed can't be used for other types of data, and in this case Yahoo! Weather uses RSS for weather data.

Note

| ![](ys8wohawzzmxcjm1mjjlzgftdc9zyzc4cjlnmgkvdgmucg9zagpwawdll24-.jpg) | RSS (Really Simple Syndication) is an XML data format designed for representing news stories in a consistent manner. A typical RSS document, or feed, contains multiple news stories with detailed information such as a title, description, publish date, and a link to the article online. The RSS format can actually be used beyond the realm of news, and is in fact useful for any text information that changes on a regular basis. There are numerous third-party applications, called RSS readers, that allow you to view and access multiple RSS feeds in a single view. |

[Figure 10.2](#ch10lev1sec2.html_ch10fig02) shows the Yahoo! Weather RSS feed Web page ([http://developer.yahoo.com/weather/](http://developer.yahoo.com/weather/)), which contains excellent documentation on the weather feed and how to customize it to suit your needs.

##### Figure 10.2\. The Yahoo! Weather RSS feed Web page offers a live XML weather feed.

[[View full size image]](ys8wohawzzmxcjm1mjjlzgftdc9zyzc4cjlnmgkvzmmuamdwz3awcznhadbsav9pmxqv.jpg)![](ys8wohawzzmxcjm1mjjlzgftdc9zyzc4cjlnmgkvzmnncdbzmghqm3auawlnms8-.jpg)

You learn the basics of how to mine weather data from the Yahoo! Weather feed throughout the rest of the chapter, but feel free to refer back to this Web page to learn more on your own.

Getting back to the design of the Forecaster application, you now have a live weather service that can be used as the basis for Ajax weather requests. Each of these requests is made as a result of the user clicking a city in the Web page text, which happens to be an info link. This means that when a city is clicked, an information window is displayed that contains weather data obtained through an Ajax request made on the Yahoo! Weather server. The next few sections explore this Ajax request in a bit more detail.

#### The Client Request

If you've been progressing through this book sequentially, you may already be thinking that the Ajax request for the weather data is going to require a server-side script in order to deal with the security limitation of not being able to request data from a remote server. In this case the Yahoo! Weather server certainly qualifies as a remote server. You therefore need a PHP script to serve as somewhat of a proxy for carrying out the actual Yahoo! Weather request from the server instead of the client.

Aside from the PHP script on the server, the client request in the Forecaster application is not too messy because the only information it must pass along to the server is the ZIP code of the desired location. The ZIP code is used to identify a location, and the Yahoo! Weather service fortunately is able to look up weather information based solely on a ZIP code. The resulting Ajax client request therefore involves a PHP server script with a ZIP code parameter.

#### The Server Response

The response to the weather request comes in the form of an RSS document full of useful weather data. If you recall, the RSS format is an XML data format, which means you can extract data from it based upon tag names without too much trouble. This is important because you won't be using all of the information returned in the Ajax response.

As you see in the next section, the standard Ajax response delivered by the Yahoo! Weather service contains much more information than is needed for this particular example. For this reason, you need to be able to pluck out specific information using the server script and only return that information to the client. The server script is already a necessity given the security workaround mentioned earlier, so adding a little more code to extract specific weather data won't present too much of a challenge.

#### Making Sense of the Client-to-Server Conversation

You now understand enough about the role of the client and server in the Forecaster application to visualize a single Ajax weather request and its associated response. [Figure 10.3](#ch10lev1sec2.html_ch10fig03) shows the client-to-server conversation taking place with respect to this request.

##### Figure 10.3\. The Forecaster application involves sending a ZIP code from the client and receiving HTML weather content from the server.

![](ys8wohawzzmxcjm1mjjlzgftdc9zyzc4cjlnmgkvzmnncdbzmghnngkuawlmms8-.jpg)

The figure reveals how the Ajax request generates a response that relays weather content back to the browser (client). I'm careful to refer to this weather information as "content" as opposed to "data" because there is a subtle difference. "Data" usually refers to raw information that hasn't been coded or formatted in any particular way, whereas "content" often refers to data that has somehow been processed. In this particular case, the weather information is classified as content because it is returned from the server as HTML code that is ready to be displayed in a popup window.

Getting back to the Ajax request shown in the figure, you pass the ZIP code for a location along with the Ajax request by packaging it into a URL as a single parameter. Here's an example of a URL used to pass along a ZIP code to the PHP script for the Forecaster application:

```
forecaster.php?zipCode=37205
```

This code reveals that the PHP script responsible for handling the Ajax weather request is in the file named `forecaster.php`. This script is responsible for retrieving the RSS weather data from the Yahoo! Weather server, extracting only the weather information desired, and then returning this information to the client. You find out exactly how this script works such weather magic later in the section titled, "The Forecaster Server Script."

The weather data returned from the server is actually much more detailed than is needed for the Forecaster application, which is why the server script only extracts a portion of the data to be returned to the client. It just so happens that the Yahoo! Weather service offers a preformatted chunk of weather information that can easily be accessed separately from the remaining weather data. This chunk is formatted as HTML code that is ready to be displayed in a popup window. Following is an example of what this code looks like:

```
<img src="http://us.i1.yimg.com/us.yimg.com/i/us/we/52/30.gif"/>
<br />
<b>Current Conditions:</b><br />
Partly Cloudy, 66 F<br /><br />
<b>Forecast:</b><br />
Wed - Partly Cloudy. High: 69 Low: 42<br />
Thu - Sunny. High: 61 Low: 32<br /><br />
<a href="http://weather.yahoo.com/forecast/37205_f.html">
  Full Forecast at Yahoo! Weather</a><br />
(provided by The Weather Channel)<br />
```

As you can see, this code is already formatted for display thanks to several HTML tags.

The preformatted weather information is identified in the main RSS weather feed using the `<description>` tag. In other words, the code you just saw is enclosed between `<description>` and `</description>` tags within the RSS XML code. Following is an abbreviated RSS weather feed document that shows how the `<description>` tag is used:

Code View:

```
<rss version="2.0"
xmlns:yweather="http://xml.weather.yahoo.com/ns/rss/1.0"
  xmlns:geo="http://www.w3.org/2003/01/geo/wgs84_pos#">
  <channel>
    <title>Yahoo! Weather - Nashville, TN</title>
    <link>http://weather.yahoo.com/forecast/37205_f.html</link>
    <description>Yahoo! Weather for Nashville, TN</description>
    ...
    <item>
      <title>Conditions for Nashville, TN at 10:53 am CST</title>
      <geo:lat>36.11</geo:lat>
      <geo:long>-86.68</geo:long>
      ...
      <description><![CDATA[
        <img src="http://us.i1.yimg.com/us.yimg.com/i/us/we/52/
          30.gif" /><br />
        <b>Current Conditions:</b><br />
        Partly Cloudy, 66 F<br /><br />
        <b>Forecast:</b><br />
        Wed - Partly Cloudy. High: 69 Low: 42<br />
        Thu - Sunny. High: 61 Low: 32<br /><br />
        <a href="http://weather.yahoo.com/forecast/37205_f.html">
          Full Forecast at Yahoo! Weather</a><br />
        (provided by The Weather Channel)<br />
      ]]></description>
      ...
    </item>
  </channel>
</rss> 
```

This code reveals how the `<description>` tag is embedded down in the RSS code for the weather feed, and how it stores away an HTML-encoded version of the weather information. The job of the Forecaster server script is just to pluck out the contents of the `<description>` tag and return them to the client as the Ajax response.

### Putting Together the Forecaster Application

In addition to the support features provided by the Ajax Toolkit, such as the information popup window, the Forecaster application consists of two components:

*   HTML Web page (`forecaster.html`)

*   PHP server script (`forecaster.php`)

The next two sections examine these pieces of the Forecaster puzzle in more detail. Hopefully you already have a decent idea as to what they do, and possibly even how they do it.

#### The Forecaster Web Page

The HTML Web page for the Forecaster application consists of a paragraph of text with weather info links (cities) sprinkled throughout. The page is deceptively simple in terms of not having any input controls. However, a click of the mouse on any of the linked cities reveals that there is more to Forecaster than initially meets the eye. [Figure 10.4](#ch10lev1sec3.html_ch10fig04) shows a quick preview of the user interface, which will help you understand the HTML code required to make it possible.

##### Figure 10.4\. The Forecaster Web page consists of a paragraph of text with info links used to connect cities to their respective live weather information.

[[View full size image]](ys8wohawzzmxcjm1mjjlzgftdc9zyzc4cjlnmgkvzmmuamdwz3awczvhadbsav9pmxqv.jpg)![](ys8wohawzzmxcjm1mjjlzgftdc9zyzc4cjlnmgkvzmnncdbzmghqnxauawlnms8-.jpg)

Following is the HTML code behind the page (`forecaster.html`) shown in the figure:

Code View:

```
<body onload="initInfoWindow(300, 240)">
  <div id="ajaxState" style="display:none"></div>
  <br />
  <h2>Forecaster - Traveling the U.S.</h2>
  <p>A great way to get the full cultural experience of
    traveling is to hit the road and cover as much terrain as
    possible. Within the U.S., it is possible to experience the
    full range of climates, which can often make planning
    difficult in terms of preparing for different kinds of
    weather.
    For example, let's say you start your trip in <a href="#"
    class="info" onclick="return showInfoWindow(
    'forecaster.php?zipCode=10001', event)">New York City</a>
    with the plan of crossing the country and finishing in
    <a href="#" class="info" onclick="return showInfoWindow(
    'forecaster.php?zipCode=94129', event)">San Francisco</a>.
    Depending upon the time of year, you will experience quite a
    variety of different weather. If your trip cuts deep into
    the south, you'll come to understand why <a href="#"
    class="info" onclick="return showInfoWindow(
    'forecaster.php?zipCode=30301', event)">Atlanta</a> is
    affectionately known as Hotlanta. Cruising west, you'll need
    to keep your eyes peeled for tornadoes as you pass through
    <a href="#" class="info"
    onclick="return showInfoWindow(
    'forecaster.php?zipCode=73101', event)">Oklahoma City</a>.
    If you thought Atlanta was hot, your stop off in
    <a href="#" class="info" onclick="return showInfoWindow(
    'forecaster.php?zipCode=85003', event)">Phoenix</a> will
    quickly let you know why they call it the Valley of the Sun.
    Traveling northward to get a break from the heat, a stop off
    in <a href="#" class="info" onclick="return showInfoWindow(
    'forecaster.php?zipCode=80012', event)">Denver</a> at the
    foot of the Rockies is sure to energize you for the final
    push toward California.
  </p> </body> 
```

The first line of code gets busy with the very important task of initializing the information popup window and establishing its default size (300x240). The remaining code in the page creates the paragraph of text and defines the weather info links. Because the info links are embedded directly in the paragraph of text, and are therefore a bit hard to make out, take a look at one of the links by itself:

```
<a href="#" class="info"
 onclick="return showInfoWindow('forecaster.php?zipCode=94129',
 event)">San Francisco</a>
```

This link is used to launch a popup window containing current weather information for the ZIP code 94129, which is located in San Francisco, California. Notice that the ZIP code is provided as a parameter (`zipCode`) to the `forecaster.php` server script. The entire URL for the server script and ZIP code is passed into the `showInfoWindow()` function, which is the Ajax Toolkit function used to display an information popup window.

Although the body of the Forecaster Web page is where the interesting stuff happens, the head of the page is also important because it imports the needed JavaScript and CSS files. Following is the head of the page:

```
<head>
  <title>Forecaster</title>

  <link rel="stylesheet" type="text/css" href="infowinkit.css" />
  <script type="text/javascript" src="ajaxkit.js"> </script>
  <script type="text/javascript" src="domkit.js"> </script>
  <script type="text/javascript" src="infowinkit.js"> </script>
</head>
```

The CSS file and three JavaScript files are all part of the book's Ajax Toolkit, which provides numerous helpful Ajax functions.

#### The Forecaster Server Script

Because the weather information in the Forecaster application is ultimately loaded from a remote server (Yahoo! Weather), you can't issue an Ajax request directly from the client. The Forecaster server script is necessary in order to circumvent this restriction and access information on a remote server. Not only that, but the server script plays an additional role in that it must extract a specific portion of the weather data and only return that data to the client. In other words, the client doesn't need the entire contents of the Yahoo! Weather feed.

The Forecaster PHP server script expects to be passed a ZIP code that it can then use to request weather data for a specific location. Following is the code for the `forecaster.php` server script:

```
<?php
header('Content-Type: text/xml');

// Parse the XML weather data into a structure
$rawFeed =
file_get_contents("http://weather.yahooapis.com/forecastrss?p=" .
  $_REQUEST["zipCode"]);
$xml = new SimpleXmlElement($rawFeed);
echo "<div>" . $xml->channel->item->description . "</div>";
?>
```

Remember that the job of this script is to retrieve an XML (RSS) document from the Yahoo! Weather service, extract the content within the `<description>` tag, and then pass that HTML code along to the client. All the client has to do is display the HTML weather code in a popup. In actuality, the client delegates these details to the information popup window support in the Ajax Toolkit.

The server script begins by making sure that the response gets treated as XML. The second chunk of code in the script then loads the RSS/XML weather document from the weather service, making sure to pass along the `zipCode` parameter so that the service knows the weather location. The raw XML data returned by the weather service is then converted to a structured XML object so that you can easily access individual elements.

The last section of code is where the weather information is actually returned from the script, which serves as the response to the Ajax weather information request. The formatted HTML weather code is wrapped inside an HTML `div` element before being returned to the client.

Before moving on to test the completed Forecaster application, it's important to first test the PHP script by itself. You've seen this done in previous chapters, but it never ceases to be an important step in any Ajax application that involves a custom server script. Following is a sample URL for testing the Forecaster script:

```
http://www.yourdomain.com/forecaster.php?zipCode=94129
```

Assuming the `forecaster.php` file is stored on your server, change the domain to match yours in this line of code and then enter the URL into your Web browser. You can also try this trick directly on the live CD that accompanies this book. Regardless of where you access the script from, [Figure 10.5](#ch10lev1sec3.html_ch10fig05) shows the HTML response data generated by the PHP script.

##### Figure 10.5\. You can examine the HTML weather information returned by the Forecaster PHP script by opening the script directly in a browser.

[[View full size image]](ys8wohawzzmxcjm1mjjlzgftdc9zyzc4cjlnmgkvzmmuamdwz3awczzhadbsav9pmxqv.jpg)![](ys8wohawzzmxcjm1mjjlzgftdc9zyzc4cjlnmgkvzmnncdbzmghqnnauawlnms8-.jpg)

Feel free to experiment with different ZIP codes in the URL for the Forecaster script. This is a great way to study the data returned by a server script to make sure it is consistent with what you expected—server script problems are much more difficult to track down when you're limited to looking at everything through the eyes of the client Web page.

### Testing Forecaster

It requires a great deal of skill and patience to test the Forecaster application. That is, of course, assuming that you consider clicking the mouse a few times a difficult job. Seriously, this is a very easy application to test out because all you have to do is click a few links on the page. [Figure 10.6](#ch10lev1sec4.html_ch10fig06) shows how the Forecaster application starts out with the weather popup window hidden from view.

##### Figure 10.6\. The Forecaster application starts off with the weather popup window hidden from view because no cities have been clicked yet.

[[View full size image]](ys8wohawzzmxcjm1mjjlzgftdc9zyzc4cjlnmgkvzmmuamdwz3awczdhadbsav9pmxqv.jpg)![](ys8wohawzzmxcjm1mjjlzgftdc9zyzc4cjlnmgkvzmnncdbzmghqn3auawlnms8-.jpg)

Notice in this figure how nothing weather-related appears on the page, meaning that the weather doesn't distract in any way from the main content. This is important because the weather is intended to be supplemental and voluntary—readily available but visible at the user's discretion.

Clicking a city (weather link) results in the display of a weather popup window with the local weather conditions for the city. But keep in mind that the weather data is being loaded from a remote Web service, which doesn't always take place instantaneously. [Figure 10.7](#ch10lev1sec4.html_ch10fig07) shows how an animated "loading" icon appears while the weather information is being loaded.

##### Figure 10.7\. When a link on the page is clicked, an animated "loading" icon appears during the loading of the weather information.

[[View full size image]](ys8wohawzzmxcjm1mjjlzgftdc9zyzc4cjlnmgkvzmmuamdwz3awczhhadbsav9pmxqv.jpg)![](ys8wohawzzmxcjm1mjjlzgftdc9zyzc4cjlnmgkvzmnncdbzmghqohauawlnms8-.jpg)

Unless you're using an antique dial-up modem or the Yahoo! Weather service is experiencing delays, you'll see the weather data for Denver, Colorado, pretty quickly, as shown in [Figure 10.8](#ch10lev1sec4.html_ch10fig08).

##### Figure 10.8\. The dynamically loaded weather information for Denver is displayed in an information popup window.

[[View full size image]](ys8wohawzzmxcjm1mjjlzgftdc9zyzc4cjlnmgkvzmmuamdwz3awczlhadbsav9pmxqv.jpg)![](ys8wohawzzmxcjm1mjjlzgftdc9zyzc4cjlnmgkvzmnncdbzmghqoxauawlnms8-.jpg)

The weather information for Denver is loaded and displayed in an information popup window that floats over the main Forecaster page. This allows you to quickly view the weather without having to actually navigate away from the page. When you're finished viewing the weather, just click anywhere on the popup window and it will disappear.

Getting the weather conditions for another city is as simple as clicking the city's name (info link). [Figure 10.9](#ch10lev1sec4.html_ch10fig09) shows the weather for Atlanta, Georgia, being displayed in a popup.

##### Figure 10.9\. The weather conditions for Atlanta, Georgia, are as readily accessible as those for Denver or any of the other cities on the page.

[[View full size image]](ys8wohawzzmxcjm1mjjlzgftdc9zyzc4cjlnmgkvzmmuamdwz3awczbhadfsav9pmxqv.jpg)![](ys8wohawzzmxcjm1mjjlzgftdc9zyzc4cjlnmgkvzmnncdbzmwhqmhauawlnms8-.jpg)

So maybe 61 degrees Fahrenheit really isn't a justification for the name Hotlanta, but you have to consider that we're talking about February here. Just check back with the Forecaster application in July and you'll see what I'm talking about.

### Giving Forecaster an Extreme Ajax Makeover

The quickest and simplest way to make over the Forecaster application is to try out some different cities. For example, change one of the cities in the Web page to your own hometown, making sure to replace the ZIP code with your own ZIP code in the info link for the city. More specifically, be sure to change the URL passed into the `showInfoWindow()` function so that it references your ZIP code. As an example, if your ZIP code is 92024 (Encinitas, CA), you'll want to change the `showInfoWindow()` function in the info link `onclick` event handler to this:

```
onclick="return showInfoWindow('forecaster.php?zipCode=92024',
  event)"
```

The info link will then pass along your ZIP code to the weather server when requesting weather data, and will therefore display your own local live weather conditions in the information popup window.

If you have some PHP and JavaScript skills and want to go for a more extreme makeover of the Forecaster application, you might want to consider drilling down into the RSS weather data returned by Yahoo! Weather. The Forecaster application currently takes advantage of the fact that the Yahoo! RSS feed includes a `<description>` tag that provides a clean, HTML-friendly weather view. The feed actually includes quite a bit more information, giving you plenty of freedom in customizing a more detailed weather view if you so desire.

As an example, the following fields can be accessed within the Yahoo! Weather feed to get information that doesn't appear in the `<description>` tag:

```
<wind chill="57" direction="230" speed="7" />
<atmosphere humidity="72" visibility="1609" pressure="30.22"
  rising="0" />
<astronomy sunrise="6:28 am" sunset="5:38 pm" />
```

Here, you see the wind chill, wind direction, and wind speed in the first `<wind>` tag. The second tag (`<atmosphere>`) addresses atmospheric conditions such as humidity, visibility, barometric pressure, and how much the pressure is either rising or lowering. And finally, the third tag (`<astronomy>`) provides the sunrise and sunset times for the location. To help make sense of the first two tags, another tag specifies the units for the weather data:

```
<units temperature="F" distance="mi" pressure="in" speed="mph" />
```

This line of code lets you know that all temperatures are expressed in degrees Fahrenheit, distances are in miles, barometric pressures are in inches, and wind speeds are in miles per hour. When assembling a custom view of the weather data, you can pluck out these units and append them to the weather values to provide additional detail and give the values more meaning.

### Summary

You already are no doubt aware of the fact that there are numerous sources on the Web for getting live weather information for pretty much any location on the planet. What isn't so easily done is closely tying such information to your own pages in a manner that doesn't take away from the user's experience. Information popup windows provide a nifty mechanism for displaying weather data within the context of your pages without requiring the user to navigate away from them. The Forecaster application serves as a good demonstration of how straightforward it is to include location-specific live weather data in a page of your own.

## Chapter 11. Building an Ajax Shipping Calculator

Ajax at High Altitude

| ![](ys8wohawzzmxcjm1mjjlzgftdc9zyzc4cjlnmgkvdgmucg9zagpwawdll24-.jpg) | If you're into ritzy skiing, you may be familiar with Ajax as it applies to mountains. Situated immediately to the south of the posh town of Aspen, Colorado, Aspen Mountain is home to the Aspen/Snowmass ski areas. Locals sometimes refer to Aspen Mountain as Ajax. |

Don't you get tired of the part of the online shopping experience where you're interested in buying something but you need to know the shipping costs in order to determine whether it's really a deal? Problem is, most shopping carts don't clue you in on the shipping charge until the very last step of the checkout process, after you've entered your name, address, date of birth, mother's maiden name, favorite childhood pet, and whatever other personal data they can get from you before finalizing the purchase. Wouldn't it be nice to get the shipping charge early on in the checkout process, definitely before entering your credit card information, and preferably without a page reload? As you might be guessing, Ajax offers a better way.

This chapter solves the shipping charge problem by showing you how to use Ajax to dynamically determine a shipping charge without the user even realizing it. Not only that, but the shipping charge is obtained directly from the U.S. Postal Service shipping charge Web service, which means you aren't guessing or dealing with potentially outdated lookup tables—you're getting the information directly from the source. The Shipper sample application in this chapter shows conclusively how Ajax can elegantly solve a common problem and sneakily improve the feel of any Web application that involves the calculation of shipping charges.

The following files are used by the Shipper application in this chapter, and are located on the live Linux CD-ROM with the book in the `chap11` example code folder:

*   `shipper.html`—Main Web page

*   `shipper.php`—Server script for loading a shipping charge

*   `ajaxkit.js`—Core Ajax functions for the Ajax Toolkit

*   `domkit.js`—DOM-related functions for the Ajax Toolkit

*   `wait.gif`—Animated "loading" icon that is displayed while loading a shipping charge

### The Challenge: Retrieving Live Shipping Charges

The challenge in this chapter is simple in theory but somewhat tricky in practice. The trickiness arises from the fact that the Web service used to obtain shipping charge information isn't as trivial to access as some of the other Web services you've seen throughout the book. More specifically, the Shipper application in this chapter uses the U.S. Postal Service Web Tools to retrieve shipping charges. The problem arises from the fact that the USPS Web service requires you to submit a fairly detailed XML document in order to obtain a shipping charge.

Of course, this shouldn't be too surprising when you consider that several pieces of information are involved in determining how much it costs to ship an item. You have the origin and destination locations (ZIP codes), plus specifics about the item itself, such as its size and weight. And then you have to choose from several possible shipping options, such as Parcel Post or Priority Mail. All of this information must be carefully tucked away into an XML document that the USPS Web service understands.

To give the Shipper application a practical feel, its user interface simulates a shopping cart where you've already selected a specific item for purchase and are ready to check out. To keep things simple, however, you aren't providing control over the contents of the shopping cart. In other words, the Shipper application focuses solely on accepting destination shipping information so that it can dynamically calculate the shipping charge. Building a fully functioning shopping cart is a bit beyond the scope of this chapter.

Even though the contents of the shopping cart can't be manipulated by the user in the Shipper application, the destination ZIP code and shipping method are both changeable. And this gets us to where Ajax enters the picture. Any time the ZIP code or shipping method changes, an Ajax request is made on the USPS Web service to grab a new shipping charge and update the cart. You're able to update the cart thanks to the fact that the application already knows how much the item in the cart costs. So it's merely a matter of adding the item price to the shipping charge to display a total for the shopping cart.

Again, even though the application gives the illusion of a fully functioning shopping cart, all it's really doing is using static shopping cart data combined with dynamic shipping information (ZIP code and shipping method) to obtain a shipping charge via Ajax and update the total price of the transaction. This keeps the challenge tightly focused on the subject at hand—using Ajax to dynamically retrieve a shipping charge from the USPS Web service.

## Chapter 11\. Building an Ajax Shipping Calculator

Ajax at High Altitude

| ![](ys8wohawzzmxcjm1mjjlzgftdc9zyzc4cjlnmgkvdgmucg9zagpwawdll24-.jpg) | If you're into ritzy skiing, you may be familiar with Ajax as it applies to mountains. Situated immediately to the south of the posh town of Aspen, Colorado, Aspen Mountain is home to the Aspen/Snowmass ski areas. Locals sometimes refer to Aspen Mountain as Ajax. |

*   `shipper.html`—Main Web page

*   `shipper.php`—Server script for loading a shipping charge

*   `ajaxkit.js`—Core Ajax functions for the Ajax Toolkit

*   `domkit.js`—DOM-related functions for the Ajax Toolkit

*   `wait.gif`—Animated "loading" icon that is displayed while loading a shipping charge

The challenge in this chapter is simple in theory but somewhat tricky in practice. Of course, this shouldn't be too surprising when you consider that several pieces of information are involved in determining how much it costs to ship an item. And then you have to choose from several possible shipping options, such as Parcel Post or Priority Mail. All of this information must be carefully tucked away into an XML document that the USPS Web service understands.

To give the Shipper application a practical feel, its user interface simulates a shopping cart where you've already selected a specific item for purchase and are ready to check out. To keep things simple, however, you aren't providing control over the contents of the shopping cart. In other words, the Shipper application focuses solely on accepting destination shipping information so that it can dynamically calculate the shipping charge. And this gets us to where Ajax enters the picture. Any time the ZIP code or shipping method changes, an Ajax request is made on the USPS Web service to grab a new shipping charge and update the cart. So it's merely a matter of adding the item price to the shipping charge to display a total for the shopping cart.

Again, even though the application gives the illusion of a fully functioning shopping cart, all it's really doing is using static shopping cart data combined with dynamic shipping information (ZIP code and shipping method) to obtain a shipping charge via Ajax and update the total price of the transaction. This keeps the challenge tightly focused on the subject at hand—using Ajax to dynamically retrieve a shipping charge from the USPS Web service.

### The Design: Designing a Shipper Application

Because the Shipper application is attempting to simulate a shopping cart, some of its user interface elements are purely for decoration and don't directly impact the functionality. [Figure 11.1](#ch11lev1sec2.html_ch11fig01) shows a sketch of the application's user interface, along with which fields play a direct role in the retrieval and display of shipping charge information.

##### Figure 11.1\. The user interface for the Shipper application includes fields for entering a ZIP code and viewing the resulting shipping charge, among others.

![](ys8wohawzzmxcjm1mjjlzgftdc9zyzc4cjlnmgkvzmnncdfzmghnmwkuawlmms8-.jpg)

The figure shows several input fields, but only two of them, ZIP code and Shipping method, directly impact the calculation of the shipping charge. This shipping charge calculation involves submitting the ZIP code, shipping method, and other details about the item being shipped to the USPS Web service for domestic shipping rates. Upon successfully retrieving the shipping charge from the USPS server, the Shipper application calculates an order total by adding the item price to the shipping charge.

Because it is certainly possible for the user to enter a ZIP code that is invalid, and therefore won't result in a shipping charge, a help text display field next to the ZIP code is used to let the user know whether a problem exists with the ZIP code. This field gets set if the server returns a shipping charge of `0`, which indicates that there was a problem with the ZIP code.

Another subtle touch to the Shipper application that isn't revealed in the figure is the usage of an animated "loading" image to indicate the status of the Ajax request. This image appears in place of the shipping charge while the charge is being looked up on the USPS server. [Figure 11.2](#ch11lev1sec2.html_ch11fig02) shows what this small image, `wait.gif`, looks like.

##### Figure 11.2\. The Shipper application makes use of a small animated "loading" image (`wait.gif`) to let the user know the Ajax request is being processed.

![](ys8wohawzzmxcjm1mjjlzgftdc9zyzc4cjlnmgkvzmnncdfzmghqmnauawlnms8-.jpg)

Because the user may still be busy entering information while the shipping charge request is being processed, it isn't imperative to make the "loading" image prominent on the page. That's why it appears in place of the shipping charge. Of course, this means the shipping charge field performs double duty, but that doesn't pose a problem. Just know that the field holds the actual shipping charge when a request isn't taking place, and it displays the "loading" image during a request.

Despite the considerable number of input fields in the Shipper application's user interface, keep in mind that only two of them (ZIP code and shipping method) directly play into the shipping charge Ajax request. And only a couple of other fields (Shipping charge and Order total) factor into the resulting display of the requested shipping charge information.

So even though the application looks fairly involved, it is really only dealing with one Ajax request, which helps to simplify things considerably. You're now ready to learn a little more about the shipping charge Ajax request.

### The Client Request

The client request for the Shipper application is one of the trickier Ajax requests you encounter in the book because it is responsible for passing along a fair amount of information to the server. Before getting into the details of that information, however, let's quickly address the fact that we're dealing with a remote Web server.

As you'll recall from numerous other sample applications, it isn't possible to issue an Ajax request directly on a third-party (remote) Web server for security reasons. The way around this limitation is to use your own server script as the basis for forwarding along the request to the remote server, which is allowed because server-to-server communication is considered perfectly legit. As it turns out, a server script is needed in this case for reasons beyond the Ajax security limitation, so you'd be dealing with a PHP script anyway.

I've mentioned already that the Shipper application only needs to pass along the ZIP code and shipping method in order to obtain a live shipping charge from the USPS Web server. Although this may be true from the perspective of the application, it isn't true at all from the perspective of the USPS. Considerably more information is needed in order to determine how much it costs to mail an item. More specifically, the following information is needed by the USPS Domestic Rates Web service in order to successfully return a shipping charge:

*   User ID

*   Password

*   Shipping method

*   Origin ZIP code

*   Destination ZIP code

*   Weight of item (pounds)

*   Weight of item (ounces)

*   Container type

*   Size

*   Machinable (within normal package limitations)

The first two pieces of information are the user ID and password for your USPS Web Tools account, which are necessary in order to even test the Shipper application. In fact, you'll have to obtain your own user ID before you can even run the application. Like I said earlier, the USPS is much stricter about allowing access to its Web services than other services you've encountered throughout the book. In turn, you'll be able to safely reference your USPS user ID and password from a server script, as opposed to a client Web page, which provides an added measure of security for the Shipper application.

Note

| ![](ys8wohawzzmxcjm1mjjlzgftdc9zyzc4cjlnmgkvdgmucg9zagpwawdll24-.jpg) | For testing purposes, you'll likely just be assigned a user ID and no password, meaning that you can leave the password blank when issuing a shipping rate request. |

You should have a decent feel for the next pieces of information because we've already talked a fair amount about the shipping method and ZIP codes. However, it is worth pointing out that the Shipper application hard-codes the origin ZIP code to a test ZIP code required by the USPS for testing. In a production environment, you'll change this ZIP code to the ZIP code from which you'll be sending packages.

Note

| ![](ys8wohawzzmxcjm1mjjlzgftdc9zyzc4cjlnmgkvdgmucg9zagpwawdll24-.jpg) | When I say the application "hard-codes" a piece of information, I simply mean that the application sets the information internally instead of allowing the user to select it or enter it. This is primarily a simplification in the case of the Shipper application, although some of the hard-coded information is unlikely to change even in a production application. |

The two weights passed along to the USPS Web service are necessary to break down a package weight into its different unit components. These weights are used together, meaning that a package that weighs 4 pounds 3 ounces results in a "pounds" value of 4 and an "ounces" value of 3\. In other words, it doesn't mean that you provide the total weight in different units. As another example, if a package weighs 6 pounds 0 ounces, then the "pounds" value is 6 and the "ounces" value is 0\. The Shipper application again hard-codes these values because it is only simulating a shopping cart and assumes that you've already selected an item at this point in the checkout process.

The container type describes the container that houses the package, and for testing purposes must be set to "Flat Rate Box." Although you might want to incorporate other container types into a production application, Flat Rate Box covers an awful lot of packages. This is another piece of information that is hard-coded in the Shipper application.

The size of the package must correspond to one of the standard package size designations imposed by the USPS. For testing purposes, you must set the package size to "Large." Not surprisingly, this piece of information is hard-coded in the Shipper application.

The last thing you need to pass along to the USPS Web service is whether or not the package is "machinable," which refers to whether or not the package fits within certain predictable constraints.
