Nonmachinable packages include extremely large packages, irregularly shaped containers, and other scenarios where you aren't shipping a reasonably sized flat- or box-shaped container. For testing purposes, you must specify that the package is machinable. I'm sure you guessed it—the machinable piece of request information is hard-coded in the Shipper application.

I don't expect you to commit to memory the different pieces of information required for a USPS shipping rate Ajax request. You see them again in a moment when you explore the specific XML code used to format them for the USPS server.

### The Server Response

Just as the USPS Domestic Rates Web service expects an XML document as the basis for calculating a shipping rate, it also returns the rate in the form of an XML document. In fact, it is typically used to return several shipping rates at once, in which case it is the job of the application processing the request to pluck out whatever specific rates are needed. In the case of the Shipper application, it is the job of the PHP server script to receive the USPS XML response data and sift through it to find the desired shipping rate.

The next section digs much deeper into the data passed back and forth with the USPS Domestic Rates Web service, including the specific XML data sent back as the USPS server response.

### Making Sense of the Client-to-Server Conversation

The Ajax request within the Shipper application is used to shuttle a user-entered ZIP code and shipping method to the server, along with several other fixed pieces of USPS shipping information. In return, the PHP server script returns to the Shipper client Web page a single shipping charge. [Figure 11.3](#ch11lev1sec5.html_ch11fig03) helps to clarify this transfer of data visually.

##### Figure 11.3\. The Shipper application involves several pieces of shipping information being sent to the server and a single shipping rate returned to the client.

![](ys8wohawzzmxcjm1mjjlzgftdc9zyzc4cjlnmgkvzmnncdfzmghnm2kuawlmms8-.jpg)

It's very important before we move any further into the details of the Shipping application to clarify that you are required to test any application that uses the USPS Web Tools on a USPS test server before being allowed to use a production server. In practical terms, this means that you are limited to a very specific set of test data for use while testing your application. This means that the test data you're about to see for the Shipper application is not arbitrary—it is provided specifically by the USPS for use with its test server.

Note

| ![](ys8wohawzzmxcjm1mjjlzgftdc9zyzc4cjlnmgkvdgmucg9zagpwawdll24-.jpg) | If you deviate from the standard USPS test data while still using the USPS test server, the Shipper application will not work. You can't try out a full range of test data (ZIP codes) until you become validated by the USPS and gain access to its production server. You find out more about how to get validated later in the chapter in the "Giving Shipper an Extreme Ajax Makeover" section. |

The test data imposed by the USPS even limits you to using a specific origin ZIP code and a specific destination ZIP code. Following is an example of an Ajax request URL that uses the standard USPS test data set to retrieve shipping rate data for the Shipper application:

```
shipper.php?userID=YOURUSPSUSERID&origZip=10022&destZip=20008&
weightLb=10&weightOz=5&container=Flat%20Rate%20Box%20&
size=Large&mailService=Priority%20Mail
```

First off, understand that this is one long URL, even though it is broken across several lines on the page. All of the information should be at least vaguely familiar because you've already learned about the pieces required for a USPS Domestic Rates shipping request. One thing I haven't fully explained is the user ID, which you'll notice is set to `YOURUSPSUSERID`. You may have also noticed that no password is specified in the URL. What gives?

In order to access the USPS Web Tools, you are required to have your own user ID that is issued to you by the USPS, which is very strict about the sharing of user IDs. This is why I couldn't just print mine here for you to use. So, before you go any further, I encourage you to register (for free) for your own USPS Web Tools user ID at [http://www.usps.com/webtools/](http://www.usps.com/webtools/). After obtaining this ID you'll be able to plug it into the URL you just saw to complete a Shipper Ajax request URL.

During the testing phase of a USPS Web services application, it isn't necessary to provide a password, which is why a password isn't specified in the URL. In a production environment, you would certainly use a password; the `shipper.php` server script accepts a password via the `password` parameter in the request URL.

It's important at this point to distinguish between the Ajax request URL that is issued on your server and the USPS request URL that is issued on the USPS server. The former URL is used to pass data along to your `shipper.php` server script, whereas the latter is used within your `shipper.php` server script to carry out the actual shipping rate query on the USPS server. So, the `shipper.php` script resides on your server, and is responsible for accepting shipping parameters via a URL and then using them to build another URL that passes along a query to the USPS server.

You just saw the URL for the Ajax request, so now let's turn our attention to the USPS request, which expects the shipping information to be formatted in XML:

```
http://testing.shippingapis.com/ShippingAPITest.dll?API=RateV2&
XML=<RateV2Request%20USERID="YOURUSPSUSERID"%20PASSWORD="">
<Package%20ID="0"><Service>All</Service><ZipOrigination>10022
</ZipOrigination><ZipDestination>20008</ZipDestination><Pounds>
10</Pounds><Ounces>5</Ounces><Container>Flat%20Rate%20Box
</Container><Size>Large</Size><Machinable>true</Machinable>
</Package></RateV2Request>
```

Yikes! I know you're thinking that is some messy looking XML code. And when crammed together in the form of a URL, it certainly is. But let's add some line breaks and tabs to show it in a more traditional XML view:

```
http://testing.shippingapis.com/ShippingAPITest.dll?API=RateV2&
XML=
<RateV2Request USERID="YOURUSPSUSERID" PASSWORD="">
  <Package ID="0">
    <Service>All</Service>
    <ZipOrigination>10022</ZipOrigination>
    <ZipDestination>20008</ZipDestination>
    <Pounds>10</Pounds>
    <Ounces>5</Ounces>
    <Container>Flat Rate Box</Container>
    <Size>Large</Size>
    <Machinable>true</Machinable>
  </Package>
</RateV2Request>
```

Now the USPS request URL should make a bit more sense. All that's going on is that the Ajax request data has now been formatted into an XML document that adheres to the USPS Domestic Rates request format. The only two pieces of information that don't map directly to data that you've passed along are the `<Service>` and `<Machinable>` tags, which are used to indicate that you want the shipping rates for all shipping methods (services) and that the package is indeed machinable (nothing unusual in terms of packaging).

Upon this request being issued to the USPS server, a different XML document is sent back in return:

Code View:

```
<RateV2Response>
  <Package ID="0">
    <ZipOrigination>10022</ZipOrigination>
    <ZipDestination>20008</ZipDestination>
    <Pounds>10</Pounds>
    <Ounces>5</Ounces>
    <Size>LARGE</Size>
    <Machinable>TRUE</Machinable>
    <Zone>3</Zone>
    <Postage>
      <MailService>Express Mail to PO Addressee</MailService>
      <Rate>39.20</Rate>
    </Postage>
    <Postage>
      <MailService>Priority Mail</MailService>
      <Rate>8.95</Rate>
    </Postage>
    <Postage>
      <MailService>Parcel Post</MailService>
      <Rate>7.80</Rate>
    </Postage>
    <Postage>
      <MailService>Bound Printed Matter</MailService>
      <Rate>3.53</Rate>
    </Postage>
    <Postage>
      <MailService>Media Mail</MailService>
      <Rate>5.14</Rate>
    </Postage>
    <Postage>
      <MailService>Library Mail</MailService>
      <Rate>4.91</Rate>
    </Postage>
  </Package>
</RateV2Response> 
```

This response document repeats some of the original request information but also includes lots of shipping rate information. In fact, because the request specified `All` as the shipping method, the response includes shipping rates for all the possible shipping methods. And because the shipping rates are in the XML format, it's not difficult at all to extract a specific rate in PHP code. As an example, the server.php script would return the following piece of data in response to querying for the Priority Mail shipping method:

```
8.95
```

Hopefully you're now starting to get the picture for how data is shuttled around in the Shipper application. Just to make sure you didn't get lost in the whirlwind of URLs and XML code, here's a recap of how the process works:

1.  Your client Web page issues an Ajax request to your PHP server script via a URL containing shipping information.

2.  Your PHP server script builds another URL with XML shipping information and issues a request to the USPS Domestic Rates Web service.

3.  The USPS Domestic Rates Web service returns an XML document containing shipping rates for all shipping methods.

4.  Your PHP server script parses out the shipping rate for the desired shipping method and returns it to your client Web page.

5.  The end.

But this isn't truly the end because you haven't even seen the code that makes all this stuff happen. It's obviously time to put everything together.

### Putting Together the Shipper Application

Despite what seems like a fairly complex data communication model, the Shipper application is pretty simple in terms of pieces and parts. In fact, only two files go into the application outside of the standard Ajax Toolkit files:

*   HTML Web page (`shipper.html`)

*   PHP server script (`shipper.php`)

The next couple sections explore these two pieces of the Shipper application puzzle in more detail.

### The Shipper Web Page

The HTML Web page for the Shipper application consists of several text input fields that allow the user to enter shipping information. Most of these fields are decoration to help support the notion of a shopping cart checkout. The ZIP code field obviously does play an important role in the function of the application, as does the shipping method selection box. [Figure 11.4](#ch11lev1sec7.html_ch11fig04) shows the Shipper application in use with an invalid ZIP code so as to reveal the error message display area to the right of the ZIP code input control.

##### Figure 11.4\. The Shipper Web page consists of several input controls and display areas, although the ZIP code input and shipping charge output fields are by far the most important.

[[View full size image]](ys8wohawzzmxcjm1mjjlzgftdc9zyzc4cjlnmgkvzmmuamdwz3axczrhadbsav9pmxqv.jpg)![](ys8wohawzzmxcjm1mjjlzgftdc9zyzc4cjlnmgkvzmnncdfzmghqnhauawlnms8-.jpg)

With the layout of the Shipper application in mind, as revealed in the figure, check out the HTML code for the body of the `shipper.html` Web page:

Code View:

```
<body onload="initForm()">
  <div id="ajaxState" style="display:none"></div>
  <br />
  <table width="500px">
    <tr>
      <td class="heading" colspan="3">Shipping Information</td>
    </tr>
    <tr>
      <td class="label">Name:</td>
      <td class="value" colspan="2"><input type="text"
        id="name" size="30" /></td>
    </tr>
    <tr>
      <td class="label">Address 1:</td>
      <td class="value" colspan="2"><input type="text"
        id="addr1" size="30" /></td>
    </tr>
    <tr>
      <td class="label">Address 2:</td>
      <td class="value" colspan="2"><input type="text"
        id="addr2" size="30" /></td>
    </tr>
    <tr>
      <td class="label">City:</td>
      <td class="value" colspan="2"><input type="text"
        id="city" size="20" /></td>
    </tr>
    <tr>
      <td class="label">State/Province/Region:</td>
      <td class="value" colspan="2"><input type="text"
        id="state" size="3" /></td>
    </tr>
    <tr>
      <td class="label">ZIP/Postal Code:</td>
      <td class="value"><input type="text" id="zipCode" size="10"
        onchange="getShipRate()" /></td>
      <td class="label" width="50%"><span id="zipCodeErr"
        style="font-size:80%; color:#BB0000;"></span></td>
    </tr>
    <tr>
      <td class="label">Country:</td>
      <td class="value" colspan="2"><input type="text"
         id="country" size="25" value="USA"
         readonly="readonly" /></td>
    </tr>
    <tr>
      <td class="label">Phone Number:</td>
      <td class="value" colspan="2"><input type="text" id="phone"
        size="10" /></td>
    </tr>
  </table>
  <br />
  <table width="500px">
    <tr>
      <td colspan="3" class="heading">Order Summary</td>
   </tr>
   <tr>
      <td class="label">Item:</td>
      <td class="label"><span id="item"></span></td>
      <td class="label"><span id="price"></span></td>
   </tr>
   <tr>
      <td class="label">Shipping & Handling:</td>
      <td class="label">
        <select id="mailService" onchange="getShipRate()">
          <option value="Parcel Post">Parcel Post</option>
          <option value="Priority Mail" selected="selected">
            Priority Mail</option>
          <option value="Express Mail to PO Addressee">
            Express Mail</option>
        </select>
       </td>
       <td class="label"><span id="shiprate">0.00</span></td>
    </tr>
    <tr>
       <td class="label">Total:</td>
       <td class="label"></td>
       <td class="label"><span id="total"></span></td>
    </tr>
  </table>
  <table width="500px">
    <tr>
       <td class="heading"><input type="button"
         value="Proceed with Checkout" /></td>
    </tr>
   </table>
 </body> 
```

Much of this HTML code is devoted to the labels and text boxes for the various input fields. The only input field of consequence is the ZIP code input control, which calls the `getShipRate()` function in its `onchange` event handler. Here's this code again just in case you're having a hard time seeing it in the big listing:

```
<input type="text" id="zipCode" size="10";
  onchange="getShipRate()" />
```

Immediately following the ZIP code `input` element is a `span` element that is used to display ZIP code error message. You have to skip on down the code a bit before you encounter another item of interest, namely the mail service (shipping method) selection list. This list contains three possible options (Parcel Post, Priority Mail, and Express Mail to PO Addressee) and also calls the `getShipRate()` function in response to an `onchange` event. Here's this list broken out by itself:

```
<select id="mailService" onchange="getShipRate()">
  <option value="Parcel Post">Parcel Post</option>
  <option value="Priority Mail" selected="selected">
    Priority Mail</option>
  <option value="Express Mail to PO Addressee">
    Express Mail</option>
</select>
```

Following the mail service selection list you'll find a few important `span` elements used to display the results of the shipping rate request. The `span` element with an ID of `shiprate` is used to store the actual shipping rate, whereas the element with an ID of `total` is used to display the order total as calculated after retrieving the shipping rate.

Finally, the Proceed with Checkout button near the end of the code is just there for decoration to indicate that there is a final step in order to complete the online order.

Going back to the start of the body HTML code for the Shipper Web page, you might have noticed a call to the `initForm()` function in the body's `onload` event handler. Here's the code for that function:

```
function initForm() {
  replaceText(document.getElementById("item"), itemDesc);
  replaceText(document.getElementById("price"), itemPrice);
}
```

The purpose of this code is to initially set the values of the item description and price on the page when the page is first loaded. Neither of these pieces of information impacts the shipping rate in any direct way, although the price does factor into the calculation of the order total.

The item description and price are hard-coded in the Web page, along with several other shipping-related variables. Following is the code that establishes values for these variables:

```
var origZip = "10022"; // Origination ZIP code
var itemDesc = "Popcorn Holiday Feast";
var itemPrice = 29.99;
var weightLb = "10";
var weightOz = "5";
var container = "Flat Rate Box";
var size = "Large";
```

Notice how the shipping-related data is deliberately set to match the USPS test data that was mentioned earlier in the chapter. This is very important for testing purposes because the USPS test server won't accept any other data.

Earlier I mentioned the `getShipRate()` function, which is called to initiate the Ajax request for obtaining a shipping rate. Following is the code for this function:

```
function getShipRate() {
  // Display the wait image
  document.getElementById("shiprate").innerHTML =
    "<img src='wait.gif' alt='Looking up shipping rate...' />";

  // Assemble the shipping info request string
  var shipInfo = "userID=YOURUSPSUSERID" +
    "&origZip=" + encodeURIComponent(origZip) +
    encodeURIComponent(
    document.getElementById("zipCode").value) +
    "&weightLb=" + encodeURIComponent(weightLb) +
    "&weightOz=" + encodeURIComponent(weightOz) +
    "&container=" + encodeURIComponent(container) +
    "&size=" + encodeURIComponent(size) +
    "&mailService=" +
    encodeURIComponent(
    document.getElementById("mailService").value);
  ajaxSendRequest("GET", "shipper.php?" + shipInfo,
    handleRequest);
}
```

The first task in this function is to set the `shiprate` element so that it displays the "loading" image (`wait.gif`). With the "loading" image displayed, the function moves on to assembling the shipping information, which comes in the form of a URL that is submitted via a `GET` request on the `shipper.php` server script. It is very important that you alter this code to include your own unique user ID in the assignment of the `userID` parameter. In other words, change `YOURUSPSUSERID` to the ID that was provided to you when you registered for USPS Web Tools.

The Ajax shipping request is handled by the `handleRequest()` function, which is responsible for receiving a single shipping rate from the server script and updating the page appropriately. Following is the code for this function:

```
function handleRequest() {
  ajaxUpdateState();
  if (request.readyState == 4 && request.status == 200) {
    var shipRate = request.responseText;
    if (parseFloat(shipRate) != 0.0) {
      replaceText(document.getElementById("zipCodeErr"), "");
      updateTotal(shipRate);
    }
    else {
      replaceText(document.getElementById("zipCodeErr"),
        "Cannot ship to this ZIP code.");
      updateTotal("0.00");
    }
  }
}
```

This function is fairly simple for an Ajax request handler, and primarily involves converting the shipping rate into a floating-point number and then calculating the order total with a call to `updateTotal()`. The error message area of the page is also set so that a message is displayed if the request failed or the message is cleared if the request is successful.

Hang on because we're almost finished with the code for the Shipper Web page. Following is the last code you need to know about, which is the `updateTotal()` function:

```
function updateTotal(shipRate) {
  document.getElementById("shiprate").innerHTML = shipRate;
  var total = itemPrice + parseFloat(shipRate);
  replaceText(document.getElementById("total"), "$" + total);
}
```

The `updateTotal()` function accepts a new shipping rate as its only parameter, and then updates the page and calculates a new order total based on the rate. One subtlety in this code that may seem confusing at first is the usage of `innerHTML` versus the `replaceText()` function. Ideally you would use `replaceText()` only, because it doesn't alter the structure of the underlying HTML code. However, the fact that the shipping rate `span` element serves double duty as a rate display and a "loading" image display means that you must use `innerHTML` instead. This is necessary because switching between text and an image does alter the structure of the HTML code, which isn't possible using the `replaceText()` function alone.

### The Shipper Server Script

With the needs of the client side of the Shipper application in mind, it's time to turn your attention to the server. You've already learned that the role of the `shipper.php` script is to use shipping information that has been passed to it to assemble an XML document for submission to the USPS Domestic Rates Web service. It then must take the resulting response XML document and extract a specific shipping rate from it.

Although this sounds like it may take a lot of messy code, the PHP server script for the Shipper application actually isn't very complex, as the following code reveals:

```
// Generate an XML document for the USPS server request
$uspsRequest =
  "http://testing.shippingapis.com/ShippingAPITest.dll?";
$uspsRequest .= "API=RateV2&XML=";
$uspsRequest .= "<RateV2Request%20USERID=\"";
$uspsRequest .= $_REQUEST["userID"];
$uspsRequest .= "\"%20PASSWORD=\"";
$uspsRequest .= $_REQUEST["password"] . "\">";
$uspsRequest .= "<Package%20ID=\"0\"><Service>" . "All";
$uspsRequest .= "</Service>";
$uspsRequest .= "<ZipOrigination>" . $_REQUEST["origZip"];
$uspsRequest .= "</ZipOrigination>";
$uspsRequest .= "<ZipDestination>" . $_REQUEST["destZip"];
$uspsRequest .= "</ZipDestination>";
$uspsRequest .= "<Pounds>" . $_REQUEST["weightLb"] . "</Pounds>";
$uspsRequest .= "<Ounces>" . $_REQUEST["weightOz"] . "</Ounces>";
$uspsRequest .= "<Container>" . urlencode($_REQUEST["container"]);
$uspsRequest .= "</Container>";
$uspsRequest .= "<Size>" . $_REQUEST["size"] . "</Size>";
$uspsRequest .= "<Machinable>" . "true" . "</Machinable>";
$uspsRequest .= "</Package></RateV2Request>";
```

This code isn't the entire PHP script, but it is the biggest chunk of code, responsible for assembling an XML document from the shipping information passed into the script as PHP parameters.

With the USPS request XML document ready to roll, the script goes ahead and issues the request, receiving a response XML document in return:

```
// Parse the shipping response data into a structure
$rawShipping = file_get_contents($uspsRequest);
$xml = new SimpleXmlElement($rawShipping);
```

You now have the XML response data stored in the `$xml` PHP variable and are ready to parse out the specific shipping rate that matches the shipping method passed along in the `mailService` parameter:

```
// Return the rate for the appropriate mail service
$rate = 0;
if ($xml->Package->Postage != null) {
  foreach ($xml->Package->Postage as $postage) {
    if ($postage->MailService == $_REQUEST['mailService']) {
      $rate = $postage->Rate;
      break;
    }
  }
}
echo $rate;
```

This code steps through the response XML document looking for `<Postage>` tags. Within each `<Postage>` tag found, the contents of the child `<MailService>` tag is checked against the specified mail service, such as Priority Mail. If a match is found, the contents of the `<Rate>` tag are returned from the script for the client to process.

Although I'm sure you're itching to get a break from all this code and test out the Shipper application, let's take a second to test the PHP script by itself before moving on. You saw how this is done in earlier chapters by opening PHP scripts directly in a browser—here's a sample URL for testing the Shipper script:

```
http://www.yourdomain.com/shipper.php?userID=YOURUSPSUSERID&
origZip=10022&destZip=20008&weightLb=10&weightOz=5&container=
Flat%20Rate%20Box&size=Large&mailService=Priority%20Mail
```

Assuming the `shipper.php` file is stored on your server, change the domain to match yours in this line of code, replace `YOURUSPSUSERID` with your user ID, and then enter the URL into your Web browser. Or use the following URL directly on the live CD accompanying the book, still making sure to replace `YOURUSPSUSERID` with your own user ID:

```
http://localhost/chap11/shipper/shipper.php?
userID=YOURUSPSUSERID&origZip=10022&destZip=20008&weightLb=10&
weightOz=5&container=Flat%20Rate%20Box&size=Large&mailService=
Priority%20Mail
```

[Figure 11.5](#ch11lev1sec8.html_ch11fig05) shows the shipping rate generated by the PHP script.

##### Figure 11.5\. The only piece of information returned by the Shipper PHP script is a single shipping charge.

[[View full size image]](ys8wohawzzmxcjm1mjjlzgftdc9zyzc4cjlnmgkvzmmuamdwz3axczvhadbsav9pmxqv.jpg)![](ys8wohawzzmxcjm1mjjlzgftdc9zyzc4cjlnmgkvzmnncdfzmghqnxauawlnms8-.jpg)

Although it would be interesting to experiment with different shipping information in the URL, such as different origin and destination ZIP codes, the USPS test server only supports the test data you see here. You'll have to get validation with the USPS and move over to its production server in order to do more thorough testing. You find out how to obtain validation later in the chapter in the section "Giving Shipper an Extreme Ajax Makeover."

### Testing Shipper

Before attempting to test the Shipper application, make sure to register for your own USPS Web Tools user ID at [http://www.usps.com/webtools/](http://www.usps.com/webtools/). You will then need to plug this user ID into the HTML code in the `shipper.html` Web page as explained in the previous section. Keep in mind that Shipper will not work unless you register for and insert your own user ID into the HTML code for the application.

With that bit of housekeeping out of the way, take a look at [Figure 11.6](#ch11lev1sec9.html_ch11fig06), which shows the Shipper application open and ready to do some serious shipping rate calculating.

##### Figure 11.6\. The Shipper application starts out with the entire user interface blank except for the default Country field, which cannot be changed from USA.

[[View full size image]](ys8wohawzzmxcjm1mjjlzgftdc9zyzc4cjlnmgkvzmmuamdwz3axczzhadbsav9pmxqv.jpg)![](ys8wohawzzmxcjm1mjjlzgftdc9zyzc4cjlnmgkvzmnncdfzmghqnnauawlnms8-.jpg)

The user interface starts out blank, so feel free to enter information as if you're finalizing the checkout of an online purchase. Or save yourself some time and jump straight to the ZIP code field on the page. First try entering a ZIP code that doesn't even exist in the real world (yet), such as 20080\. [Figure 11.7](#ch11lev1sec9.html_ch11fig07) shows how the application displays an error/helper message to indicate that shipping rate information for the ZIP code doesn't exist.

##### Figure 11.7\. Entering an invalid ZIP code results in an error/helper message beside the ZIP code field.

[[View full size image]](ys8wohawzzmxcjm1mjjlzgftdc9zyzc4cjlnmgkvzmmuamdwz3axczdhadbsav9pmxqv.jpg)![](ys8wohawzzmxcjm1mjjlzgftdc9zyzc4cjlnmgkvzmnncdfzmghqn3auawlnms8-.jpg)

In reality, any ZIP code you enter other than 20008 (Washington, DC) is considered invalid for testing purposes, so you can enter just about anything and get the error message. To see a successful shipping rate calculation, enter 20008 as the ZIP code and navigate off the ZIP code field. [Figure 11.8](#ch11lev1sec9.html_ch11fig08) shows the dynamically retrieved shipping charge and the newly calculated order total.

##### Figure 11.8\. Entering a valid ZIP code results in a successful Ajax request of the shipping rate, which also results in the calculation of the order total.

[[View full size image]](ys8wohawzzmxcjm1mjjlzgftdc9zyzc4cjlnmgkvzmmuamdwz3axczhhadbsav9pmxqv.jpg)![](ys8wohawzzmxcjm1mjjlzgftdc9zyzc4cjlnmgkvzmnncdfzmghqohauawlnms8-.jpg)

Unless you were paying careful attention, you might have missed the "loading" image that appeared in place of the shipping charge while the request was taking place. Try again by selecting a different shipping method and watching the shipping rate closely. [Figure 11.9](#ch11lev1sec9.html_ch11fig09) shows the "loading" image in action.

##### Figure 11.9\. The small "loading" image appears in place of the shipping charge while an Ajax request is being carried out.

[[View full size image]](ys8wohawzzmxcjm1mjjlzgftdc9zyzc4cjlnmgkvzmmuamdwz3axczlhadbsav9pmxqv.jpg)![](ys8wohawzzmxcjm1mjjlzgftdc9zyzc4cjlnmgkvzmnncdfzmghqoxauawlnms8-.jpg)

This figure shows how the "loading" image provides a subtle but effective indication that something is taking place in the background related to the shipping rate. When the new rate is obtained, the image disappears and the new rate is displayed (see [Figure 11.10](#ch11lev1sec9.html_ch11fig10)).

##### Figure 11.10\. Changing shipping methods also results in a request, and therefore a new shipping rate and order total.

[[View full size image]](ys8wohawzzmxcjm1mjjlzgftdc9zyzc4cjlnmgkvzmmuamdwz3axczbhadfsav9pmxqv.jpg)![](ys8wohawzzmxcjm1mjjlzgftdc9zyzc4cjlnmgkvzmnncdfzmwhqmhauawlnms8-.jpg)

Although I'd like to tell you to experiment with all kinds of different ZIP codes to see how the application responds to shipping an item all over the country, this isn't an option with the USPS test server. You'll need to take an additional step to enable the application so that it can access the full range of shipping rates. The next section tells you what to do.

### Giving Shipper an Extreme Ajax Makeover

Because the Shipper application bases its shipping options on U.S. Postal Service shipping methods, it is highly dependent upon the U.S. Postal Service Domestic Rates calculator Web service, which is part of the USPS Web Tools ([http://www.usps.com/webtools/](http://www.usps.com/webtools/)). Unlike many of the other third-party Web services used throughout the book, the USPS Web Tools are tightly controlled and can only be used by carefully coordinating your development efforts with the USPS.

More specifically, you must go through a validation process in order to move beyond the limited test data on the USPS test server and try out your application on the USPS production server. This validation is carried out by the USPS Internet Customer Care Center (ICCC), which is available at [icustomercare@usps.com](mailto:icustomercare@usps.com.html) or 1-800-344-7779 (7:00 a.m. to 11:00 p.m. EST). You must already be a registered USPS Web Tools user (with an ID) and have tested the application using the Domestic Rates Web Tool server before contacting the ICCC for access to the production server. The first part of the Shipper extreme makeover, therefore, is simply to gain access to the USPS production server so that you can try out a full range of test ZIP codes.

Note

| ![](ys8wohawzzmxcjm1mjjlzgftdc9zyzc4cjlnmgkvdgmucg9zagpwawdll24-.jpg) | You will have to contact the ICCC in order to use the Shipper application in any real capacity because it is limited to using only USPS-specified test data when running on the USPS test server. |

After you've been validated by the ICCC and obtained the URL of the Domestic Rates Web Tool production server, be sure to change the Shipper application to use the production server by changing the following line of code near the beginning of the `shipper.php` file:

```
$uspsRequest =
  "http://testing.shippingapis.com/ShippingAPITest.dll?
  API=RateV2&";
```

The first part of the URL in this code, [http://testing.shippingapis.com/Shipping-APITest.dll](http://testing.shippingapis.com/Shipping-APITest.dll), is the URL of the USPS test server and is the part of the code you'll need to change to the production URL. This should be the only change required to move the Shipper application from the USPS test server to the production server, and thus open you up to trying out any ZIP codes to calculate shipping charges.

Once you get the Shipper application successfully running on the USPS production server, you'll want to seriously consider incorporating the Ajax code into your own Web pages. Unfortunately, this facet of the extreme makeover is entirely dependent upon your specific site design, so it's hard to give specifics about how to carry out the design and implementation. What I can do, however, is provide a general strategy for incorporating the Ajax shipping calculator into an existing shopping cart.

First off, you'll need to import the two relevant Ajax Toolkit files, like this:

```
<script type="text/javascript" src="ajaxkit.js"> </script>
<script type="text/javascript" src="domkit.js"> </script>
```

You then need to make sure you have an `input` text element for the ZIP code input field, as well as a `span` element for the ZIP code error message. If you already have a ZIP code `input` element, make sure to add an `onchange` event handler similar to the one in the Shipper application. Here's the relevant code from the Shipper Web page:

```
<td class="value"><input type="text" id="zipCode" size="10"
  onchange="getShipRate()" /></td>
<td class="label" width="50%"><span id="zipCodeErr"
  style="font-size:80%; color:#BB0000;"></span></td>
```

Notice that the ZIP code input element calls the `getShipRate()` function to dynamically grab the shipping charge from the USPS server. You saw this function earlier, so I won't repeat it here. However, you'll need to copy and paste its code into the head of your page, along with the response handler function, `handleRequest()`.

You may already have a `select` element for choosing the shipping method, but if you don't you'll need to add that to your page. Following is the selection list used in the Shipper application to offer a choice of USPS shipping methods:

```
<select id="mailService" onchange="getShipRate()">
  <option value="Parcel Post">Parcel Post</option>
  <option value="Priority Mail" selected="selected">
    Priority Mail</option>
  <option value="Express Mail to PO Addressee">
    Express Mail</option>
</select>
```

It is extremely important to notice how the value of each option is set to exactly match the shipping method as specified in the Domestic Rates documentation for the USPS Web Tools. This is important because the Shipper application uses this value as the basis of the rate request, which means it must exactly match a shipping method in order for the lookup to work. In other words, `value=`"`US Priority Mail`" won't work because the shipping method is "`Priority Mail`", not "`US Priority Mail`".

Note

| ![](ys8wohawzzmxcjm1mjjlzgftdc9zyzc4cjlnmgkvdgmucg9zagpwawdll24-.jpg) | It is possible to support other USPS shipping methods beyond the three shown in the Shipper application. To find out about all of the available shipping methods, take a look at the USPS Web Tools documentation at [http://www.usps.com/webtools/technical.htm](http://www.usps.com/webtools/technical.htm). If you'd like to support a different shipping provider such as FedEx or UPS, you'll need to dig into its documentation and do some work to support its request/response formats. |

Finally, there are several "shopping cart" variables in the Shipper application that you'll need to provide or wire to your existing shopping cart fields:

```
var origZip = "10022"; // Origination ZIP code
var itemDesc = "Popcorn Holiday Feast";
var itemPrice = 29.99;
var weightLb = "10";
var weightOz = "5";
var container = "Flat Rate Box";
var size = "Large";
```

Keep in mind that most of these variables will be somehow tied to real data that has either already been entered or selected in your shopping cart. I opted to make them static in the Shipper application just to keep things simple; my goal was showing how to calculate a shipping charge with Ajax, not how to build an entire shopping cart.

If you follow these general design guidelines, you should be able to tie the Ajax dynamic shipping charge lookup into your own Web site without too much trouble.

### Summary

This chapter explored the dynamic lookup of shipping charges, which falls clearly within the realm of a problem needing an Ajax solution. It simply shouldn't be necessary to navigate from one page to the next in a shopping cart just to find out shipping charges for an online purchase. Not only that, but all you really need to provide in order to determine the charges is your ZIP code. The Shipper application in this chapter demonstrated how the clever use of Ajax can make a subtle but substantial improvement to a shopping cart or any other application that needs to calculate a shipping charge on the fly and present it to the user.

## Chapter 12. Adding an Ajax-Powered Rating System to Your Site

Ajax Yet Again in Cartoons

| ![](ys8wohawzzmxcjm1mjjlzgftdc9zyzc4cjlnmgkvdgmucg9zagpwawdll24-.jpg) | MGM's Ajax Corporation isn't the only time Ajax has been used in cartoons. Duckman was a regular show on the USA Network in the mid 1990s, and featured a character named Ajax Duckman, who was the son of the title character, Eric Duckman. Ajax Duckman was voiced by musician Dweezil Zappa, and was somewhat of a slacker teenager on the show. |

An increasingly popular component of many popular Web sites is the ability to rate things. Amazon probably started the trend when it provided the ability to rate products and write your own reviews. Since then, all kinds of different Web sites offer a rating system of some sort, be it for rating movies, music, photographs, blog posts, whatever. If it can be posted online, it can be rated. I suppose deep down most of us are critics at heart, so the idea of being able to rate anything and everything somehow taps into that need to share our criticism and in many cases our praise. Admittedly, it probably isn't fair to simplify everything in the world to a five-star rating, but it can give you a quick glimpse at how people perceive something.

This chapter is without a doubt the most ambitious chapter in the book in terms of how it utilizes Ajax to add five-star ratings to any content on a Web page. By ambitious, I simply mean that the Rater sample application is considerably more advanced in its usage of Ajax when you consider that it has to be able to read and write multiple ratings to a file or database on the server while preventing duplicate votes by the same user. The good news is that the vast majority of this complexity is hidden within the Ajax Toolkit, meaning that your job primarily boils down to properly using the Toolkit, as opposed to constructing your own rating code.

The following files are used by the Rater application in this chapter, and are located on the live Linux CD-ROM with the book in the `chap12` example code folder:

*   `rater.html`—Rater main Web page

*   `cookiereset.html`—Cookie reset Web page for simulating unique users

*   `ajaxkit.js—` Core Ajax functions for the Ajax Toolkit

*   `domkit.js`—DOM-related functions for the Ajax Toolkit

*   `ratingkit.js`—Rating-related functions for the Ajax Toolkit

*   `ratingkit.php`—Server script for saving and loading ratings

*   `ratingkit.css`—Style sheet for five-star ratings

*   `star.gif`—Star images used to represent a five-star rating

*   `star_sm.gif`—Smaller star images used to represent a smaller five-star rating

### The Challenge: Adding Ratings to a Web Site

A handy way of determining the importance of an item is to consider whether it would make the cut if you were to be stranded on a deserted island and could only bring a selected number of items. For example, what if you could only take a single music CD to listen to for the many years you might be stranded with no companionship (but hopefully plenty of batteries to power your CD player!)? What CD would you choose?

Hopefully you never end up having to actually make the deserted island CD selection. Short of that, you might find it useful to rate your favorite CDs on [Amazon.com](http://Amazon.com) so that other people can make more educated music buying decisions. [Amazon.com](http://Amazon.com) allows you to rate music CDs and pretty much everything else it has for sale using a five-star rating system. If you're near a computer right now, take a moment to visit [Amazon.com](http://Amazon.com) and view a music CD. This will clue you in as to how an online rating system works.

If you look at the five stars below the name of the artist and CD title, you'll notice that out beside the stars is a count of how many customers issued ratings. This is very important because it gives you an idea as to how accurate the rating might be—if only a handful of people have rated the item, the rating might not mean so much.

The real reason for mentioning this rating example is to point out how the [Amazon.com](http://Amazon.com) rating approach involves a fairly unimpressive user interface for choosing the rating. I'm referring to the selection list used to select a rating, which allows you to choose a text description of the star rating, such as "five stars" or "four stars." Aside from the fact that [Amazon.com](http://Amazon.com) also supports a text review to go with the rating, you must also preview and accept your rating before it goes into effect. It would be much more intuitive and efficient if you could just click directly on the five-star rating to rate the item right there on the main item page. Granted, this wouldn't address the need for writing a review, but not everyone is interested in writing a review—sometimes a quick "hit-and-run" rating is all you need.

The challenge for this chapter is to use Ajax to incorporate an interactive five-star rating component into a Web page so that users can vote on items without leaving the page. This is in keeping with much of what you've already learned about Ajax in terms of minimizing page reloads when all you're doing is tweaking a small amount of data. In this case, all you want to do is submit a single piece of rating data to the server and then see the total rating tally after the vote is processed. This is definitely within the realm of something Ajax can handle.

One thorny part of this challenge that you may already be curious about is the persistent storage of the rating votes. If you think back on all the sample Ajax applications you've seen thus far, this is the first one that has to remember something the user submits. More specifically, the Rater application must somehow store on the server each vote for each item after it is issued. Fortunately, I provide you with a prebuilt rating component that handles all the details of storing rating data on the server in an XML document. I also provide you with some ideas about possibly using a database instead of an XML file if you're so inclined.

This rating component I'm talking about is included as part of the Ajax Toolkit included on the CD-ROM with this book. The component involves both client- and server-side code, as well as a few images and a style sheet. Behind the scenes, an Ajax-powered five-star rating system is a bit trickier than you might imagine, which is why so many files are necessary in order to work the magic.

Within the client-side JavaScript code for the rating component (contained in `ratingkit.js`), the following two functions are used to control the rating system:

*   `initRatings`()—Initializes the ratings for all of the items on a page

*   `setRating`()—Issues a rating vote for a single item

With a little help from the PHP server script (`ratingkit.php`), these functions make it possible to assign a rating to as many individual items on a page as you want. Not only that, but the rating component is smart enough to limit the number of votes on a given computer to a single vote per user. It does this through the clever use of cookies, which provide a perfect mechanism for remembering whether or not the user has taken a certain action (such as rating a particular item).

Hopefully I've sold you on the fact that I've already done the lion's share of the ratings work by handing you a prebuilt component. What does that leave for the Rater application challenge? The challenge for the Rater application is to properly set up the HTML code for ratable items so that they tie into the rating component and issue their votes correctly. Also, several CSS rating classes are part of the rating component and must be carefully assigned to elements on the Rater page in order for the rating to work.

To keep the HTML code for the Rater application fairly simple and not involve a bunch of extra images or other resources, the items being rated are famous quotations. The Rater Web page consists of five famous quotations, and therefore five different ratings—one per quotation. The Rater Web page needs to include a unique rating component for each quotation and properly identify it so that votes on that quotation are stored on the server for future retrieval. When all is said and done, the Rater application will allow countless people all over the Web to vote on their favorite quotations, with all the rating votes tallied on the server and the total number of votes displayed on the page.

What you'll take with you after developing the Rater application is the ability to reuse the rating component to rate any kind of Web content.

## Chapter 12\. Adding an Ajax-Powered Rating System to Your Site

Ajax Yet Again in Cartoons

| ![](ys8wohawzzmxcjm1mjjlzgftdc9zyzc4cjlnmgkvdgmucg9zagpwawdll24-.jpg) | MGM's Ajax Corporation isn't the only time Ajax has been used in cartoons. Duckman was a regular show on the USA Network in the mid 1990s, and featured a character named Ajax Duckman, who was the son of the title character, Eric Duckman. Ajax Duckman was voiced by musician Dweezil Zappa, and was somewhat of a slacker teenager on the show. |

An increasingly popular component of many popular Web sites is the ability to rate things. Amazon probably started the trend when it provided the ability to rate products and write your own reviews. Since then, all kinds of different Web sites offer a rating system of some sort, be it for rating movies, music, photographs, blog posts, whatever. If it can be posted online, it can be rated. I suppose deep down most of us are critics at heart, so the idea of being able to rate anything and everything somehow taps into that need to share our criticism and in many cases our praise. Admittedly, it probably isn't fair to simplify everything in the world to a five-star rating, but it can give you a quick glimpse at how people perceive something.

This chapter is without a doubt the most ambitious chapter in the book in terms of how it utilizes Ajax to add five-star ratings to any content on a Web page. By ambitious, I simply mean that the Rater sample application is considerably more advanced in its usage of Ajax when you consider that it has to be able to read and write multiple ratings to a file or database on the server while preventing duplicate votes by the same user. The good news is that the vast majority of this complexity is hidden within the Ajax Toolkit, meaning that your job primarily boils down to properly using the Toolkit, as opposed to constructing your own rating code.

The following files are used by the Rater application in this chapter, and are located on the live Linux CD-ROM with the book in the `chap12` example code folder:

*   `rater.html`—Rater main Web page

*   `cookiereset.html`—Cookie reset Web page for simulating unique users

*   `ajaxkit.js—` Core Ajax functions for the Ajax Toolkit

*   `domkit.js`—DOM-related functions for the Ajax Toolkit

*   `ratingkit.js`—Rating-related functions for the Ajax Toolkit

*   `ratingkit.php`—Server script for saving and loading ratings

*   `ratingkit.css`—Style sheet for five-star ratings

*   `star.gif`—Star images used to represent a five-star rating

*   `star_sm.gif`—Smaller star images used to represent a smaller five-star rating

### The Challenge: Adding Ratings to a Web Site

A handy way of determining the importance of an item is to consider whether it would make the cut if you were to be stranded on a deserted island and could only bring a selected number of items. For example, what if you could only take a single music CD to listen to for the many years you might be stranded with no companionship (but hopefully plenty of batteries to power your CD player!)? What CD would you choose?

Hopefully you never end up having to actually make the deserted island CD selection. Short of that, you might find it useful to rate your favorite CDs on [Amazon.com](http://Amazon.com) so that other people can make more educated music buying decisions. [Amazon.com](http://Amazon.com) allows you to rate music CDs and pretty much everything else it has for sale using a five-star rating system. If you're near a computer right now, take a moment to visit [Amazon.com](http://Amazon.com) and view a music CD. This will clue you in as to how an online rating system works.

If you look at the five stars below the name of the artist and CD title, you'll notice that out beside the stars is a count of how many customers issued ratings. This is very important because it gives you an idea as to how accurate the rating might be—if only a handful of people have rated the item, the rating might not mean so much.

The real reason for mentioning this rating example is to point out how the [Amazon.com](http://Amazon.com) rating approach involves a fairly unimpressive user interface for choosing the rating. I'm referring to the selection list used to select a rating, which allows you to choose a text description of the star rating, such as "five stars" or "four stars." Aside from the fact that [Amazon.com](http://Amazon.com) also supports a text review to go with the rating, you must also preview and accept your rating before it goes into effect. It would be much more intuitive and efficient if you could just click directly on the five-star rating to rate the item right there on the main item page. Granted, this wouldn't address the need for writing a review, but not everyone is interested in writing a review—sometimes a quick "hit-and-run" rating is all you need.

The challenge for this chapter is to use Ajax to incorporate an interactive five-star rating component into a Web page so that users can vote on items without leaving the page. This is in keeping with much of what you've already learned about Ajax in terms of minimizing page reloads when all you're doing is tweaking a small amount of data. In this case, all you want to do is submit a single piece of rating data to the server and then see the total rating tally after the vote is processed. This is definitely within the realm of something Ajax can handle.

One thorny part of this challenge that you may already be curious about is the persistent storage of the rating votes. If you think back on all the sample Ajax applications you've seen thus far, this is the first one that has to remember something the user submits. More specifically, the Rater application must somehow store on the server each vote for each item after it is issued. Fortunately, I provide you with a prebuilt rating component that handles all the details of storing rating data on the server in an XML document. I also provide you with some ideas about possibly using a database instead of an XML file if you're so inclined.

This rating component I'm talking about is included as part of the Ajax Toolkit included on the CD-ROM with this book. The component involves both client- and server-side code, as well as a few images and a style sheet. Behind the scenes, an Ajax-powered five-star rating system is a bit trickier than you might imagine, which is why so many files are necessary in order to work the magic.

Within the client-side JavaScript code for the rating component (contained in `ratingkit.js`), the following two functions are used to control the rating system:

*   `initRatings`()—Initializes the ratings for all of the items on a page

*   `setRating`()—Issues a rating vote for a single item

With a little help from the PHP server script (`ratingkit.php`), these functions make it possible to assign a rating to as many individual items on a page as you want. Not only that, but the rating component is smart enough to limit the number of votes on a given computer to a single vote per user. It does this through the clever use of cookies, which provide a perfect mechanism for remembering whether or not the user has taken a certain action (such as rating a particular item).

Hopefully I've sold you on the fact that I've already done the lion's share of the ratings work by handing you a prebuilt component. What does that leave for the Rater application challenge? The challenge for the Rater application is to properly set up the HTML code for ratable items so that they tie into the rating component and issue their votes correctly. Also, several CSS rating classes are part of the rating component and must be carefully assigned to elements on the Rater page in order for the rating to work.

To keep the HTML code for the Rater application fairly simple and not involve a bunch of extra images or other resources, the items being rated are famous quotations. The Rater Web page consists of five famous quotations, and therefore five different ratings—one per quotation. The Rater Web page needs to include a unique rating component for each quotation and properly identify it so that votes on that quotation are stored on the server for future retrieval. When all is said and done, the Rater application will allow countless people all over the Web to vote on their favorite quotations, with all the rating votes tallied on the server and the total number of votes displayed on the page.

What you'll take with you after developing the Rater application is the ability to reuse the rating component to rate any kind of Web content.

### The Design: Designing a Rater Application

The design of the Rater application begins with the quotations themselves because they are driving the rating process in this particular example. Because the quotations are just text, it is sufficient to wrap them up in `div` elements without any special formatting or other code. Although a rating appears immediately below a quotation, there is nothing directly tying a rating and a quotation together in terms of HTML code.

The connection between a rating and a quotation has everything to do with the "item ID" of the rating, which is a piece of text used to uniquely identify a rating among other ratings. If you think of all the ratings on your Web site as one big collection, which they are, then each of them has a unique ID that allows you to tell them apart. The item ID should somehow give you a clue as to what quotation the rating is connected to. For example, you could use the last name of the quotation author as the ID for a rating, which is in fact what the Rater application uses.

The item ID is critical because it gets stored along with the rating votes, and is therefore what is used to associate a stored rating with a quotation on the page. Speaking of quotations on the page, [Figure 12.1](#ch12lev1sec2.html_ch12fig01) shows the basic layout of the Rater user interface, including the quotations and their respective ratings.

##### Figure 12.1\. The user interface for the Rater application consists of several text quotations with a rating component for each.

![](ys8wohawzzmxcjm1mjjlzgftdc9zyzc4cjlnmgkvzmnncdjzmghnm2kuawlmms8-.jpg)

The figure reveals how each quotation is immediately followed by a rating and its associated helper text. The helper text initially just instructs you to click the rating in order to submit a vote. Once you've voted on a rating, the helper text changes to show you how many votes have been cast for the rating. So the helper text serves double duty—first as an instruction and then as a means of displaying rating information.

The rating component provided in the Ajax Toolkit handles all the details of the Ajax requests in the Rater application. The goal in this chapter is not to dissect the inner workings of the rater component but instead to show you how to use it to incorporate ratings into Web pages. Even so, it's worth taking a quick peek at how Ajax is at work in the component in the form of client requests and server responses.

#### The Client Request

Two different client requests are involved with the rating component: one for initially obtaining rating information for all of the ratings on a page, and another for casting a vote for a particular rating. The first request occurs exactly one time when a page is initially loaded, whereas the second request can occur multiple times if you have multiple ratings on a page. Of course, the rating component does use cookies to prevent you from casting more than one vote on a given rating. But the fact remains that each vote on each rating results in an Ajax request.

When an Ajax request is made for all of the ratings, the client isn't required to pass along any information. In other words, the URL for the Ajax request consists of nothing more than the PHP server script that reads the ratings from an XML file and returns the results.

Unlike the rating retrieval request, the Ajax request for casting a rating vote does require parameters. The parameters required of this request are the item ID and the rating itself (one to five stars). These two parameters are passed along to the server where they are properly integrated into the rating data, meaning that they are used to increment the number of votes and recalculate the average rating.

#### The Server Response

The server responses for the rating component in the Ajax Toolkit vary considerably depending on the request. When retrieving all of the ratings for the purposes of initializing the ratings on a page, the server returns all of the ratings that have been stored as an XML document. It is then up to the code on the client to determine which ratings apply to the page, based upon the rating IDs.

The server response for the setting of a rating involves the newly calculated rating average and the total number of votes that have been cast for the specified item ID. These two pieces of information are returned to the client script, which then updates the rating on the page appropriately.

#### Making Sense of the Client-to-Server Conversation

To help get a feel for the flow of data that takes place between the client and server in the Rater application (rater component), take a look at [Figure 12.2](#ch12lev1sec2.html_ch12fig02).

##### Figure 12.2\. Behind the scenes in the Rater application are two Ajax requests for initializing the ratings and submitting new rating votes.

![](ys8wohawzzmxcjm1mjjlzgftdc9zyzc4cjlnmgkvzmnncdjzmghnngkuawlmms8-.jpg)

The figure reveals how the initial rating request involves no flow of data from the client but returns a full-blown XML document containing all of the ratings. Because no parameters are required when the Ajax request is submitted, the URL for the request is simply the name of the PHP server script:

```
ratingkit.php
```

Although the data flowing from the client to the server is quite minimal, the data returned in the other direction is much more interesting. Following is an example of what the response XML document looks like:

Code View:

```
<?xml version="1.0" encoding="utf-8"?>
<ratings>
  <rating>
    <id>twain</id>
    <avg>4.5</avg>
    <votes>2</votes>
  </rating>
  <rating>
    <id>ali</id>
    <avg>3.33333333333</avg>
    <votes>3</votes>
  </rating>
  <rating>
    <id>einstein</id>
    <avg>3</avg>
    <votes>2</votes>
  </rating>
  <rating>
    <id>shaw</id>
    <avg>4</avg>
    <votes>2</votes>
  </rating>
  <rating>
    <id>paine</id>
    <avg>4</avg>
    <votes>1</votes>
  </rating>
 </ratings> 
```

The structure of this XML document is pretty simple in that it consists of a list of ratings, each of which has three distinct pieces of data:

*   Item ID (`<id>`)

*   Rating average (`<avg>`)

*   Number of votes (`<votes>`)

This information is sufficient to describe a rating for a single item. Each `<rating>` tag in the XML document corresponds to a rating for a single item. The document as a whole serves as the data format for the ratings, meaning that if you open the `ratings.xml` file generated on the server, you'll see the same XML code. It is important to note that this file serves as the main database for all ratings on your site, potentially across multiple pages. The key is the item ID, which allows you to use ratings anywhere you want on your site as long as they are properly ID'd.

Note

| ![](ys8wohawzzmxcjm1mjjlzgftdc9zyzc4cjlnmgkvdgmucg9zagpwawdll24-.jpg) | As you find out later in the chapter in the "Giving Rater an Extreme Ajax Makeover" section, item IDs can be any text you want. Although the Rater application uses names as IDs, you can just as easily use numbers or a combination of letters and numbers. The important thing is to use item IDs that are unique and easy to associate with an item. For example, an item ID for a rating of a book might use the book's International Standard Book Number (ISBN) so that the ID is guaranteed unique and also has an obvious association with the book. |

If you ever want to view the XML data stored for the ratings on your site, just enter the rating component's PHP server script (`ratingkit.php`) in your browser with no parameters or anything. [Figure 12.3](#ch12lev1sec2.html_ch12fig03) shows the rating XML code as it appears in a browser by directly opening the server script.

##### Figure 12.3\. Directly entering the URL of the PHP server script for the rating component results in the display of the rating XML document.

[[View full size image]](ys8wohawzzmxcjm1mjjlzgftdc9zyzc4cjlnmgkvzmmuamdwz3ayczvhadbsav9pmxqv.jpg)![](ys8wohawzzmxcjm1mjjlzgftdc9zyzc4cjlnmgkvzmnncdjzmghqnxauawlnms8-.jpg)

The other Ajax request used in the Rater application involves storing a new rating. The underlying code in the server script used to process this request is a bit messier because it has to be smart enough to create a new XML file (`ratings.xml`) if it doesn't already exist (i.e., this is the very first vote on any item). Assuming the XML file does in fact exist, the server script has to search through the XML code and find the rating based upon the item ID. It then has to update the rating by calculating a new average and incrementing the number of votes.

The URL used to issue a new rating involves passing along the item ID and rating as parameters to the server script. However, unlike other Ajax requests you've seen throughout the book, this request is issued as a `POST` instead of a `GET` because the intent of the request is to alter information on the server. In a `POST` request, the URL is simply the name of the server script, while the parameters (item ID and rating) are passed as a separate parameter to the `ajaxSendRequest()` function in the Ajax Toolkit in the file `ajaxkit.js`. I realize I'm drifting into some details that you may not be all that interested in knowing, given that the focus here is code reuse, but I want you to understand enough about the inner workings of the rater component to potentially be able to modify it if need be.

Getting back to the rating submission request, the response from the server includes a newly calculated rating average and the total number of votes that have been cast. Although XML could certainly be used to format this return data, it just isn't necessary. So the data is returned as a colon-delimited pair, like this:

```
4.5:2
```

This is the data returned after issuing the last vote made on the first quote in the XML data you just saw. If you look back at that data, you'll see the following code:

```
<rating>
  <id>twain</id>
  <avg>4.5</avg>
  <votes>2</votes>
</rating>
```

So, the server response to a rating vote that has been cast is just a matter of returning a couple pieces of data that have just been written to the XML file: the `<avg>` and `<votes>` content.

### Putting Together the Rater Application

In addition to several files that are part of the Ajax Toolkit included on the CD-ROM accompanying the book, the Rater application consists of a single Web page and a helper Web page that isn't strictly required:

*   HTML Web page (`rater.html`)

*   Cookie Reset Web page (`cookiereset.html`)

The Rater application is contained within the Rater Web page (`rater.html`), whereas the Cookie Reset Web page (`cookiereset.html`) is a separate helper page for clearing the rating cookies for testing purposes. The next couple sections examine these files in more detail.

#### The Rater Web Page

The Rater Web page makes use of five individual ratings that are each applied to a text quotation that appears on the page just before each rating. The idea is that you are able to read a quotation and then immediately rate it. [Figure 12.4](#ch12lev1sec3.html_ch12fig04) shows the Rater Web page with several rating votes already made but a few items remaining in an unrated state.

##### Figure 12.4\. The Rater Web page displays several quotations along with a five-star rating component for each one.

[[View full size image]](ys8wohawzzmxcjm1mjjlzgftdc9zyzc4cjlnmgkvzmmuamdwz3ayczzhadbsav9pmxqv.jpg)![](ys8wohawzzmxcjm1mjjlzgftdc9zyzc4cjlnmgkvzmnncdjzmghqnnauawlnms8-.jpg)

If you pay close attention to the figure, you'll see that the rating for the Muhammad Ali quotation appears with larger stars and text. This demonstrates how the rating component supports two different sizes. In practical terms, the difference between the small and large versions of the rating component simply involves the usage of the different CSS style classes for the ratings.

The actual code for the Rater Web page is admittedly on the long side, so brace yourself. Just keep in mind that the main reason for the long code listing is because the page is using five different ratings, each of which requires several lines of code. Here's abbreviated code for the body of the Rater page, which is where all of the interesting stuff takes place:

Code View:

```
<body onload="initRatings()">
  <div id="ajaxState" style="display:none"></div>
  <br />
  <div>A banker is a fellow who lends you his umbrella when the
    sun is shining, but wants it back the minute it begins to
    rain.<br />
    - Mark Twain</div>
  <div>
    <span class="rating-label-small">Rating:</span>
    <span class="inline-rating">
      <ul class="star-rating small-star">
        <li id="twain-rating" class="current-rating"
style="width:0%">Rating</li>
        <li><a href="javascript:setRating('twain', 1);"
          class="one-star">1</a></li>
        <li><a href="javascript:setRating('twain', 2);"
          class="two-stars">2</a></li>
        <li><a href="javascript:setRating('twain', 3);"
          class="three-stars">3</a></li>
        <li><a href="javascript:setRating('twain', 4);"
          class="four-stars">4</a></li>
        <li><a href="javascript:setRating('twain', 5);"
          class="five-stars">5</a></li>
     </ul>
   </span>
   <span id="twain-info" class="rating-info-small">
     (click to vote)</span>
  </div>
  <hr />
  <div>Learn from yesterday, live for today, hope for tomorrow.
   The important thing is not to stop questioning.<br />
   - Albert Einstein</div>
  <div>
    <span class="rating-label-small">Rating:</span>
    <span class="inline-rating">
      <ul class="star-rating small-star">
        <li id="einstein-rating" class="current-rating"
          style="width:0%">Rating</li>
        <li><a href="javascript:setRating('einstein', 1);"
          class="one-star">1</a></li>
        <li><a href="javascript:setRating('einstein', 2);"
          class="two-stars">2</a></li>
        <li><a href="javascript:setRating('einstein', 3);"
          class="three-stars">3</a></li>
        <li><a href="javascript:setRating('einstein', 4);"
          class="four-stars">4</a></li>
        <li><a href="javascript:setRating('einstein', 5);"
          class="five-stars">5</a></li>
      </ul>
    </span>
    <span id="einstein-info" class="rating-info-small">
      (click to vote)</span>
  </div>
  ...
</body> 
```

The key to this code is the careful usage of CSS style classes for the ratings. These classes are defined in the `ratingkit.css` style sheet and are responsible for the animated hover effect when you drag the mouse over the rating stars. This CSS approach to implementing a five-star rating was originally created by Rogie King of Komodo Media, which is located on the Web at [http://www.komodomedia.com](http://www.komodomedia.com)/. The code works so well that I used it as the basis for the rating component in the Ajax Toolkit.

Each rating in the Rater Web page is created as an unordered list where each star in the rating is an item in the list. The twist is that the list items are displayed horizontally instead of vertically, which is what causes the stars to appear horizontally. This may seem like a strange way to use a list, and it is, but it ends up working quite well for ratings. The trick is then to apply the appropriate styles to the list so that the hover effect works as intended.

The first step is to create a `span` element that houses the label for the rating, which is just static text:

```
<span class="rating-label-small">Rating:</span>
```

Of course, even the static text must have the appropriate CSS style class applied to it (`rating-label-small`). If you want a larger label for the larger-sized rating component, use `rating-label` instead of `rating-label-small`.

The next step is to create another `span` element that serves as the outer shell for the rating component:

```
<span class="inline-rating">
```

Within this `span` element you then add the unordered list container element, `ul`, like this:

```
<ul class="star-rating small-star">
```

This `ul` element is styled for a small rating component, meaning that it will use the smaller star images found in the `star_sm.gif` image file. If you want to use the larger rating component instead, just leave off the `small-star` class so that the code looks like this:

```
<ul class="star-rating">
```

Up to now the rating component is entirely generic with no code that is specific to an item. That's about to change with the addition of the first list item, which is a special item that controls how many of the stars are visible:

```
 <li id="twain-rating" class="current-rating"
style="width:0%">Rating</li>
```

Now you see the first evidence of an item-specific rating, as the item ID `twain` is used to identify the rating via its `id` attribute (`twain-rating`).

Next up are the stars themselves, which are all coded as `li` list elements:

```
 <li><a href="javascript:setRating('twain', 1);"
     class="one-star">1</a></li>
   <li><a href="javascript:setRating('twain', 2);"
     class="two-stars">2</a></li>
   <li><a href="javascript:setRating('twain', 3);"
     class="three-stars">3</a></li>
   <li><a href="javascript:setRating('twain', 4);"
     class="four-stars">4</a></li>
   <li><a href="javascript:setRating('twain', 5);"
     class="five-stars">5</a></li>
```

Each line of code here represents one of the five stars in a five-star rating. Not only does each star serve as a visual indication of a rating, but it also acts as a trigger for issuing a rating vote. This is evident in the fact that the `setRating()` function is called in response to each link within the list items. The `setRating()` function accepts as its first parameter the item ID for the rating, which in this case is `twain`, followed by the rating to be set. So the second parameter to the `setRating()` function is the number of stars to be set for the rating.

You're now ready to close up the main part of the rating component, like this:

```
 </ul>
</span>
```

The only remaining code is one last `span` element for the helper text that appears to the right of the rating:

```
<span id="twain-info" class="rating-info-small">
  (click to vote)</span>
```

Notice that this code uses the `rating-info-small` CSS style class, which corresponds to the small rating component. If you're creating a large rating component, change this style to `rating-info`.

This code also uses the item ID (`twain`) in the formation of the `id` attribute for the element (`twain-info`). Keep in mind that the purpose of this element is to instruct the user on how to cast a vote (prior to voting) and to display the total number of votes (after voting). A third role that the helper text plays is to display a status message while a vote is being stored on the server. This last role is very similar to the "loading" images you've seen in other examples, except in this case "loading" text is used instead.

That completes the breakdown of an individual rating component within the Rater Web page. If you look back at the rating code used throughout the page, you'll see five distinct chunks of code that exactly follow the structure you just examined.

The only loose end remaining in the Rater application is the call to the `initRatings()` function, which must be made in the `onload` event handler for the page's body. This is the function that initially sets the values for all of the ratings on the page. Without this function, the benefit of writing the ratings to a file on the server would be lost.

#### The Cookie Reset Web Page

The Cookie Reset Web page (`cookiereset.html`) is a helper tool for the Rater application that is used to reset the cookies and allow you to vote again on any of the quotations on the Rater page. This is helpful for testing out the application and experimenting with different ratings without having to get access to a bunch of unique computers. Of course, you would never publish the Cookie Reset Web page so that it is accessible to other users; it is purely an internal tool to use for your own testing.

[Figure 12.5](#ch12lev1sec3.html_ch12fig05) shows the Cookie Reset Web page in action, which admittedly isn't very exciting at all.

##### Figure 12.5\. The Cookie Reset Web page displays a quick message to let you know that the Rater cookies have been reset.

[[View full size image]](ys8wohawzzmxcjm1mjjlzgftdc9zyzc4cjlnmgkvzmmuamdwz3ayczdhadbsav9pmxqv.jpg)![](ys8wohawzzmxcjm1mjjlzgftdc9zyzc4cjlnmgkvzmnncdjzmghqn3auawlnms8-.jpg)

The lack of excitement in the Cookie Reset Web page stems from the fact that it doesn't do anything beyond clearing the value of the five cookies associated with the five quotation ratings. Following is the complete HTML code for the page, `cookiereset.html`, which should illuminate the cookie-clearing process:

```
<html>
  <head>
    <title>Rater - Cookie Reset</title>
    <script type="text/javascript">
      document.cookie = "twain-rating=";
      document.cookie = "einstein-rating=";
      document.cookie = "ali-rating=";
      document.cookie = "paine-rating=";
      document.cookie = "shaw-rating=";
    </script>
  </head>

 <body>
   <p>The cookies are reset.</p>
 </body>
</html>
```

As you can see, the five cookies are cleared in the head of the page. Clearing a cookie simply involves setting the cookie to nothing. It's extremely important to get the name of each cookie correct, and in this case I stuck with the naming convention established in the Rater application where the quotation ID is placed in front of the text–`rating` to arrive at the cookie name.

Keep in mind that the Cookie Reset Web page is really just a time-saving tool. Your Web browser likely includes an option for clearing all of the cookies it has amassed. However, some browsers only offer an option to clear all cookies. Even when there is an option to clear individual cookies, it can be extremely tedious hunting down the rating cookies when you consider that you likely have hundreds of cookies stored in your browser at any given time.

Note

| ![](ys8wohawzzmxcjm1mjjlzgftdc9zyzc4cjlnmgkvdgmucg9zagpwawdll24-.jpg) | You will likely end up using different item IDs when reusing the rating component in your own Web pages, in which case you'll want to modify the Cookie Reset page so that it clears your new cookies. |

### Testing Rater

The Rater application makes for a fairly interesting application to test because it involves data that is stored away on the server, meaning that the application maintains a certain state. This is another way of saying that it remembers the votes that have been made. Also keep in mind that the application is designed to prevent more than one vote on a particular item from a given computer. So to test the application thoroughly on one computer, you'll need to use the `cookiereset.html` Web page to reset the rating cookies created.

[Figure 12.6](#ch12lev1sec4.html_ch12fig06) shows the Rater application with a clean slate (i.e., no votes have been cast for any of the ratings).

##### Figure 12.6\. When you first launch the Rater application, all of the quotations are ready to be rated.

[[View full size image]](ys8wohawzzmxcjm1mjjlzgftdc9zyzc4cjlnmgkvzmmuamdwz3ayczhhadbsav9pmxqv.jpg)![](ys8wohawzzmxcjm1mjjlzgftdc9zyzc4cjlnmgkvzmnncdjzmghqohauawlnms8-.jpg)

Because no rating votes have been cast at this point, no cookies have been set, and therefore all of the ratings are available for voting. Hovering the mouse over one of the ratings allows you to see the potential rating by highlighting the light stars with darker stars (see [Figure 12.7](#ch12lev1sec4.html_ch12fig07)).

##### Figure 12.7\. Hovering over a rating with the mouse allows you to preview the rating you're about to make.

[[View full size image]](ys8wohawzzmxcjm1mjjlzgftdc9zyzc4cjlnmgkvzmmuamdwz3ayczlhadbsav9pmxqv.jpg)![](ys8wohawzzmxcjm1mjjlzgftdc9zyzc4cjlnmgkvzmnncdjzmghqoxauawlnms8-.jpg)

To finalize a rating for a particular item, just click the number of stars for the rating. This will result in the rating vote being cast, in which case the helper text beside the stars changes to indicate that the vote is being cast. [Figure 12.8](#ch12lev1sec4.html_ch12fig08) shows this effect on the first rating component on the page, which corresponds to a quotation by Mark Twain.

##### Figure 12.8\. Upon a rating vote being issued, the helper text next to the five-star rating changes to indicate that the vote is being cast.

[[View full size image]](ys8wohawzzmxcjm1mjjlzgftdc9zyzc4cjlnmgkvzmmuamdwz3ayczbhadfsav9pmxqv.jpg)![](ys8wohawzzmxcjm1mjjlzgftdc9zyzc4cjlnmgkvzmnncdjzmwhqmhauawlnms8-.jpg)

Once the vote is cast, the average rating for the item appears in the form of gold stars. This is significant because you aren't allowed to see the average rating until after casting your own vote. The total number of votes received for the item is also displayed in the helper text next to the stars. [Figure 12.9](#ch12lev1sec4.html_ch12fig09) shows the Mark Twain quotation after receiving its first vote of four stars.

##### Figure 12.9\. Upon a rating vote being successfully submitted, the rating component changes to reveal the average rating and the total number of votes.

[[View full size image]](ys8wohawzzmxcjm1mjjlzgftdc9zyzc4cjlnmgkvzmmuamdwz3ayczfhadfsav9pmxqv.jpg)![](ys8wohawzzmxcjm1mjjlzgftdc9zyzc4cjlnmgkvzmnncdjzmwhqmxauawlnms8-.jpg)

In addition to demonstrating the smaller five-star rating component, the Rater application also shows how to use a larger version with larger star images and related text. The Muhammad Ali quotation uses the larger version of the component—[Figure 12.10](#ch12lev1sec4.html_ch12fig10) shows a vote being cast using this component.

##### Figure 12.10\. The rating component also supports a larger version that uses both a larger star image and larger text.

[[View full size image]](ys8wohawzzmxcjm1mjjlzgftdc9zyzc4cjlnmgkvzmmuamdwz3ayczjhadfsav9pmxqv.jpg)![](ys8wohawzzmxcjm1mjjlzgftdc9zyzc4cjlnmgkvzmnncdjzmwhqmnauawlnms8-.jpg)

The real power of the Rater application and the rating component is many different people being able to rate items.
