Although the component is designed to prevent duplicate voting on the same machine through the use of a cookie, you can circumvent this by loading a special Web page that clears the rating cookies. If you open the `cookiereset.html` Web page in your browser, it will clear out all of the cookies for the Rater Web page, thereby allowing you to view the page as someone else would see it on his or her own computer.

Note

| ![](ys8wohawzzmxcjm1mjjlzgftdc9zyzc4cjlnmgkvdgmucg9zagpwawdll24-.jpg) | Make sure to refresh the Cookie Reset Web page if you end up using it more than once, and also refresh the Rater Web page after resetting the cookies. Otherwise, the cookie changes won't be noticeable. |

[Figure 12.11](#ch12lev1sec4.html_ch12fig11) shows the Rater application with several votes made on the quotations. In fact, the figure shows all of the rating averages, which means that a vote has been cast on every quotation.

##### Figure 12.11\. By resetting the cookies, you can vote again on items and bypass the limitation of one vote per computer.

[[View full size image]](ys8wohawzzmxcjm1mjjlzgftdc9zyzc4cjlnmgkvzmmuamdwz3aycznhadfsav9pmxqv.jpg)![](ys8wohawzzmxcjm1mjjlzgftdc9zyzc4cjlnmgkvzmnncdjzmwhqm3auawlnms8-.jpg)

The cool thing about the rating component is that it stores the ratings in a manner such that the XML file on the server doesn't grow when additional votes are cast. So as many people as you want can vote on items without you having to worry about a rating data file growing out of control.

### Giving Rater an Extreme Ajax Makeover

There are a variety of different ways to expand on the Rater application, but the most likely thing you'll want to do first is apply the rating component to your own Web pages. I'm assuming you have some items in mind that you'd like to add the capability of rating on a five-star scale. These items can be images, music clips, videos, text, or anything else you dream up. The main thing is that you need to be able to add the rating component HTML code somewhere near the item on the page so that it can be easily accessed.

The first step in adding the rating component to a page of your own is to import the appropriate Ajax Toolkit files. The following code needs to be added to the head of the page that will contain the ratings:

```
<link rel="stylesheet" type="text/css" href="ratingkit.css" />
<script type="text/javascript" src="ajaxkit.js"> </script>
<script type="text/javascript" src="domkit.js"> </script>
<script type="text/javascript" src="ratingkit.js"> </script>
```

This code imports the rating CSS style sheet along with several JavaScript files that are all required in order for the rating component to work. The next thing to do is to call the `initRatings()` function from the `onload` event handler in the `<body>` tag, like this:

```
<body onload="initRatings()">
```

This makes sure that all of the ratings on the page get initialized properly.

From here on, the code largely depends on how you want to structure the ratings. Let's say you have lots of products in an online store that you'd like to rate. In this case, you'll want to use an item ID for the ratings that somehow ties into an existing product ID. This is likely a number of some sort, along the lines of a retail UPC code or Amazon.com's ASIN numbers, which the company uses to uniquely identify products. Let's assume for simplicity that your products are simple integers that start at 1 and count up. So your products are numbered 1, 2, 3, 4, and so on.

You now have an item ID to uniquely identify the rating for each item, which is all you need to add the ratings to your page. Here's the code for a small rating for the first product (item ID of 1):

```
<div>
  <span class="rating-label-small">Rating:</span>
  <span class="inline-rating">
    <ul class="star-rating small-star">
      <li id="1-rating" class="current-rating"
        style="width:0%">Rating</li>
      <li><a href="javascript:setRating('1', 1);"
        class="one-star">1</a></li>
      <li><a href="javascript:setRating('1', 2);"
        class="two-stars">2</a></li>
      <li><a href="javascript:setRating('1', 3);"
        class="three-stars">3</a></li>
      <li><a href="javascript:setRating('1', 4);"
        class="four-stars">4</a></li>
      <li><a href="javascript:setRating('1', 5);"
        class="five-stars">5</a></li>
    </ul>
  </span>
  <span id="1-info" class="rating-info-small">
    (click to vote)</span>
</div>
```

Do you see the pattern in the code? The item ID appears in exactly seven places, and all of them are important. The first place the item ID appears is in the `id` attribute of the `<li>` list item tag, which is set to `1-rating`. The next five appearances are in the calls to the `setRating()` function for each of the possible votes (one star through five stars). In this case, the value '`1`' is passed into the function as its first parameter; this is the item ID. Finally, the item ID is referenced once more as `1-info` in the `id` attribute of the `<span>` element near the end of the code. This is what ties the helper text for the rating component to the particular item.

All of these item ID references must be set properly for each individual rating in a page. Just to make sure you understand how this works, let's fast-forward through the product catalog a bit until we arrive at the product with an item ID of 379. Here's the code to add a rating component for this item:

```
<div>
  <span class="rating-label-small">Rating:</span>
  <span class="inline-rating">
    <ul class="star-rating small-star">
      <li id="379-rating" class="current-rating"
        style="width:0%">Rating</li>
      <li><a href="javascript:setRating('379', 1);"
        class="one-star">1</a></li>
      <li><a href="javascript:setRating('379', 2);"
        class="two-stars">2</a></li>
      <li><a href="javascript:setRating('379', 3);"
        class="three-stars">3</a></li>
      <li><a href="javascript:setRating('379', 4);"
        class="four-stars">4</a></li>
      <li><a href="javascript:setRating('379', 5);"
        class="five-stars">5</a></li>
    </ul>
  </span>
  <span id="379-info" class="rating-info-small">
    (click to vote)</span>
</div>
```

Hopefully this code hammers home the placement of the item ID in the code for a rating. You can paste this chunk of code as many times as you want to create different ratings, as long as you are careful to change the item ID each time.

Note

| ![](ys8wohawzzmxcjm1mjjlzgftdc9zyzc4cjlnmgkvdgmucg9zagpwawdll24-.jpg) | One other quick rating makeover that is extremely easy to apply is changing out the star images with images of your own. The large and small star images are stored in the files `star.gif` and `star_sm.gif`, respectively. Each of these files contains three different stars that are required for the hover effect. The stars are arranged vertically in the GIF image, and are all the same size (25x25 for large and 10x10 for small). If you use images that are the same size and arrange them vertically in the same manner, you can easily change the entire look of the rating component—just copy your own star image file over `star.gif` and/or `star_sm.gif`. |

Reusing the rating component in your own pages isn't the only extreme makeover worth considering. Although the rater component works great as is, you may not be fully satisfied with the usage of an XML file for storing the rating data on the server. I opted for this approach primarily because I didn't want to make the application any more complex than necessary, and adding a database into the mix is a fair amount of additional complexity. But it's not a bad idea.

If you have some experience with MySQL or some other database, you might consider reworking the `ratingkit.php` server script so that it reads and writes rating data using a database instead of an XML file. Rather than get into the actual coding details of an implementation in MySQL, I'll just paint you a general picture of how this can be done.

In short, the following steps are required to change the rating component in the Ajax Toolkit so that it uses a MySQL database for rating storage and retrieval instead of an XML file:

1.  Create a database table named `rating` with the following fields: `id, avg`, and `votes`. These fields map one-to-one with the tags of the same name in the `ratings.xml` file.

2.  Change `ratingkit.php` so that it establishes a database connection instead of opening an XML file.

3.  Change `ratingkit.php` so that it reads all of the rows from the `rating` table via a database query instead of reading in the `ratings.xml` file.

4.  Change `ratingkit.php` so that all of the code that iterates through the ratings in the XML data structure instead iterates through the rows returned from the database query.

5.  Change `ratingkit.php` so that it modifies the appropriate row in the `rating` table to store a rating instead of writing out the XML file.

I realize this list of steps is incredibly generalized and requires a fair amount of MySQL experience to carry out. But in all honesty, that's kind of the point—I'd rather see you stick with the XML file solution if you don't have the MySQL skills to pull this off. Trust me, this is a very basic database challenge to anyone with database skills. If you're short on database skills, as many of us are, just roll with the XML version knowing that it should meet your needs just fine.

### Summary

Although you may not always like the feedback you get, it's hard to argue the benefits of allowing users the freedom to rate content on your site. Ratings provide one of the key features of any interactive online community, and help people feel more involved and more informed. The Ajax rating component you learned how to use in this chapter is flexible enough to support any number of individually rated items, as well as sports a highly intuitive user interface for submitting a rating in the range of one to five stars. And you don't even have to be a database guru to start using ratings thanks to the fact that a simple XML file is used for storing ratings on the server. If you do happen to be a database guru, however, you may want to consider applying the extreme Ajax makeover ideas to incorporate the rating storage into your own database.

## Appendix A. The Past, Present, and Future of Ajax

Maybe you couldn't care less where Ajax came from, but just in case you do, I thought a little history lesson might be worthwhile. By understanding a bit more about the origins of Ajax, you'll hopefully be more capable of assessing the role it will play in the future of Web development. It's a very exciting technology, but one that is in many ways still in an exploratory state. So where exactly did Ajax come from and where is it headed? Let's find out.

### The Road to Ajax

When you get right down to it, Ajax is really just a term used to describe a particular application of existing technologies. This particular application has been around for a few years, but not until February of 2005 was given a catchy name. The man responsible for the name is Jesse James Garrett, who needed a more efficient way to describe a Web solution to a client of his employer, Adaptive Path. The name Ajax hit Garrett one day in the shower, and it stuck. People began thinking that Ajax was actually an acronym (AJAX) that stood for Asynchronous JavaScript and XML, but this isn't entirely the case. Although the name Ajax does in fact refer to a development approach involving asynchronous JavaScript and XML, the word "Ajax" isn't strictly an acronym.

Surprisingly enough, the road leading up to Garrett's catchy name can be traced back to Microsoft's Remote Scripting technology, which was officially introduced in 1998 in Internet Explorer 4 and Netscape Navigator 4\. A functional application that utilized Microsoft's Remote Scripting technology was Outlook Web Access, which was certainly pioneering for its time. Taking a step back a few years earlier, the `iframe` (Internet Explorer) and `layer` (Netscape Navigator) elements supported the asynchronous loading of Web content as far back as 1996\. The `iframe` and `layer` elements in combination with crafty JavaScript coding ushered in the notion of Dynamic HTML, or DHTML, which was popular for several years prior to the advent of Ajax techniques.

The true birth of Ajax took place in 2000 when Microsoft added the `XMLHTTPRequest` object to Internet Explorer 5\. It still took a few years for the technology to mature to a point where it could be used consistently, and even then it took a groundbreaking application for everyone to truly see the power of Ajax.

## Appendix A. The Past, Present, and Future of Ajax

Maybe you couldn't care less where Ajax came from, but just in case you do, I thought a little history lesson might be worthwhile. By understanding a bit more about the origins of Ajax, you'll hopefully be more capable of assessing the role it will play in the future of Web development. It's a very exciting technology, but one that is in many ways still in an exploratory state. So where exactly did Ajax come from and where is it headed? Let's find out.

### The Road to Ajax

When you get right down to it, Ajax is really just a term used to describe a particular application of existing technologies. This particular application has been around for a few years, but not until February of 2005 was given a catchy name. The man responsible for the name is Jesse James Garrett, who needed a more efficient way to describe a Web solution to a client of his employer, Adaptive Path. The name Ajax hit Garrett one day in the shower, and it stuck. People began thinking that Ajax was actually an acronym (AJAX) that stood for Asynchronous JavaScript and XML, but this isn't entirely the case. Although the name Ajax does in fact refer to a development approach involving asynchronous JavaScript and XML, the word "Ajax" isn't strictly an acronym.

Surprisingly enough, the road leading up to Garrett's catchy name can be traced back to Microsoft's Remote Scripting technology, which was officially introduced in 1998 in Internet Explorer 4 and Netscape Navigator 4\. A functional application that utilized Microsoft's Remote Scripting technology was Outlook Web Access, which was certainly pioneering for its time. Taking a step back a few years earlier, the `iframe` (Internet Explorer) and `layer` (Netscape Navigator) elements supported the asynchronous loading of Web content as far back as 1996\. The `iframe` and `layer` elements in combination with crafty JavaScript coding ushered in the notion of Dynamic HTML, or DHTML, which was popular for several years prior to the advent of Ajax techniques.

The true birth of Ajax took place in 2000 when Microsoft added the `XMLHTTPRequest` object to Internet Explorer 5\. It still took a few years for the technology to mature to a point where it could be used consistently, and even then it took a groundbreaking application for everyone to truly see the power of Ajax.

## Appendix A. The Past, Present, and Future of Ajax

Maybe you couldn't care less where Ajax came from, but just in case you do, I thought a little history lesson might be worthwhile. By understanding a bit more about the origins of Ajax, you'll hopefully be more capable of assessing the role it will play in the future of Web development. It's a very exciting technology, but one that is in many ways still in an exploratory state. So where exactly did Ajax come from and where is it headed? Let's find out.

### The Road to Ajax

When you get right down to it, Ajax is really just a term used to describe a particular application of existing technologies. This particular application has been around for a few years, but not until February of 2005 was given a catchy name. The man responsible for the name is Jesse James Garrett, who needed a more efficient way to describe a Web solution to a client of his employer, Adaptive Path. The name Ajax hit Garrett one day in the shower, and it stuck. People began thinking that Ajax was actually an acronym (AJAX) that stood for Asynchronous JavaScript and XML, but this isn't entirely the case. Although the name Ajax does in fact refer to a development approach involving asynchronous JavaScript and XML, the word "Ajax" isn't strictly an acronym.

Surprisingly enough, the road leading up to Garrett's catchy name can be traced back to Microsoft's Remote Scripting technology, which was officially introduced in 1998 in Internet Explorer 4 and Netscape Navigator 4\. A functional application that utilized Microsoft's Remote Scripting technology was Outlook Web Access, which was certainly pioneering for its time. Taking a step back a few years earlier, the `iframe` (Internet Explorer) and `layer` (Netscape Navigator) elements supported the asynchronous loading of Web content as far back as 1996\. The `iframe` and `layer` elements in combination with crafty JavaScript coding ushered in the notion of Dynamic HTML, or DHTML, which was popular for several years prior to the advent of Ajax techniques.

The true birth of Ajax took place in 2000 when Microsoft added the `XMLHTTPRequest` object to Internet Explorer 5\. It still took a few years for the technology to mature to a point where it could be used consistently, and even then it took a groundbreaking application for everyone to truly see the power of Ajax.

### The Ajax Boom

Major technological shifts rarely happen in the abstract. In the case of Ajax, it took a real-world example to demonstrate how compelling the technology could be. That example was and still is Google Maps. Released to the world in beta form in February of 2005, Google Maps boggled the minds of many Web developers who marveled at how the map could be dragged in real time. How could it be possible for that much data to be transferred so fast? As it turned out, it was a very clever usage of Ajax that allowed Google Maps to appear so responsive. In reality, the application attempts to anticipate your next move and preloads map data asynchronously.

Note

| ![](ys8wohawzzmxcjm1mjjlzgftdc9zyzc4cjlnmgkvdgmucg9zagpwawdll24-.jpg) | It's worth noting that the social networking site Orkut was technically the first mainstream application of Ajax, but it had nowhere near the shock and awe of Google Maps. Orkut used (and continues to use) Ajax to allow users to rate their friends with a star rating system. |

In my mind at least, Google Maps formally ushered in the Ajax era of Web applications. The stampede began, and now numerous mainstream applications have an Ajax feel that is unmistakable. Gmail, Flickr, and Netflix are but a few of the other mainstream applications that are now heavily injected with Ajax, never to return to the land of click, wait, reload. With the huge momentum behind the technology, we will no doubt see many more applications become Ajax-powered as time goes by.

### What's Next for Ajax?

In lieu of the rapid ascent of Ajax, it is worth putting things into perspective as we chart a path into the future. One thing for certain is that many of us in the tech industry still have not learned our lesson from the dot-com bubble bursting of the late 90s. The term "irrational exuberance" has been used to describe the mentality that lead to unchecked rises in tech stocks during that period. A big part of the problem had to do with people latching on to the next "big idea" as if it was going to change the world as we know it. Of course the Internet as a whole has changed the world a fair amount, but many of us have a tendency to hype each new advance as if it alone holds the promise of unimaginable progress, efficiency, riches, etc. Ajax is one of these new advances.

When observed with a bit more perspective, it becomes apparent that Ajax is but one advance in the evolution of the Web. I'm not attempting to understate the significance of the advance, but I am cautioning that we all shouldn't get carried away in the excitement of the newness. Ajax indeed offers the promise of a more responsive and ultimately more usable Web, and that is worthy of some excitement. But at the same time, let's not forget that it solves a very specific problem that isn't necessarily an issue with every Web application. Ajax is not a magic bullet that you fire at every Web page you develop.

As we venture into the uncharted future of the Web plus Ajax, the degree to which we restrain the usage of Ajax may ultimately play a critical role in Ajax's continued success. This has to do with a variety of reasons, one of which is the fact that Ajax can actually put more of a workload on servers despite the fact that less information is typically being transferred. Sure, you've eliminated an entire page of data being sent with each request, but you've opted to fire off a couple dozen requests instead of just one. Depending on the specific server configuration, multiple small requests can potentially present more of a bottleneck than a single large request.

All this is to say that you should approach future Web projects knowing that Ajax is an incredibly handy tool, but it's ultimately still just a tool. The old saying that to a hammer every problem looks like a nail certainly applies here. Just because you have the knowledge and code to inject Ajax into everything you do doesn't necessarily make it right. At the same time, even the smallest properly structured Ajax application can be incredibly compelling and dramatically improve the usability of the app. If we all use Ajax with a tiny bit of creative restraint, the Web of the future will certainly be a more enjoyable place to live and work.

## Appendix B. A Quick and Dirty XMLHttpRequest Reference

Although the major premise of this book is to empower you to use Ajax without having to immerse yourself in the nitty-gritty details of asynchronous JavaScript coding, you may get motivated enough by the examples to dive into Ajax programming. If so, you will need to understand the `XMLHttpRequest` object inside and out—this object houses the primary functionality responsible for making Ajax work. This appendix provides you with a concise reference to the `XMLHttpRequest` object, along with a few helpful hints about how to best use its methods and properties. For a complete reference on all of the objects supported by the core JavaScript language, please refer to [http://developer.mozilla.org/en/docs/Core_JavaScript_1.5_Reference](http://developer.mozilla.org/en/docs/Core_JavaScript_1.5_Reference).

### Methods

The `XMLHttpRequest` object contains several methods used to establish a connection with a Web server and send and receive data. Although the modern usage of the object typically involves asynchronous communication, the object is perfectly capable of performing synchronous operations as well. It's how you use the methods of the object that determines the nature of a particular request.

Following are the main methods supported by the `XMLHttpRequest` object:

*   `open()`

*   `send()`

*   `abort()`

*   `overrideMimeType()`

*   `setRequestHeader()`

*   `getAllResponseHeaders()`

*   `getResponseHeader()`

Of these methods, the first two are by far the most important and most commonly used. The next few sections explore these methods in more detail.

#### open()

```
open(method, url[, async[, userName[, password]]])
```

The `open()` method opens a server request and prepares it for impending communication. The two required parameters (`method` and `url`) specify the request method and URL of the server connection, respectively. The request method parameter can be either `"GET"` or `"POST"`, depending on whether you are making a fairly simple data retrieval request (`"GET"`) or needing to send a fair amount of data to the server as part of the request (`"POST"`). You can still use the `GET` method if you're just sending along a few pieces of information, but for larger data-submission requests (greater than 512 bytes), `POST` is your best bet.

Tip

| ![](ys8wohawzzmxcjm1mjjlzgftdc9zyzc4cjlnmgkvcgnqcglzcghnas4vda--.jpg) | Another handy way to know when to use `GET` or `POST` as the request method for an Ajax request is to assess whether or not data will change on the server based upon the request. If the state of the server changes due to the request, then `POST` is the answer; otherwise, go with `GET`. |

The `url` parameter may be specified as an absolute (full) or relative URL, but keep in mind that in either case the URL must match that of the JavaScript code generating the request. In other words, an Ajax connection can only make a request on the same server as the Ajax script code itself. This is a security limitation of the `XMLHttpRequest` object, but one that is designed to protect users from malicious Ajax coders.

The third parameter to the `open()` method, `async`, is an optional Boolean value that determines whether or not the request is handled asynchronously. The default value of `true` indicates an asynchronous (Ajax) request, whereas `false` indicates a synchronous request. In practice, this means that a synchronous request makes your script wait for a response before doing anything else, whereas an asynchronous request takes place in the background, only reporting back when it's finished. In a few rare situations, a synchronous request might make sense, such as when waiting for absolutely critical data before allowing the user to proceed with a task, but in the vast majority of cases, you'll want to go with an asynchronous request. Besides, asynchronicity is what makes Ajax so cool.

Note

| ![](ys8wohawzzmxcjm1mjjlzgftdc9zyzc4cjlnmgkvdgmucg9zagpwawdll24-.jpg) | Most browsers that support Ajax limit Ajax requests to HTTP requests (`http:` protocol), which means you won't be able to test an Ajax script from your local hard drive using the `file:` protocol. Of course, you can always install a Web server locally, in which case you can use the `http:` protocol to open Ajax pages. |

The last two parameters to `open()` are entirely optional, and are ultimately dependent upon whether your server requires a username and password in order to carry out the request.

Following is a simple example of creating an `XMLHttpRequest` object and calling the `open()` method to establish an asynchronous request:

```
var request = new XMLHttpRequest;
request.open("GET", "processSomeStuff.php", true);
```

Two aspects of the `XMLHttpRequest` object are closely tied to the `open()` method: the `send()` method and the `onreadystatechange` event handler. The `send()` method must be called after the `open()` method in order to actually carry out an Ajax request. The `onreadystatechange` event is assigned a callback method of your own that gets automatically called when the Ajax request completes. In other words, you must write your own function that will get called whenever an Ajax request finishes doing its job. This function is where you process the data returned from the completed Ajax request.

#### send()

```
send(content)
```

The `send()` method is responsible for actually sending a request to a server for processing. The connection must have already been prepared with a call to `open()` prior to `send()` being called. The single parameter to the `send()` method, `content`, is data to be sent to the server as part of the request. In many cases you will be strictly making a server request without sending any data, in which case it is perfectly acceptable to pass `null` into the `send()` method, like this:

```
request.send(null);
```

Passing `null` to the `send()` method is often done when using the `"GET"` request method. The `"POST"` method, on the other hand, is designed specifically for passing along data to the server, in which case you'll want to include meaningful data in the content parameter.

Content passed into the `send()` method via the content parameter can be a string, an array of unsigned bytes, or an XML DOM object. In many cases you'll just pass a string into the method, in which case it usually takes the familiar `POST`-style name/value pair, like in this example:

```
request.send("artist=phish&album=rift&track=weigh");
```

It's important to note that for asynchronous requests you'll need to set the `onreadystatechange` event handler before calling the `send()` method. Otherwise, your event handler callback method will never get called and the Ajax effect will be entirely lost. Following is an example of how this handler is set, which must take place prior to calling the `send()` method:

```
request.onreadystatechange = yourHandler;
```

Keep in mind that when the Ajax request finishes, a call is automatically made to the `yourHandler()` function so that you can do something useful with the data returned by the request.

#### abort()

```
abort()
```

The `abort()` method stops the server request. This method can be handy in a timeout situation when you elect to give up on a lengthy request. You can also call the `abort()` method to make sure that the request object is reset before reusing it to carry out another request. Following is an example of calling the `abort()` method to terminate an Ajax request:

```
request.abort();
```

#### overrideMimeType()

```
overrideMimeType(type)
```

The `overrideMimeType()` method provides you with a means of overruling the MIME type of the data returned from the server in response to a request. This can be handy in situations where you are expecting XML but for some reason can't get the server to cooperate. For example, the server may be rejecting XML data because it isn't perfectly well-formed, in which case the server is returning the data as raw text via the `responseText` property. If you call the `overrideMimeType()` method and specify `"text/xml"` as the MIME type, the server will attempt to return XML data in the `responseXML` property:

```
request.overrideMimeType("text/xml");
```

Note

| ![](ys8wohawzzmxcjm1mjjlzgftdc9zyzc4cjlnmgkvdgmucg9zagpwawdll24-.jpg) | The `overrideMimeType()` method should be called after you call the `open()` method but before calling the `send()` method. |

#### setRequestHeader()

```
setRequestHeader(label, value)
```

The `setRequestHeader()` method is used to add a label/value pair to the request header. This method is not necessary for normal `GET` requests but is essential when making `POST` requests. For example, the following request header setting lets the server know that the data in a `POST` request is going to be in the form of an encoded URL:

```
request.setRequestHeader("Content-Type",
  "application/x-www-form-urlencoded");
```

XML is another popular data format for issuing a `POST` request. If you plan on passing XML data to the server in a `POST` request, then the following request header setting is necessary:

```
request.setRequestHeader("Content-Type",
  "text/xml");
```

The need to set the request header ultimately depends upon your specific request. If your request is a `POST`, you should always set it. Otherwise, in the case of a `GET`, you should be just fine without setting anything on the request header.

#### getAllResponseHeaders()

```
getAllResponseHeaders()
```

The `getAllResponseHeaders()` method returns all of the response headers associated with the response as a string containing a series of newline-separated label/value pairs. Of perhaps a bit more use is the `getResponseHeader()` method, which returns a single value for a particular header label.

#### getResponseHeader()

```
getResponseHeader(headerLabel)
```

The `getResponseHeader()` method accepts the label of a specific header and returns the string value of the header for that label. As an example, you can determine the content type of a request by getting the response header for the `"Content-Type"` label, like this:

```
var header = request.getResponseHeader("Content-Type");
```

In this example, the return value stored in the `header` variable might be something along the lines of `text/xml` or `text/plain`, depending upon the type of data returned by the request.

## Appendix B. A Quick and Dirty XMLHttpRequest Reference

Although the major premise of this book is to empower you to use Ajax without having to immerse yourself in the nitty-gritty details of asynchronous JavaScript coding, you may get motivated enough by the examples to dive into Ajax programming. If so, you will need to understand the `XMLHttpRequest` object inside and out—this object houses the primary functionality responsible for making Ajax work. This appendix provides you with a concise reference to the `XMLHttpRequest` object, along with a few helpful hints about how to best use its methods and properties. For a complete reference on all of the objects supported by the core JavaScript language, please refer to [http://developer.mozilla.org/en/docs/Core_JavaScript_1.5_Reference](http://developer.mozilla.org/en/docs/Core_JavaScript_1.5_Reference).

### Methods

The `XMLHttpRequest` object contains several methods used to establish a connection with a Web server and send and receive data. Although the modern usage of the object typically involves asynchronous communication, the object is perfectly capable of performing synchronous operations as well. It's how you use the methods of the object that determines the nature of a particular request.

Following are the main methods supported by the `XMLHttpRequest` object:

*   `open()`

*   `send()`

*   `abort()`

*   `overrideMimeType()`

*   `setRequestHeader()`

*   `getAllResponseHeaders()`

*   `getResponseHeader()`

Of these methods, the first two are by far the most important and most commonly used. The next few sections explore these methods in more detail.

#### open()

```
open(method, url[, async[, userName[, password]]])
```

The `open()` method opens a server request and prepares it for impending communication. The two required parameters (`method` and `url`) specify the request method and URL of the server connection, respectively. The request method parameter can be either `"GET"` or `"POST"`, depending on whether you are making a fairly simple data retrieval request (`"GET"`) or needing to send a fair amount of data to the server as part of the request (`"POST"`). You can still use the `GET` method if you're just sending along a few pieces of information, but for larger data-submission requests (greater than 512 bytes), `POST` is your best bet.

Tip

| ![](ys8wohawzzmxcjm1mjjlzgftdc9zyzc4cjlnmgkvcgnqcglzcghnas4vda--.jpg) | Another handy way to know when to use `GET` or `POST` as the request method for an Ajax request is to assess whether or not data will change on the server based upon the request. If the state of the server changes due to the request, then `POST` is the answer; otherwise, go with `GET`. |

The `url` parameter may be specified as an absolute (full) or relative URL, but keep in mind that in either case the URL must match that of the JavaScript code generating the request. In other words, an Ajax connection can only make a request on the same server as the Ajax script code itself. This is a security limitation of the `XMLHttpRequest` object, but one that is designed to protect users from malicious Ajax coders.

The third parameter to the `open()` method, `async`, is an optional Boolean value that determines whether or not the request is handled asynchronously. The default value of `true` indicates an asynchronous (Ajax) request, whereas `false` indicates a synchronous request. In practice, this means that a synchronous request makes your script wait for a response before doing anything else, whereas an asynchronous request takes place in the background, only reporting back when it's finished. In a few rare situations, a synchronous request might make sense, such as when waiting for absolutely critical data before allowing the user to proceed with a task, but in the vast majority of cases, you'll want to go with an asynchronous request. Besides, asynchronicity is what makes Ajax so cool.

Note

| ![](ys8wohawzzmxcjm1mjjlzgftdc9zyzc4cjlnmgkvdgmucg9zagpwawdll24-.jpg) | Most browsers that support Ajax limit Ajax requests to HTTP requests (`http:` protocol), which means you won't be able to test an Ajax script from your local hard drive using the `file:` protocol. Of course, you can always install a Web server locally, in which case you can use the `http:` protocol to open Ajax pages. |

The last two parameters to `open()` are entirely optional, and are ultimately dependent upon whether your server requires a username and password in order to carry out the request.

Following is a simple example of creating an `XMLHttpRequest` object and calling the `open()` method to establish an asynchronous request:

```
var request = new XMLHttpRequest;
request.open("GET", "processSomeStuff.php", true);
```

Two aspects of the `XMLHttpRequest` object are closely tied to the `open()` method: the `send()` method and the `onreadystatechange` event handler. The `send()` method must be called after the `open()` method in order to actually carry out an Ajax request. The `onreadystatechange` event is assigned a callback method of your own that gets automatically called when the Ajax request completes. In other words, you must write your own function that will get called whenever an Ajax request finishes doing its job. This function is where you process the data returned from the completed Ajax request.

#### send()

```
send(content)
```

The `send()` method is responsible for actually sending a request to a server for processing. The connection must have already been prepared with a call to `open()` prior to `send()` being called. The single parameter to the `send()` method, `content`, is data to be sent to the server as part of the request. In many cases you will be strictly making a server request without sending any data, in which case it is perfectly acceptable to pass `null` into the `send()` method, like this:

```
request.send(null);
```

Passing `null` to the `send()` method is often done when using the `"GET"` request method. The `"POST"` method, on the other hand, is designed specifically for passing along data to the server, in which case you'll want to include meaningful data in the content parameter.

Content passed into the `send()` method via the content parameter can be a string, an array of unsigned bytes, or an XML DOM object. In many cases you'll just pass a string into the method, in which case it usually takes the familiar `POST`-style name/value pair, like in this example:

```
request.send("artist=phish&album=rift&track=weigh");
```

It's important to note that for asynchronous requests you'll need to set the `onreadystatechange` event handler before calling the `send()` method. Otherwise, your event handler callback method will never get called and the Ajax effect will be entirely lost. Following is an example of how this handler is set, which must take place prior to calling the `send()` method:

```
request.onreadystatechange = yourHandler;
```

Keep in mind that when the Ajax request finishes, a call is automatically made to the `yourHandler()` function so that you can do something useful with the data returned by the request.

#### abort()

```
abort()
```

The `abort()` method stops the server request. This method can be handy in a timeout situation when you elect to give up on a lengthy request. You can also call the `abort()` method to make sure that the request object is reset before reusing it to carry out another request. Following is an example of calling the `abort()` method to terminate an Ajax request:

```
request.abort();
```

#### overrideMimeType()

```
overrideMimeType(type)
```

The `overrideMimeType()` method provides you with a means of overruling the MIME type of the data returned from the server in response to a request. This can be handy in situations where you are expecting XML but for some reason can't get the server to cooperate. For example, the server may be rejecting XML data because it isn't perfectly well-formed, in which case the server is returning the data as raw text via the `responseText` property. If you call the `overrideMimeType()` method and specify `"text/xml"` as the MIME type, the server will attempt to return XML data in the `responseXML` property:

```
request.overrideMimeType("text/xml");
```

Note

| ![](ys8wohawzzmxcjm1mjjlzgftdc9zyzc4cjlnmgkvdgmucg9zagpwawdll24-.jpg) | The `overrideMimeType()` method should be called after you call the `open()` method but before calling the `send()` method. |

#### setRequestHeader()

```
setRequestHeader(label, value)
```

The `setRequestHeader()` method is used to add a label/value pair to the request header. This method is not necessary for normal `GET` requests but is essential when making `POST` requests. For example, the following request header setting lets the server know that the data in a `POST` request is going to be in the form of an encoded URL:

```
request.setRequestHeader("Content-Type",
  "application/x-www-form-urlencoded");
```

XML is another popular data format for issuing a `POST` request. If you plan on passing XML data to the server in a `POST` request, then the following request header setting is necessary:

```
request.setRequestHeader("Content-Type",
  "text/xml");
```

The need to set the request header ultimately depends upon your specific request. If your request is a `POST`, you should always set it. Otherwise, in the case of a `GET`, you should be just fine without setting anything on the request header.

#### getAllResponseHeaders()

```
getAllResponseHeaders()
```

The `getAllResponseHeaders()` method returns all of the response headers associated with the response as a string containing a series of newline-separated label/value pairs. Of perhaps a bit more use is the `getResponseHeader()` method, which returns a single value for a particular header label.

#### getResponseHeader()

```
getResponseHeader(headerLabel)
```

The `getResponseHeader()` method accepts the label of a specific header and returns the string value of the header for that label. As an example, you can determine the content type of a request by getting the response header for the `"Content-Type"` label, like this:

```
var header = request.getResponseHeader("Content-Type");
```

In this example, the return value stored in the `header` variable might be something along the lines of `text/xml` or `text/plain`, depending upon the type of data returned by the request.

### Properties

Methods are only half of the `XMLHttpRequest` equation. Several properties also come into play and are in fact quite important in successfully carrying out Ajax requests. Following are the most important properties supported by the `XMLHttpRequest` object:

*   `readyState`

*   `status`

*   `statusText`

*   `responseText`

*   `responseXML`

These properties are important because they tell you critical information about the status and state of Ajax requests, such as if a request is finished and successful, along with storing the data returned by Ajax requests. All of these properties are read-only, meaning that you can't set their values. The first three properties relate to the state and status of a request, whereas the last two properties are used to access the actual data returned from a request. The next few sections dig deeper into how these properties work.

#### readyState

The `readyState` property reflects the current state of a request and is your primary means of keeping track of the progress of the request. The property steps through a series of values as a request is carried out, with the last of these values indicating that the request has completed. Following are the possible values for the `readyState` property, in order of occurrence:

*   `0`—Uninitialized

*   `1`—Loading

*   `2`—Loaded

*   `3`—Interactive

*   `4`—Complete

Of these property values, the last one is the only one you will typically be concerned with because it indicates the completion of a request. However, a value of `4` for the `readyState` property isn't the end of the story when it comes to detecting a successful Ajax request. You also need to consider the `status` property.

Note

| ![](ys8wohawzzmxcjm1mjjlzgftdc9zyzc4cjlnmgkvdgmucg9zagpwawdll24-.jpg) | The custom callback method assigned to the `onreadystatechange` event handler is called every time the value of the `readyState` property changes. This is why it is important to check for the `readyState` property equaling `4` in the callback method before taking action in response to a request—you need to confirm that the request has completed before doing anything. |

#### status

The `status` property keeps up with the current HTTP server status, which can be one of many different values, including `404` for "Not Found" or `200` for "OK." Practically speaking, all you're really interested in from an Ajax perspective is a `status` setting of `200`, which indicates that a request was carried out "OK." Plenty of other codes could feasibly pop up, but you're primarily interested in `200` because anything else means there was a problem.

You will typically check for the `status` property equaling `200` (OK) while also checking for the `readyState` property equaling `4` (Complete). If both properties check out, you know the request has completed successfully and you can begin processing the request data.

#### statusText

The `statusText` property contains a string message associated with the current status code. For example, the status text for the successful status code of `200` is `"OK"`. You might consider displaying the status text for diagnostic or error-handling purposes should a request fail with a status code other than `200`.

#### responseText

The `responseText` property is half of the property pair used to store the data returned from an Ajax request. I say it's half of the property pair because another property, called `responseXML`, is capable of returning data in a format different from `responseText`. If you plan on receiving request data as raw text, `responseText` is the property you'll want to use because it simply provides you with a string of text containing the data.

#### responseXML

The other half of the response data property pair is the `responseXML` property, which formats the return data from a request as a DOM-compatible XML document node object. Because the `responseXML` property provides you with a DOM node object, you can use all of the incredibly powerful and flexible DOM methods and properties to explore and manipulate the data. Although it is entirely possible and often useful to work with Ajax request data as raw text via the `responseText` property, it's the structure of XML request data supplied by `responseXML` that gives Ajax much of its power.

It's important to note that request data is only stored in the `responseXML` property when the response's content type is set to the XML MIME type, `text/xml`. In many cases the XML MIME type is automatically set correctly based upon the recognition of XML data in the response. However, this is not always the case, and you may run into a situation where the `responseXML` property is coming up empty even though you know the response is receiving XML data. The solution is to explicitly set the MIME type of the request to `"text/xml"` via the `overrideMimeType()` method.

### Events

The event that works all the magic behind the scenes in any Ajax application is `onreadystatechange`, which is triggered any time the ready state of a request changes. This event is typically assigned a custom event-handler method that takes action based upon a change in the ready state. It's your job to check and make sure within this method that the request completed (`readyState == 4`) and that it proceeded without error (`status == 200`).

## Appendix C. Using the Live Linux CD

A live CD is an entire computer operating system on a removable disc. The CD can be booted directly on most standard PCs, ignoring the contents of your hard drive, and run as a desktop, server, or other specialty kind of system. The Ajax Construction Kit live CD included with this book contains elements of all three. Using this live CD, you can run all the book's examples live, follow along with the code as you read the book, and modify the code to reuse later.

### Inside the Live CD

The live CD is a remastered Ubuntu Linux live CD that has been adapted to include the following components:

*   Ajax Construction Kit examples— All the example applications described in this book are included on the live CD.

*   Web server— The XAMPP server is included on the live CD. With one click, you can start the server to try out the Ajax examples on your local computer or from another browser over the network.

*   Tools— The live CD also includes the Firefox Web browser (for displaying the examples) and Bluefish editor (for modifying the code). Because the CD is a full-featured, desktop live CD, other command-line tools and applications are also available to you.

*   Documentation— Links in the top panel let you access useful Ajax, PHP, and XAMPP documentation, in case you need reference material as you work with the code.

Using the Ajax Construction Kit CD as a live CD means that, after rebooting your PC, all the software you need to display, use, and modify the Ajax projects is available to you without any special configuration. However, in cases where you don't want to (or are not allowed to) reboot the PC you are working on, the live CD also offers other ways to work with the code.

On the CD we have included a version of the XAMPP Web server package that will easily install on Windows systems. Following the instructions for using that software and adding the code examples from the CD, you can run the Ajax projects from your Windows desktop. Everything you need is included on the CD. (You just need to do a bit more setup.)

## Appendix C. Using the Live Linux CD

A live CD is an entire computer operating system on a removable disc. The CD can be booted directly on most standard PCs, ignoring the contents of your hard drive, and run as a desktop, server, or other specialty kind of system. The Ajax Construction Kit live CD included with this book contains elements of all three. Using this live CD, you can run all the book's examples live, follow along with the code as you read the book, and modify the code to reuse later.

### Inside the Live CD

The live CD is a remastered Ubuntu Linux live CD that has been adapted to include the following components:

*   Ajax Construction Kit examples— All the example applications described in this book are included on the live CD.

*   Web server— The XAMPP server is included on the live CD. With one click, you can start the server to try out the Ajax examples on your local computer or from another browser over the network.

*   Tools— The live CD also includes the Firefox Web browser (for displaying the examples) and Bluefish editor (for modifying the code). Because the CD is a full-featured, desktop live CD, other command-line tools and applications are also available to you.

*   Documentation— Links in the top panel let you access useful Ajax, PHP, and XAMPP documentation, in case you need reference material as you work with the code.

Using the Ajax Construction Kit CD as a live CD means that, after rebooting your PC, all the software you need to display, use, and modify the Ajax projects is available to you without any special configuration. However, in cases where you don't want to (or are not allowed to) reboot the PC you are working on, the live CD also offers other ways to work with the code.

On the CD we have included a version of the XAMPP Web server package that will easily install on Windows systems. Following the instructions for using that software and adding the code examples from the CD, you can run the Ajax projects from your Windows desktop. Everything you need is included on the CD. (You just need to do a bit more setup.)

### Running the Live CD

With an average PC, containing at least 256MB of RAM and a Pentium III, 650MHz (or better) processor, the live CD should perform quite well. Here's what you do:

1.  Insert the CD into a standard PC and reboot.

2.  Press Enter at the boot screen to select Start or Install.

3.  When the desktop appears after a few minutes (be patient), open the Start LAMPP icon to start the server.

4.  After the server is running, open the Start Projects icon to view and work with projects.

At this point you should see the Firefox Web browser open to the index page, from which you can run all the code examples in the book. The live CD's Ubuntu desktop, along with the Ajax Construction Kit components, appears in [Figure C.1](#app03lev1sec2.html_app03fig01).

##### Figure C.1\. The live CD provides easy access to launching the XAMPP Web server and exploring the Ajax example applications.

[[View full size image]](ys8wohawzzmxcjm1mjjlzgftdc9zyzc4cjlnmgkvzmmuamdwz3bjczfhadbsav9pyxqv.jpg)![](ys8wohawzzmxcjm1mjjlzgftdc9zyzc4cjlnmgkvzmnncgnzmghqmxauawlnys8-.jpg)

If you want to start modifying the code examples, select the Bluefish icon in the top panel. The Bluefish editor (shown in the upper-right corner of [Figure C.1](#app03lev1sec2.html_app03fig01)) displays the Ajax code example files in the left column. You can change any of those files as you like. Then see the results live in your Firefox browser by reloading the page.

Besides the icon for the Bluefish editor, there are other useful icons in the top panel. Each of the small book icons in the panel represents a link to different support information. They include links to Ajax and related documentation and links to information on the XAMPP server.

The entire XAMPP server files are stored in the `/opt/lampp` folder. The code examples are stored in the `/opt/lampp/htdocs` folder. Any changes you make to the code or server settings that you want to keep must be saved before you shut down (which I'll discuss later). Without saving those files, the next time you reboot, the entire contents of your live CD system are returned to their original state.

#### Setting Up Networking

Many of the Ajax projects require Internet connectivity to work properly. Most wired Ethernet cards in Linux will be automatically detected and configured. If a DHCP server is available on the line, you will most likely be connected automatically and have immediate access to the Internet.

Wireless connectivity can be more challenging in Linux. Because many wireless cards require proprietary firmware from the manufacturer that cannot be legally redistributed with Linux, those wireless cards may require some extra work to get going in Linux. Other wireless cards won't work at all (although the number of completely unsupported cards has shrunk greatly in the past few years).

Whether wired or wireless, the first place to try setting up networking on the live CD is the Network Setup window. From the live CD desktop, select System, Administration, Networking. That window shows you immediately if your network interface hardware was detected and configured to run. You can go ahead and do any manual configuration of those interfaces (such as to set up a static IP address, if needed) that are shown as available.

If the Network Setup window tells you that your wireless card is not configured, open a Terminal window (from the Terminal icon in the top panel) to do a bit of detective work. Type the following command to try to determine the type of wireless card you are using:

```
$ lspci -v |grep -i wireless
```

You will likely see something like this after issuing the command:

```
02:02.0 Network controller: Intel Corporation PRO/Wireless
2200BG Network Connection (rev 05)
```

In this example, the wireless card is an Intel PRO/Wireless 2200BG card. With that information, go to your favorite search engine (presuming you have some machine to get you on the network or can temporarily hook up to a wired network) and search for the wireless card model and the word Linux (as in "Linux 2200BG"). This example turned up the ipw2200 driver project. I'll continue with that example. First I check to see if the driver had been loaded:

```
$ lsmod |grep ipw2200
```

A match for this command reveals that the driver has indeed been loaded:

```
ipw2200     115652   0
```

If the driver had not been installed, I would have to download (or otherwise get the driver on the machine) then load it using the `modprobe` command (such as `sudo modprobe ipw2200`). Next, I have to get the firmware needed by the card. Drivers for many wireless cards are in the `/lib/firmware` folder. If the firmware you need is not there, download the firmware and add it to the `/lib/firmware` folder.

At this point, you should be able to configure your wireless card. Select your wireless card from the Network Setup window and select the Properties window. Set up your wireless settings (ESSID, password, and configuration type, such as static or DHCP). Restart your network interfaces by typing `sudo /etc/init.d/networking restart` from a Terminal window. You should now be able to select the Network Monitor icon in the top panel to check the activity of your wireless card.

#### Backing Up Your Data

As I mentioned before, any changes you make during a live CD session will just disappear when you reboot, unless you back up your data in some way. You can back up your Ajax Construction Kit data to a local hard disk, to USB flash drive, or over the network. Then, the next time you start the live CD, you can restore that data. Here's an example of backing up data with a USB flash drive.

Insert the storage (such as a pen drive or a thumb drive) into a USB port. If it is formatted, the live CD should detect it and open any formatted partitions in a separate window on the desktop. Next, open the Archive Manager (select Applications, Accessories, Archive Manager). From the Archive Manager, select New and then add the name of the archive (such as `AjaxProjects`) and the folder to save it to (such as `/media/usbdisk` or other name representing your pen drive). Select New.

Open the Ajax Applications folder from the desktop. Then select some or all of the files and folders from that folder and drag and drop them on the Archive Manager window. The archive (tar and gzipped) is now saved on your USB drive.

The next time you boot your Ajax Construction Kit live CD, open the Archive Manager window again. Select Archive, Open and open the archive from your inserted USB drive. Select Edit, Select All to highlight all files in the archive. Then choose Archive, Extract.

Select Extract in Folder to choose the `/opt/lampp/htdocs` folder to extract the files to. With the defaults set, you will recreate folders and overwrite existing files. Make sure that is what you want to do. Then select Extract.

The procedure just described can also be used to copy your Ajax project files and folders to another computer system (such as a Windows box) to work on or run the projects from that machine. If that is the case, you may want to select an archive type such as `.zip` to make sure you can extract the files on the new system.

Note

| ![](ys8wohawzzmxcjm1mjjlzgftdc9zyzc4cjlnmgkvdgmucg9zagpwawdll24-.jpg) | If you want to backup the entire Web server (`/opt/lampp`), you might need to do so from the command line, instead of from the Archive Manager window. That's because you might need root permissions to access some of the files. Here's a command you could then use to backup the whole XAMPP server (including Ajax projects) to your USB disk. The second command lets you restore it: 
```
$ sudo tar cvfz /media/usbdisk/lampp-backup.tar.gz /opt/lamp
$ cd / ; sudo tar xvfz /media/usbdisk/lampp-backup.tar.gz
```

 |

### Running the Live CD Directly on a Windows PC

Some people may prefer not to reboot their computer to try the examples in this book. If that's your case, you can install the XAMPP server software on your Windows system and try the code examples from there. You won't get all the tools that come on the live CD, but you will be able to work from the Windows environment you are accustomed to.

To install and run the XAMPP server and code examples from this book on your Windows system, do the following:

1.  From your running Windows system, insert the live CD that comes with this book.

2.  Open the live CD in an Explorer window and navigate to the `\Windows` folder.

3.  From the CD's `\Windows` folder, open the XAMPP installer (`xampp-win32-1.6.0a-installer.exe`) and follow the installation instructions. It is made to run in Windows 2000, NT, and XP Professional. If you need help, refer to the XAMPP for Windows FAQ ([www.apachefriends.org/en/faq-xampp-windows.html](http://www.apachefriends.org/en/faq-xampp-windows.html)).

4.  If you chose all the defaults, the software will be extracted and you should end up with an XAMPP Control Panel desktop icon. Open that icon.

5.  When the XAMPP Control Panel Application window appears, select the Start buttons for Apache and MySql. (If Windows prompts you to unblock your ports, you don't need to if you just want to work with the server from your desktop.)

6.  Insert your Ajax Construction Kit CD into the CD drive and browse to the Windows folder (for example, `D:\Windows`).

7.  Open the `Examples` folder, choose Edit, Select All and Edit, Copy. Using your Explorer window, browse to the `htdocs` folder on the XAMPP window. This folder is on your hard disk and is likely something like `C:\Program Files\xampp\htdocs`. Then select Edit, Paste.

8.  With all the Ajax projects in place, you can start using them. From any Web browser on your machine, type this Web address to open the Ajax Construction Kit examples:

    ```
    http://localhost/index.html
    ```

You can use any text editors or Web content management tools you have on your Windows system to begin working with the Ajax projects.

### Running the Live CD Directly on a Mac PC

If you have an Intel-based Mac computer, you can run the live CD directly. However, you also have the option of running the Ajax code examples from Mac OS X instead using the included Apache software.

The live CD can be booted on the newer, Intel-based Mac computers (such as the Mac Mini) and used as described earlier in this appendix. Insert the live CD, reboot with the Alt key held down, and select to boot from the CD. (It will be noted as a Windows CD.) Your wired Ethernet card should work without any additional configuration. However, wireless Ethernet cards may not work in Linux from the computer. Otherwise, the live CD should work as it would on any Intel-based PC.

If you prefer to run the code examples natively in Mac OS X, you're in luck. Apache is included on new Mac systems, so you just need to do a bit of configuration, add the examples, and start the server. Here's how:

1.  Open a Terminal window, become root user (`su -`), and use any text editor to edit the `/etc/httpd/httpd.conf` file. You need to search for four lines and remove the comment character (`#`) in front of each of those lines to enable PHP and Perl for your Apache server. Here's what the lines look like after they have been uncommented:

    ```
    LoadModule perl_module  libexec/httpd/libperl.so
    LoadModule php4_module  libexec/httpd/libphp4.so
    AddModule mod_perl.c
    AddModule mod_php4.c
    ```

    Close the Terminal window and return to the OS X desktop.

2.  Insert the live CD, then open the Windows, Examples folder from the CD. You should see all the Ajax code example files and folders.

3.  Open a file browser (select the Macintosh HD icon) and navigate to your home folder. From your `home` folder, open the `Sites` folder.

4.  Rename the existing `index.html` file in the `Sites` folder to some other name (such as `index-original.html`).

5.  Select everything from the `Examples` folder, then drag and drop that content to your Sites folder.

6.  Open the `System Preferences` folder and select the `Sharing` folder (under Internet & Network).

7.  From the Sharing window, select Personal Web Sharing. This will start the Apache Web server and begin serving the Ajax code examples.

8.  On the Sharing window, select the link shown after "...your personal website at..." which should look something like the following:

    ```
    http://10.0.0.1/~user
    ```

    Or you could type something like `http://localhost/~user` (where `user` is replaced by your user name) into the Web browser location box.

The Ajax code examples should appear in the browser window, ready to play.

### Running the Live CD in Other Ways

With the massive amount of open source and proprietary software available today, people have developed all kinds of ways to run software in different environments. If the live CD and Windows methods of working with the Ajax Construction Kit CD don't exactly suit you, here are some other ways you can set up the software from that CD to use with the book:

*   Windows desktop with emulator— Using virtualization software, such as VMPlayer ([www.vmware.com](http://www.vmware.com)) or QEMU ([www.qemu.org](http://www.qemu.org)), you can run the live CD as a virtual client. In effect, the live CD will be running in a window on your Windows or Linux desktop.

*   Your current Web server— You can copy the projects from the `/Windows/Examples` folder on the CD to your own Web server to run the code from there.

*   Any hard disk partition— If you have free space outside of your Windows or Linux partitions on your hard disk, you can install the entire contents of the live CD to your computer's hard disk. Unless you have some free disk space outside your existing disk partitions or you don't mind wiping out the contents of a hard disk on your computer, the process can be a bit tricky. That's because you will need to resize an existing disk partition to do it.

    Once you have an available disk or partition, however, the process of actually installing the live CD is quite easy. Just select the Install icon from the live CD desktop. The installer will step you through the process of installing the live CD so you can work with its contents from your hard disk.

*   Any Linux System— The full XAMPP stack and Ajax examples are included in a tar archive on the CD (`/Linux/xampp-linux-1.6-Ajax.tar.gz`). You can untar the contents of that archive to almost any Linux system that isn't already running a Web server and use the projects from that system. As root user, with the CD inserted, the following commands will untar the archive to your `/opt` folder and start the server:

    ```
    # cd /opt ; tar xvfz /media/disk/Linux/xampp-linux-1.6-Ajax.tar.gz
    # /opt/lampp/lampp start
    ```

Keep in mind that the live CD is set up primarily as an educational tool for learning to write Ajax code. Although XAMPP can be used as a professional quality Web server, it is not currently configured for security. Before using the example code or Web server on a public network, be sure to take steps to lock down the security of that content.
